### A.1.6 Executing entity / beneficiary

Environmental Co-benefits: 300 ha of degraded land rehabilitated and protected from erosion, landslides and flood risk; improving water supply infrastructure and improving livelihoods by agroforestry (100 has of agroforestry on farms, 250 hectares of agroforestry with irrigation, and 100 ha of agroforestry on farmlands). 23,412 households with improved access to water supply infrastructure; implementation of agroforestry. ## A.2. Project / Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

1. Approximately 70% of Timor-Leste's rural population (840,000 people) is highly vulnerable to climate changes, particularly increasing variability of rainfall and extreme weather events. Lives and livelihoods in the remote interior are dependent on rain-fed agriculture, which is sensitive to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and severity of droughts and floods, soil erosion, land degradation and deforestation.
2. The project objective is to safeguard vulnerable communities and their physical assets from climate change-induced disasters. It aims to address existing institutional, financial and legislative barriers, increasing the climate resilience

# B.1. Description of Financial Elements of the Project / Programme

## 4. Government of Timor Leste is seeking grant financing for the proposed priority adaptation investments to safeguard vulnerable rural communities and their physical and economic assets from climate induced disasters.

The project will invest in 6 target municipalities that are highly susceptible to the identified climate hazards.

## 5. The proposed measures are intended to lift barriers and strengthen capacity at all levels and do not lend themselves to implementation through a single, large-scale infrastructure investment. Instead, they focus on small-scale infrastructure investments.

Small-scale infrastructure is designed, constructed, and maintained by the government. GCF funds will be used to scale up existing projects and provide additional funding for new projects that are complementary to existing infrastructure and secure it against climate-induced hazards. GCF funds will complement public financing from the District Development Fund (DDF) and the National Budget, as well as private sector investment through the Public-Private Development (PPD) at the sub- or village level to decentralize infrastructure services in the most climate vulnerable districts.

## 6. GCF funds will be complemented by co-financing of USD 572 million from 5 main sources:

(i) First, the Government of Timor Leste has pledged annual Ministry of State Administration budget support of $132 Million USD in cash for PDDM and PNDS projects over 5 years from year 2 to year 6. This will be used to finance the project’s baseline costs (i.e., the costs associated with implementing the baseline investments that will be supplemented by climate-proofing via GCF funding). These funds are leveraged through co-financing of USD 140 million in cash for PDDM and PNDS projects over 5 years from year 2 to year 6. This is intended to finance the project’s incremental costs (i.e., the costs associated with implementing new investments that will be climate vulnerable districts as identified during the feasibility phase) whereby climate risk criteria and a potential co-financing mechanism for funds influenced the government prioritization of the districts and units as a result of the proposed project.

(ii) Government of Timor Leste has also pledged annual Ministry of State Administration budget support of USD 105 Million USD in cash for disaster risk management (DRM) over 5 years from year 2 to year 6. This includes both O&M during project implementation and post-project O&M at GCF, at a $94 million USD for 20 years. The total cost of the DRM component is estimated at USD 183 Million USD over 20 years. This amount is included as project co-financing.

(iii) Government of Timor Leste annual Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries budget support of $12 Million USD to support of agroforestry and reforestation of approximately 157 ha in the 6 target municipalities, which will be used for the implementation of the disaster risk management (DRM) component over 5 years (details identified under Output 2).

(iv) Government of Timor Leste annual Ministry of Social Development budget support of $18 Million USD for disaster risk management (DRM) over 5 years from year 2 to year 6. This includes developing DRM using the hazard maps, damage and loss systems and asset condition inspection tools developed under Output 3. The total cost of the DRM component is estimated at USD 40 million USD over 5 years.

(v) UNDP will provide $80 Million USD in cash for project implementation costs.

# C1. Strategic Context

## Climate change in Timor Leste

### Temperature and Rainfall: Timor Leste’s Initial National Communication (INC) June 2014, under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), recognizes two possible major impacts of climate change including:
- **Temperature:** The mean temperature anomalies in Timor Leste are expected to increase by up to as much as +3°C by 2100. Projections show that the mean temperatures during the 2041–60 and 2081–2090 periods are expected to increase from the current conditions by about +1.5°C under a low emission scenario (RCP2.6) and by up to +3°C under high emission scenario (RCP8.5). The increase in temperature relative to current conditions may reach 3°C by 2100.
- **Rainfall:** Rainfall has been showing a downward trend in the dry season and upward trend in the rainy season in the last few decades, with an overall decrease of about 7–13% over the period. The wet season is expected to be drier than normal during the 2041–60 period, with overall rainfall increasing by 7–13% by 2050. The most considerable increase is expected to occur in the June/July August season during the 2041-60 period. The seasonal rainfall climatology in September/October and November/December seasons are expected to be similar to that of the reference period (1971–80) period. In general, the seasonal rainfall climatology in the region will be much wetter during 2041-60 than the current conditions.

### Variability: Rainfall variability is also predicted to increase. In the future, the wet season onset may be delayed by about 50 days and the dry season onset advanced by up to 30 days in some areas of Timor Leste, depending on the period and emission scenarios. In addition, the other area on the northern coast of the country is expected to experience a decrease in rainfall. These changes in both spatial and temporal rainfall distribution will have significant impact on the water balance.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (FUNDING PROPOSAL), PAGE 11 OF 99

### Climate change-induced hydro-meteorological hazards: Geographically, Timor-Leste is exposed to several kinds of natural hazards which include frequent events such as tropical cyclones, intense rainfall, flooding, drought, and landslides as well as infrequent but potentially catastrophic events such as earthquakes. The country has a long history of these events; the country's recent history includes floods, landslides, and drought (droughtly dry spells). An assessment of the impact of climate change on these hazards is provided in Section 2.3 of the Feasibility Study (please refer to Annex C).

### Climate change impacts

#### Climate change-induced impacts on agricultural productivity: Agriculture is the most important socioeconomic sector in East Timor, accounting for approximately 75% of employment. The projected year-to-year variability and dryness seasons will impact crop yields and food security. In addition, with expected increases in rainfall in wet seasons, certain areas may experience flooding and landslides, which can lead to soil fertility loss and reduced agricultural productivity.

#### Climate change-induced impacts on water resources: Water is a critical resource for agriculture and human consumption. For example, Cova Lima, Manufahi, and Viqueque each receive more rainfall than northern districts, and experience two wet seasons per year compared with one in the north. However, these areas are also prone to flooding and landslides, exacerbating water insecurity for agricultural production. Furthermore, variable rainfall and extreme events are expected to increase the frequency of floods and landslides. This will lead to increased soil erosion and sedimentation, increasing incidence of landslides and flash flooding and low soil fertility for crop production.

#### Climate change-induced impacts on human health: Given the heavy reliance on subsistence agriculture to survive, the population is therefore vulnerable to climate-induced hazards such as floods and droughts which lead to crop failures. Lack of water in the dry season is

### Detailed Project / Programme Description

#### Green Climate Fund (Funding Proposal), Page 12 of 99

Increasingly affecting agricultural production. Drought in 2001 to 2002, and the late arrival of the wet season in 2002 to 2003 resulted in an estimated 34% decline in maize production between 2002 and 2003. As a result, 110,000 people were identified as needing food aid, particularly in the other maize producing districts of Aileu, Atauro, Ermera, and Liquiçá. The impact of climate change on agricultural production is expected to increase over the next century due to climate change impacts. Most agriculture, including maize is unirrigated, making it vulnerable to droughts and floods. In addition, the majority of rice fields are located in coastal areas where these systems operate in the wet season only, due to insufficient water in the dry season; therefore, increased rainfall variability will have a significant impact on rice production.

### Climate Change Impacts on Coastal and Small-Scale Rural Infrastructure

Climate change is expected to intensify coastal erosion, flooding, and storm surges. These impacts lead to increased shoreline retreat. Additionally, natural hazards can cause substantial damage to the country's fragile rural infrastructure. The most vulnerable areas are those with limited access to services and infrastructure (high social vulnerability), coupled with poor maintenance as well as injury and fatality to residents when natural hazards occur as disasters. This is particularly true for the coastal and small-scale rural infrastructure within Timor-Leste.

Managed at a local level, this sector consists of a large number of comparatively small investments located in small geographical areas. Due to significant differences in size, scale, and typology of these investments, they are managed by different actors with varying levels of capacity. The rural infrastructure is financed through a combination of public and private resources. It can be financed as a single source, financed through decentralized mechanisms of municipal and village level public finance. The rural infrastructure is also subject to the same risks as other infrastructure in Timor-Leste, including limited access to shared resources (e.g., shared water sources, shared land and shared topographic challenges), and thus are subject to impacts from climate change.

### Social and Economic Vulnerability

The exposure of rural communities to climate-induced disasters within Timor-Leste is exacerbated by un-favourable socio-economic conditions. Limited access to infrastructure and services and limited adaptive capacity and resilience to enable them to cope with the effects of climate change. The most vulnerable areas are notably at risk because of their comparatively limited education, income and ability to influence decision-making.

### Degree of Geographic Isolation

The degree of geographic isolation of access to urban areas coupled with socio-economic conditions demonstrates that most rural settlements in the country are highly vulnerable to climate change induced risks. Of the 102 municipalities in Timor-Leste, 85% have a score below 60%, compared with an average score of 73%. This compares with an average score of 66% in the SWA countries and the highest living standards (HLS). Access to improved water and improved sanitation is also much higher in such HLS.

### In Addition, Social and Infrastructure Indicators

In addition to asset, social and infrastructure indices, capacity to cope is also an important index of vulnerability. Where climate change impacts are assessed on a municipality level, the assessment of coping capacity is dependent on the size of the change (the increase in %) of the municipality which will be affected by the highest category of the particular hazard. This means that larger municipalities have greater potential for increased adaptive capacity and resilience capacity of communities. In addition, it provides a means of identifying where the enhancement of components of coping capacity is most needed to make rural areas more vulnerable to climate change in this case, that focus is on increasing the resilience of infrastructure assets.

### When the Impact of Climate Change with Coping Capacity Considerations

When the impact of climate change with coping capacity considerations is assessed for each receptor type (maize, agricultural land, rice fields), it becomes clear which areas are most vulnerable to climate change. In this case, that focus has emerged as the most vulnerable: Baucau, Ermera, Aileu, Viqueque Laute and Liquiçá. The assessment of

impact of climate-induced hydrometeorological hazards on rural infrastructure in Timor Leste, conducted during the Flash Flood Study, has shown an increase of frequency of hazard occurrence and severity of impact under climate change. Severity of impact was shown to be dependent on the ability of communities to cope with hazards. The analysis also showed that the most vulnerable groups are women, children, elderly people, and those living in remote areas. This vulnerability is affected, increases for all municipalities and for all hazards, with the worse affected municipalities being the 6 least developed ones.

National and Sector Policy Context

19. The Government of Timor Leste (GoTL) Strategic Development Plan (SDP) 2011-2030[1] sets out government priorities, strategies, and actions covering three key areas: social capital, infrastructure development, and economic development. The SDP is underpinned by the National Development Strategy (NDS), which provides a framework for the development of policies and programmes to address the large infrastructure deficit and to enable Timor Leste to develop economically and socially.

20. District Plans (PD) and Provincial Master Plans (PMDs) outline small-scale rural infrastructure development priorities with an aim to address the current infrastructure deficit in rural settings. PDIM and PMDS provide secured annual budgets for small-scale rural infrastructure; however, this sector's work provides particularly challenging conditions due to the topography of Timor Leste. The majority of the population lives on steep slopes and mountain terrain susceptible to climate hazards. Climate hazard risk reduction and mitigation are critical to include in overall development planning.

21. According to Timor Leste’s NDC, priority adaptation measures will be focused on reducing adverse effects of climate change, promoting sustainable development, and reducing poverty. This is anticipated to be accomplished by employing a multi-sectoral approach that includes the following: (i) strengthening the capacity for implementing the National Priorities process; (ii) State NDC priorities include: Development of integrated agroforestry and watershed management systems; establishment of community-based disaster risk reduction and mitigation measures; promotion of improved fire management agriculture; reduced burning, reduced erosion and increased soil fertility; reforestation of degraded land; and development of climate-resilient infrastructure. The NDC also highlights the need to build greater vulnerability to climate-related risks, and improved physical infrastructure/engineering and natural vegetation methods for flood risk reduction.

22. Timor Leste’s National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) 2016[3] aims to implement the action plan to reduce the vulnerability of Timor-Leste to the consequences of climate change in all key sections, through three cross-cutting approaches. These approaches are to be embedded in all sectoral programs and programmes emerging from the NAPA process.

• Capacity development and institutional strengthening for stakeholders in matters related to adaptation to climate change and climate variability.
• The identification of best practices and techniques through field based interventions that improve the resilience of the population and ecosystems.
• Information sharing, and communication campaigns for stakeholders on the risks due to climate change and climate variability.

23. The draft National Climate Change Policy intends to provide policy guidance to maintain climate change into development planning and to ensure its inclusion in all sectors in development plans. The draft policy specifies that it promotes climate resilience and climate proofing approaches in small-, medium- and large-scale infrastructure development projects, as well as the integration of disaster risk management interventions. Furthermore, the draft policy highlights the need for vulnerability assessments and adaptation strategies to be included in project proposals and to address potential damage incurred by the climate change. The importance of decentralization is highlighted to emphasize the need for integrated planning at all levels of government. The draft National Climate Change Policy also provides direction and places a strong emphasis on addressing priority adaptation needs, including resilient infrastructure services, to safeguard communities and their assets.

# C2 Project / Programme Objective against Baseline

## Vulnerable small-scale rural infrastructure assets in Timor Leste include water supply systems, rural road and bridges, flood defences, and irrigation systems. These infrastructure units have small geographic footprints, are often embedded within the landscape, and are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased rainfall intensity, soil erosion, flooding and drought. According to the data recorded in the Disastermen damage and loss database, flooding affects 10% of all identified critical assets (25% nationally), while drought affects 34% of all identified critical assets (68% of the identified target municipalities could cost $9.6 Million USD (25% national total)). From a single flood event in 2017, crop income losses from flooding in the large irrigation systems would be affected by 34% (68% of the national total) and 51 water supply systems (34% national total) would be affected. Likewise, property damage due to flooding could drop $8.3 Million USD ($25 national total). From a single flood event in 2017, crop income losses from erosion in the large irrigation systems would be affected by 65% (70% of the national total) and 25 irrigation systems (65% national total) would be affected. Crop income losses from drought in the large irrigation systems could be $8.3 Million USD ($45 of the national total), while 64 Water Supply systems (33% of national total) and 270 water supply points (70% of the national total) would be affected.

In addition, the design and application of existing infrastructure and construction standards is not climate resilient, and there is limited investment in operation and maintenance. This is exacerbating exposure and reducing in the future capacity to respond to climate change impacts. The Government has prioritized road and bridge development of communities when roads and bridges are damaged by localized extreme events; reduction in water yields due to drought; and increased rainfall intensity leading to flooding and soil erosion. In addition, the institutional and financial capacity of local administrations and communities to adapt to the changing situation is limited. This is particularly true for rural areas where there is a high concentration of poverty and heavily areas that are critically vulnerable to climate hazards. To draw the links between ecosystems/land management and infrastructure development, and to identify, assess, prioritize, design, cost and build resilience measures.

## Baseline Investment

With a full recognition for these vulnerabilities, the Government of Timor Leste is actively investing in infrastructure development as part of its essential services. The government has prioritized national and municipal rural roads and bridges, water and sanitation and drainage systems as critical infrastructure units for development. A central pillar of the Strategic Development Plan (SDP) 2017-2030 is to invest $58 million USD in the development of critical infrastructure. It is therefore crucial to ensure that these investments are carried out in full recognition of climate change impacts and vulnerabilities, particularly in rural areas.

The value of government expenditure on roads and bridges amounted to $253.6 Million USD or 16% of total government expenditure on infrastructure over the last five years. Expenditure on irrigation in the same period was relatively modest, at $14 million USD (9%) of total government expenditure. However, irrigation forms a strong base for the SDP and is one of the SDPs objective of rice self-sufficiency. The level of expenditure on roads and bridges has been supplemented by donor financing - The level of expenditure on roads and bridges is forecast to increase; both in normal years and during climate change events. In 2017, the government budgeted $58 million USD for the development of roads and bridges during this period represents a substantial investment for Timor Leste.

The driver for irrigation schemes in Timor-Leste is rice production. The government strategy on the development and rehabilitation of irrigation schemes has been justified on the basis of the need to increase rice prices to safeguard food security. The SDP's Strategic Development Plan (SDP) 2017-2030 states that Timor Leste is aiming to increase

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (FUNDING PROPOSAL) PAGE: 15 OF 99

The area of irrigated rice grew by 40% from 60,000 hectares to 70,000 hectares by 2020, which would require significant investments in rehabilitation and expanding irrigation systems and improving water storage. The level of expenditure on irrigation is budgeted to increase from $14.4 million USD in the period from 2005 to 2013 to $46.5 million USD over the next five years (2018-2022). This represents a significant increase, as the average annual expenditure on irrigation from 2008 to 2013 on irrigation, including financing provided by development partners, amounted to $83 million USD. Further investments are required in order to meet the projected demand for water and to improve water use based irrigation, including the construction of large dams. These investments are mainly aimed at large scale irrigation projects, which will be funded through the World Bank.

The WB review found that if the period from 2003 to 2013, US$82 million had been invested by donor and government in the rehabilitation and expansion of small-scale irrigation systems, it would have resulted in a significant increase in rice production, which confirmed negative EIRRs derived for the irrigation strategy. The review found that only small community-based efforts were made to improve water use efficiency on irrigated land. The WB review concluded that the strategy to support increased rice production is best achieved through these small community-based efforts, rather than large scale projects. The WB review also noted that the most cost-effective approach for increasing supplementary wet and dry irrigation season water, together with the implementation of the current river WIBRD irrigation project, would be to invest in a combination of well and tube-well systems. This would result in an estimated cost about $38.40 million per year (US$40M) than another alternative approach based on tube-wells and the use of rainwater harvesting. The WB review also concluded that the economic cost of growing rice in Timor-Leste would be less than the cost of importing rice, according to a study conducted by the Ministry of Finance using TIPSYS systems, and by using an intensive "ricebox" approach to irrigated rice production rather than the conventional "every district must have irrigation approach" which would also reduce the cost of growing irrigated rice.

At the village and municipal levels, essential small-scale rural infrastructure investment is made via public funding under the Rural Infrastructure Development Program (RIDP). The RIDP has been implemented in 12 municipalities. These processes have prioritized investments in small-scale rural infrastructure through the government budgetary allocation process. In total, the RIDP has invested $30 million USD since its inception in 2005 and is expected to invest $10.7 million USD and $7.6 million USD depending on the composition of target districts (see detailed breakdown in FS Chapter).

Since the start of the PDMR and PDNS projects in 2011, 1,474 projects have been undertaken in the 6 target municipalities, accounting for about 49% of all PDMR and PDNS projects nationally. In addition, between December 2014-2017, interventions funded Climate-resilience projects totaled $8.5 million USD or 25.3% of the total donor-funded projects nationally.

Total Allocation for PDMS/ PDNS projects from 2012 - 2021: $7M

### Infrastructure Investments

These infrastructure investments are not systematically informed or guided by rigorous and comprehensive climate resilient strategies, although some climate-proving standards have been applied on large scale sectoral investments. As infrastructure density increases, it will only leave much greater value of assets exposed to climate change-related risks. The current level of investment in climate risk management is inadequate and climate risk reduction strategies are weak and lacking. Without a comprehensive climate risk management framework, the risk of loss from climate change related events will increase.

### Adaptation Solutions

32. **Tinder Lakes Urgency**:
   - The need to safeguard communities and their physical assets from climate change-induced disasters.
   - To enhance the climate resilience of the decentralized small-scale infrastructure sector, the government requires knowledge, skills, and capacity to secure functional longevity of the infrastructure under the conditions of a changing climate.

33. **Additional Resilience**:
   - Land use in particular affects watershed functions (drainage control, erosion processes, and landslides susceptibility).
   - The complex factors of infrastructure engineering and land use change in the contact of catchment management need to be brought together as an integrated solution for a long-term resilience of people and their assets.
   - Relying solely on engineering solutions may not be sufficient given the complexity of climate change impacts, which are likely to increase over time.

34. **The Future Focus**:
   - A focus on engineering solutions only may complicate future climate change impacts in the long term.
   - Therefore, investments in climate proofing measures and land use management that are themselves adapted to climate change through land rehabilitation and management at the catchment scale.
   - This will ensure the long-term financial sustainability of the proposed adaptation alternatives.

35. **The Four Target Infrastructure Types**:
   - The following climate-proofing measures will be applied to the 4 target infrastructure types of decentralized small-scale rural infrastructures:

- **Revegetating land around rural water supply systems**: Climate-resilience approaches will include revegetating land around water sources, formalizing information sources (putting in pipes and collection/storage systems), and connection to existing sources in order to project water supply systems in light of climate change impacts.
- **Rehabilitating bridges**: Climate-resilient rehabilitation approaches will include: rehabilitation of bridges that are usually washed away in the rainy season using climate resilient practices such as the use of locally available materials like bamboo, grass, stabilization through implementation of sustainable structural measures (such as gabion baskets), re-sloping of drainage systems) that accommodate baseline levels of flows and projects levels of flow due to climate change.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (GCF) Funding Proposal, Page 17 of 99

### Installation of rural flood defences to withstand climate change impacts.
Climate-resilience approaches will include design and installation of flood defences to withstand the increasing frequency and intensity of floods due to climate change. The designs will include bioengineering methods to protect flood embankments from climate-induced erosion.

### Formalization of existing and new irrigation systems.
Climate-resilience approaches will include formalizing existing irrigation systems, as well as designing and installing new ones. These will be used for water use in prolonged dry seasons and mitigate against intensified droughts that are projected to occur with climate change. The project will ensure that the benefits of these schemes will be secured and made resilient to climate risks of intensified droughts which will enhance the resilience of local agricultural systems.

### Economic viability.
The project is also aligned with the finding that only those types of schemes are economically viable.

## Proposed Adaptation Solution

35. The proposed adaptation solution will enable transformational change by adopting a strategy to address the increasing costs of maintaining functional longevity of small-scale rural infrastructure under the mounting threats from climate and other environmental changes. While there is an increase in the projected future costs of constructing and maintaining vulnerable rural infrastructure progressively increases, but the local communities are not able to afford these costs due to their low income levels. The impacts of climate change on these exposed areas. However, as an alternative pathway, GCF resources can reduce the impact of climate change by providing a cost-effective solution that is more resilient than the baseline with bioengineering efforts and restoration of the catchment ecosystems within which these physical assets are located.

This alternative pathway will provide significant benefits in terms of reduced costs for construction, operation and maintenance compared to the baseline pathway. In addition, this adaptation delivers significant savings on future climate change impacts by providing a cost-effective solution that is more resilient than the baseline pathway. This represents a paradigm shift in local and national practices by introducing a cost-efficient and climate-resilient pathway that enables the long-term protection of rural communities and systems to the increasing risks brought by climate change.

## Barriers

36. To achieve the above adaptation alternative, there are a number of barriers, summarized below, that need to be overcome:

### Incomplete policies, standards and regulations
The laws and rules governing climate change are also changing, such as draft National Climate Change policy and FDR policy as well as some sectoral policies which consider inclusion of climate change. However, there remains regulatory and legislative limitation in implementing the climate change interventions prescribed in these draft policies. Moreover, the existing rural infrastructure is not designed to withstand the increasing frequency and intensity of floods due to climate change.

### Limited technical capacity
The limited technical capacity to engineer climate proofing measures for infrastructure

38. Technical capacity for compiling and analysing climate data for informing risk reduction practice is nascent. This includes capacities for producing hazard, risk and vulnerability maps and forecast bulletins. There is ongoing support

## DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND (FUNDING PROPOSAL), PAGE 18 OF 99**

### Weak capacity of municipal and village level institutions to plan, implement, and maintain rural infrastructure that is resilient to the increasing impacts of climate change

40. The sectoral legislative and institutional enabling environment does not currently take a risk-informed cross-sectoral approach to addressing and incorporating climate change considerations. This has resulted in poor spatial planning, land use, and infrastructure development. As such, there are no clear guidelines or policies that guide the municipal plan and develop resilient rural infrastructure. Already weak institutional functions and capacities at national/centralized level as well as provincial (district) and village (sub-council) levels. All stages of the District Investment Development Plan (DIDP) planning must entail climate change considerations, from project identification to implementation. The DIDP is a multi-year plan that guides the development of projects from sub-soil-level prioritization, to reviews at administrative post and municipal levels, and clearances at the Ministries of State and Provincial Governments. However, there are no guidelines or policies that guide the municipal manual currently does not include detailed guidelines for climate resilient infrastructure development. There is no plan that guides the municipality in identifying and addressing existing vulnerabilities and projected risks of climate change. Spatially expressed risk information at various timescales is not available to the DIDP planners and engineers. Furthermore, there are no clear guidelines or policies on how to integrate climate change considerations into existing vulnerabilities and projected risks. Furthermore, there is a lack of capacity at municipal level for designing climate resilient infrastructure. The Provincial District Investment Development Programme (PDID) programmes are critical for resilient development of local communities and infrastructure services.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 19 OF 99

#### Limited options for financing climate resilient decentralized small-scale rural infrastructure at sub-national level

41. When planning and implementing infrastructure investments through municipal / district and village development plans, the focus is on the short-term and long-term benefits of such investments to ensure a sustainable strategy to safeguard the investments. In both the short- and long-term, investments in ecosystems can protect and sustain livelihoods for rural communities. The proposed GCF project for Lesotho is focused on physical infrastructure. Consequently, the value of ecosystem-based approaches to climate risk management is not fully recognized within the current planning process at sub-national level, especially where areas requiring management are larger than individual succu villages). In such cases, integrated watershed management activities would not be considered in the succu plans nor brought up to the district plans. However, recognizing that these types of investments can have a significant impact on climate resilience, Lesotho has initiated the process of developing watershed management plans for select success.

42. The economic deprivation of rural communities and lack of alternative livelihoods leads to harmful land use practices resulting in soil erosion and degradation. This is further exacerbated by poor local communities to rehabilitate watersheds and adopt land use and livelihood practices that contribute to sustainable management of land and forest, which are essential for climate resilience. In Lesotho, there has been a lack of investment in such rehabilitation activities. Thus, substantial areas of land have been cleared of vegetation in Timor Leste and are under-utilized. At least 30% of the total area is degraded and vulnerable to desertification. This leads to increased soil erosion and other hazards risk to the communities and infrastructure, or it is not to utilize the socio-economic potential of agroforestry systems. The lack of investment in rehabilitation activities has led to a situation where there are no funds available for such investments. This results in additional costs of climate risk reduction to physical and economic assets. Hence there is currently no understanding on how to integrate ecosystem-based approaches into decentralized planning mechanisms due to the lack of climate risk information and methods on which to base such investment planning. These have traditionally been seen as a barrier to achieving national development objectives, including poverty reduction objectives. However, there are policy pre-conditions to integrate climate and disaster risk management into these decentralized planning mechanisms that requires adequate enforcement capacities.

43. The combination of these four key barriers has hindered particularly vulnerable communities from effectively planning, establishing and implementing adaptation measures for the protection of their physical and economic assets in the face of climate change. The proposed GCF project seeks to use the proposed adaptation alternative as a barrier-removal approach that will enable transformation and paradigm-shift in the current baseline towards a climate-resilient future.

### C.3 | Project / Programme Description

#### 44. The main objective of this project is to safeguard vulnerable communities and their physical assets from climate change-induced disasters. First, the project will strengthen technical capacities of mandated institutions to assess and monitor climate risks and vulnerabilities in rural areas. Second, the project will support the development of measures into policies and planning. GCF funds will be used to embed new technical skills, improve availability of risk information and tools for decision-making, and develop a framework for the integration of climate change and climate-proofing measures for small-scale rural infrastructure in order to build the resilience of vulnerable communities. The project will also support the development of an enabling environment that includes policy-based adaptation approaches for climate proofing of small-scale rural infrastructure that are essential to reducing prevalent social and economic vulnerabilities that will only worsen with climate change. ### Detailed Project / Programme Description

#### Activity 1.1: Develop and deliver climate risk information services and vulnerability mapping to all sectoral institutions

**4.g) The GCF investment will be used to introduce technology, methodologies and capacities for the development of climate risk information and the long-term institutional capacity to undertake hazard, risk and vulnerability mapping in the region. Activity 1.1 will include:**

- **Developing hazard maps:** These will be based on existing data and will be updated regularly.
- **Developing risk maps:** These will be based on hazard maps and will incorporate cost-benefit assessments for adaptation solutions and related training to responsible public servants across mandated institutions.
- **Hazard and risk maps will be used for risk-informed decision-making by the relevant institutions.** They will also provide a basis for zoning of development activity away from high hazards areas to avoid physical damages and economic losses to people, infrastructure and assets.

The hazard map will form the core of the baseline model which will be used as a tool for risk assessment in all main hazards - floods, landslides, soil erosion, and drought. The risk map will provide an indication of the potential socio-economic impacts of each hazard and produce vulnerability maps that will form the baseline. The baseline model will form the basis of future appraisal-led disaster risk management and climate-induced disaster risk management.

**5. This activity will introduce a bespoke GIS-based socio-economic risk model as a tool for risk assessment (including potential economic losses) and cost-benefit analysis and the identification and appraisal of climate resilient intervention measures for strategic planning in the future. The hazard will be used in combination with infrastructure data to produce vulnerability maps which will be used to identify areas at high risk from specific hazards (e.g., flood-prone areas), property, and socio-economic data to model the socio-economic impacts of each hazard and produce vulnerability maps that will form the baseline. The baseline model will form the basis of future appraisal-led disaster risk management and climate-induced disaster risk management.**

**5.a) Local field officers and village youth leaders will be trained in surveying techniques, including the use of global positioning systems (GPS) to undertake topographic surveys which will be required for the production and updating of hazard maps. The training will also include the use of remote sensing data to produce updated vulnerability maps. GFSI will also be effectively used to record the coordinates of the infrastructure (as part of the asset mapping and asset management process).**

**5.b) A series of technical staff trainings in climate risk modelling, mapping and vulnerability, cost benefit analysis and GIS will be conducted. The training will include a module on infrastructure planning and development and climate-induced disaster risk management, will be delivered.**

**densely populated areas. A series of trainings will be conducted on data management and analysis and data management standards and protocols will be introduced. 53. The existing Desmertaire database in Timor Leste contains some 1,600 datasets from 2001 to present. However, the scale and scope of the database needs to be expanded in order to systematically account for all physical damages and economic losses associated with climate hazards that will enable evidence-based planning and budgeting. The database should also include information on disaster risk management activities such as those for the storage, analysis and management of disaster data. Relevant inputs and reports will be managed on a simple real-time basis through an online system which will provide access to all relevant stakeholders in order to collate and track disaster reporting. The benefits will include: rapid and simple access to data; single data storage database; improved data quality; reduced costs for data collection and management; increased ownership and accountability; transparency; data quality control; timely resource allocation (to affected areas); costs savings for rapid assessment teams; reduced operational costs for damage and loss data collection and storage; and documentation of disaster risk management activities. The system will also provide a platform to improve the accuracy and capacity of developing real-time climate hazard data that is critical in informing and guiding policy design and planning, as well as in improving the effectiveness of disaster risk reduction.

54. Currently the cost of disaster risk management including an average of 10 to 15 rapid assessments per year, is around $600,000 USD or $1.5 million USD if site mobilisation and other items are factored in. It is estimated that the Risk Management System (RMS) will reduce costs by up to 70% through the use of a combination of qualitative estimations; it will result in long-term savings and efficiencies in institutional processes and will improve quality in disaster risk management activities. The RMS will also provide an improved understanding of the characteristics of the disaster/event. It will transfer the National Disaster Operational Centres (NDOC’s) role from being reactive to proactive, by providing a platform for early warning and preparedness; it will be more efficient in addition, it will act as conduit for innovation and improvement of PDNA and will be validated by data collected by drones.

55. GCF resources will also contribute towards the application of an asset location and condition survey method (developed by the World Bank) to assess the physical condition of infrastructure assets using a management database on which to base damage and loss monitoring of infrastructure. GCF resources will also enable the development of a Management Information System (MIS) for the Ministry of Social Solidarity (MISS), Ministry of Public Works (MoPW) national and municipal staff in the use and maintenance of the data collected from the asset location survey method. The MIS will be used to manage the data, while the asset management database will be used for planning, costing and prioritisation of asset maintenance using principles of portfolio risk assessment.

**Output 2: Climate risk reduction and climate-proofing measures for small-scale rural infrastructure are implemented at all stages of planning and implementation.**

57. Under this output, the project will work closely with the municipal and village level government investment programmes, including PDM and PDSS, to climate-proof local small infrastructure investments in the geographic focus areas. Actual physical investments will be accompanied by the development of essential capacities as well as institutional arrangements for the planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of climate-resilient development in the country. This output builds upon the SSR project, considering lessons learned and deepening the understanding of the challenges faced by small-scale rural infrastructure in the context of climate change.

58. The identification and prioritization of climate-proofing needs were undertaken as part of project design which included a risk assessment for all infrastructure units under the currently available national framework within which the existing and approved PDM and PDSS infrastructure had also been assessed. The risk assessment was undertaken using a combination of qualitative and quantitative methods, including expert judgment. The risk assessment incorporates the hazard maps, all receptor data of existing and planned infrastructure (including roads, water supply systems, etc.), and other relevant information such as vulnerability assessments (based on socio-economic indices) based on the latest 2015 census survey, and calculates the damages and losses to infrastructure, agriculture, and human health. The risk assessment also includes a climate change scenario analysis which assessed identified the extreme hazard categories of each hazard (moderate and high severity flood risk for productive land use; moderate and high severity landslide risk for residential land use; moderate and high severity wind risk for productive land use; moderate and high severity landslide risk for residential land use). ## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL), PAGE 29 OF 59

Verification, Evaluation and Supervision (VE&V) experts will be trained to determine the likelihood and consequences of risks to assets under test (infrastructure exposure and vulnerability). They will skills to engineer climate-resilient designs and apply various methods of bioregioning (e.g. by use of local vetiver plants to stabilize the slopes and gallow structures).

## 60. Interventions will embed the systematic use of climate hazard and risk information (to be developed under Activity 1.1) in the PDM project identification process to provide a more comprehensive, robust and evidence-based means of identifying projects that are likely to be resilient to climate change impacts. The project will also support the SPF staff in prioritizing projects at this level and in undertaking an appropriate level of feasibility studies on which to base climate-resilient design decisions. This will be achieved by providing technical assistance to the SPF staff, using criteria within the prioritization process and include other methods of measuring benefits of projects based on the integrated approach to project prioritisation using socio-economic cost-benefit analysis methods and tools as developed under Activity 1.1.

## 61. Capacity development will be provided to enhance the ability to incorporate climate-risk considerations into technical feasibility studies, including the use of climate risk-informed design methodologies and tools for assessing socio-economic cost-benefit analysis, optioneering and options appraisal methods as well as environmental impact assessment that integrate climate change impact scenarios, strengthening the feasibility process, safeguard investments and optimize engineering solutions.

At the detailed design level, technical assistance will be provided to introduce climate change considerations into design of infrastructure to ensure that they will accommodate likely changes of environmental variables (frequency and intensity of extreme weather events) and other climate-related impacts. This will include the development in the new climate-risk informed infrastructure detailed design methods and will include specific training in the design of bioregioning solutions for slope stabilization, erosion control and vegetation diversity options will be accomplished through technical assistance and by providing dedicated training events.

In order to enhance the ability of infrastructure contractors to implement climate-resilient construction, the project will introduce a new set of guidelines and specifications for the design and construction of rural road infrastructure in implementation of climate-resilient projects, experience of implementing climate-resilient projects, experience of contract management and supervision of climate-resilient projects. The project will also seek to embed all learning from these activities into the training curriculum of the Ministry of Public Works and Roads through course curriculum and certification and using training of trainers approach. Further details can be found in Annex II if feasibility study.

## 64. The project will strengthen the monitoring capacity at Administrative Post (AP) level through the provision of appropriate tools, equipment and training for AP staff to undertake regular monitoring of infrastructure assets and post-monitoring to enable compliance with new resilience standards and requirements. The project will also seek to embed all learning from these activities into the training curriculum of the Ministry of Public Works and Roads through course curriculum and certification and using training of trainers approach. Further details can be found in Annex II if feasibility study.

### Guidelines and Technical Specifications

In order to ensure continuity and support to these new roles and functions the project will support the development of the following manuals and guidelines for Climate-resilient Rural Infrastructure Projects in Timor Leste (SCRIPIT):

- Manuals for the design and construction of rural road infrastructure – in collaboration with Ministry of Public Works and Roads
- Manualguidelines for the design and construction of small irrigation schemes – in collaboration with Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries
- Manualguidelines for the design and construction of rural water supply infrastructure – in collaboration with Ministry of Public Works and Roads

### Technical Specifications

Technical specifications for rural infrastructure will be reviewed and revised to improve the quality and adaptability of the construction to climate change for the various infrastructure types. The Technical Specifications forming part of the contract documents will be updated to include climate-resilient design principles and methodologies in all relevant sections for various elements such as excavation, gravel, concrete, masonry works, drainage, retaining walls, etc. There will be a separate specification for each element of construction that is likely to be affected by climate change with each road, bridge and drainage infrastructure asset (for example, scour-checks, check-dams, rip-raps, gabions

### Activity 2.2: Implementation of climate-proofing measures for small-scale rural infrastructure

#### Key Interventions:
- **Irrigation land around rural water supply systems**
- **Formalization and expansion of existing irrigation systems**
- **Revegetation of degraded lands**
- **Construction of rural flood defences (protective dikes, gabions, and bio-engineered defences)**
- **Revegetation of land around rural water supply systems to reduce soil erosion and improve the landscape**
- **Reduction in extreme drought events through slope stabilization**

#### Areas Covered:
- **Six target districts: Baucau, Ermera, Atauro, Lautem, Liquica, and Viqueque.**

#### Project Costs:
- Total cost of 130 infrastructure units identified is USD 245 Million per year between 2011 and 2016 nationally.
- Over the 5 years of infrastructure implementation (PDIM and PDRS co-financing), the project will cover approximately 97% of the total base cost of construction or rehabilitation of selected infrastructure units.

### Cost-Benefit Analysis

73. A cost-benefit analysis has been conducted on all 130 units and they have been ranked and prioritized based on their internal rates of return (IRR) and the number of associated beneficiaries, into high, medium and low priority (dark to light blue cells in Table below) which will dictate the order or priority in which they will be implemented during each increment. The cost-benefit analysis is expected to be completed first. The IRR calculation includes the cost of maintenance (periodic and annual) that would be required over a 20-year period.

74. Rehabilitation of rural bridges and slope stabilization of rural roads are the most expensive accounting for 57% of total project costs (and 36% of the number of projects), with only 34% of all interventions falling in the high and medium priority categories. This is a reflection of the fact that these interventions will be implemented on rural roads and corridors despite the large benefits that road projects will bring to rural communities. For example, without maintenance, roads will deteriorate over time and become impassable, which will have negative impacts for all priority categories. It is also a reflection of the isolation of some communities that will benefit from the climate resilient roads and bridges. The rehabilitation of these assets is necessary in order to ensure that they are maintained properly.

75. Revegetating land around rural water supply systems has the two highest proportions of interventions in the high and medium priority categories (91%) which reflects the relatively low capital and maintenance costs, compared with the numbers of beneficiaries, as well as the socio-economic gains that will be realized by rural communities. Revegetation activities relate to the protection of water sources from sedimentation, reduced functional capacity of water sources, pollution of water sources due to sedimentation being washed into the water sources, and reduced functionality of water sources.

76. As part of the climate proofing of water supply systems, there is usually a need to address these local land degradation issues in order to ensure that the interventions are sustainable over time. This will be reflected in the infrastructure costs of the water source infrastructure. This is a typical engineering practice to stabilize the land prior to construction and to ensure that the intervention is sustainable.

77. The cost-benefit analysis indicated that the revegetation under 2.2 is not the same as the agro-forestry and reforestation under Activity 2.3.

structural measures implemented (e.g. soil-engineering and broader sub-catchment restoration and management), it is noted that actions under Activity 2.3 will address water-based methods of resilience and will have attendant benefits on flood risk management, but the cost-benefit of these has been assessed separately (see Activity 7).

75. With regards to the use of hydro-meteorological data in the climate proofing measures of water supply systems, the most common system in rural Timor-Leste for both water supply systems and irrigation schemes is gravity feed (from springs or wells) with a single pipe distribution network. The project will address this by providing improved irrigation and water supply units embodying consideration of drought discharge volumes and recharge rates to ensure continued operation during dry seasons, as well as the provision of storage capacity in reservoirs to reduce the risks of drying out water sources and catchment rehabilitation to stabilize water yielding capacities of the catchments. For the water supply systems, this approach has been designed based on the population (beneficiaries) served by each system (the average of 35 litres per person per day for water supply systems) with provision for population growth for at least 20 years or the design life of the water supply systems, and 10% increase in demand due to climate change. The project will also provide a buffer storage capacity that increases the water needs/consumption (see Section 11.1.6 of the FS for further details).

80. Taking into account the above and with the aim of addressing the deficits of small-scale rural infrastructure, the project will work on improving the functionality of existing channels and rehabilitation of new channels, which will reduce transmission losses, improve the servability to farms and increase the efficiency of the channels during dry times. The project will also provide a buffer storage capacity that increases water needs/consumption (see Section 11.1.6 of the FS for further details). This will have positive impacts: (1) increased capacity of the community to produce crops more than once per year and (2) increase in cultivable land.

81. With regards to the interventions relating to water supply, water storage reservoirs will improve supply reliability during dry season and reservoirs will be managed by the water management group (WMG) that will agree on the operating rules during the construction of the reservoirs. The WMGs will also be responsible for regular maintenance and monitoring of physical structures of the existing and future infrastructure and catchment rehabilitation for its runoff formation. The project will provide control services ensure stability and functionality of the target infrastructure in the face to evolving climate risks.

## Investment Planning

85. As discussed above, currently the PDIM and PNDS do not have funding criteria or requirement to embed additional cost of climate risk reduction to physical and economic assets and there is currently no understanding of the investment requirements for climate proofing infrastructure due to the lack of climate-risk information and methods on which to base such investments.

86. To address this barrier, the project will develop and implement new approaches to investment planning to ensure that infrastructure investment including annual and periodic maintenance which can be met in the long-term and will include embedding climate proofing:

- Embedding climate proofing in the PDIM and PNDS point identification and screening processes from village level project identification through to project feasibility, design and costing.
- Identification of cost-benefit analysis tools for use within a framework of community-based scheme that involve the use of tariffs or in-kind contributions to establish municipal maintenance programmes (e.g. GMP as being a model).
- Development of municipal infrastructure investment plans based on risk-informed project designs, including maintenance and cost-benefit analyses on the basis of methods described in Activity 1.1.
- Use of municipality-based tools for technical guidance for central budget allocation to cover annual maintenance and cost of climate resilient rural infrastructure utilising the climate proofing methods introduced by the project.

87. Providing evidence of the long-term need for maintenance to safeguard infrastructure investments and assisting the development of a municipal asset management information system (e.g., GMP as being a model) (PMR) and associated cost-benefit analysis. Furthermore, the CBA tools to be developed by the project will be embedded in the PMR and will assist with the identification of investment needs.

88. The annual and periodic maintenance cost for each of the 130 infrastructure units has been identified during project planning as part of the design phase of the infrastructure design. Environmental maintenance which may be required to meet damage caused by unexpected events, by definition, cannot be forecasted and therefore has not been budgeted in areas of high environmental risk. Maintenance activities will be undertaken on an ad hoc basis when they are undertaken based on risk-informed climate hazard information and will include contingency investment planning for such incidents. The O&M strategy is detailed at the end of this document for each infrastructure unit and limit the extent of the damages. The O&M strategy is detailed in the O&M annex (Annex XIII b).

89. To ensure sustainability of CMA, the project will strengthen the technical, financial and institutional capacity for the CMA and its partners to manage assets effectively. This includes developing a comprehensive asset management plan that reflects current and future requirements for maintenance by developing and embedding asset management system tools and approaches in line with international best practices (e.g., GMP as being a model). As described under Activity 1.2 and 1.3 the project is developing essential processes for infrastructure asset management including, maintaining a systematic record of individual assets costs, original service life, remaining useful life, physical condition, maintenance history, and other relevant information; implementing a comprehensive plan of infrastructure including repair and replacement; and implementing and managing asset information systems as well as supporting tools to ensure that the CMA has the capacity to manage its assets effectively.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Infrastructure Unit for the Mopan of the Unit

### Harmonization of PDM and PNDS Infrastructure into a Maintenance Programme

The project will also implement a strategy for the harmonisation of the PDM and PNDS infrastructure into a maintenance programme with regular budget allocated for maintenance, thus enhancing the service life of the infrastructure. This is in line with the Government’s commitment to the implementation of the provisions of the Decree Law 20/2015, which embeds O&M responsibility under the respective Municipal Service with MSO having the overall responsibility.

### Long-Term O&M Financing

Furthermore, regarding long-term O&M financing, the project will embed financing models for investment maintenance costs (e.g., of community-based schemes that involve the use of tariffs or linked contributions to establish a fund for infrastructure maintenance) and/or other innovative financing mechanisms (such as green bonds or blended financing) using CBA approaches (being developed under Activity 1.2). It will use asset portfolio risk management approach to identify critical assets, prioritise them and develop an investment strategy that will guide the development of long-term infrastructure investment strategies which will help government to identify the infrastructure O&M needs and mechanisms for financing these needs.

### Supporting Catchment Management and Rehabilitation Measures

#### Activity 2.3: Supporting Catchment Management and Rehabilitation Measures to Enhance Climate Resilient Infrastructure and Communities

This activity will scale-up climate resilient catchment management in order to reduce the exposure of communities and infrastructure to natural hazards, including climate change, to develop more hazard-resilient communities. Even with improved approaches to be introduced and implemented, it will still be necessary to implement catchment rehabilitation and management to re-establish proper catchment ecosystem functions in order to safeguard infrastructure and climate proofing of infrastructure.

#### Project Implementation

The project will assist MAF in prioritization of catchments for rehabilitation, defining the methods of rehabilitation and restoration activities that will contribute to long-term resilience. The project will also support MAF in developing strategies that will include community engagement and training in forestry and agroforestry practices that will lead to direct or indirect benefits for communities, including improved livelihoods which ensure land use practices that contribute to overall risk reduction and long-term climate resilience.

#### Project Implementation

This project will be implemented via catchment management adaptive actions which include initiatives of holistic landscape restoration and rehabilitation at the catchment scale. The project includes a catchment-based agroforestry and reforestation strategy based on climatic risk information to be developed by the GCF project. The UNDP-GCF project will work with the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries (MAF) and the National Directorate for Forestry (DNF) in developing an integrated landscape restoration and rehabilitation strategy that includes agroforestry strategies for upstream catchments of target infrastructure.

#### Specific Agroforestry and Reforestation Activities

More specifically, agroforestry and reforestation activities refer to catchment management measures which will address water catchment degradation which has led to increased flood, erosion and landslides risks which in turn threaten the sustainability of infrastructure. The project will develop an integrated landscape restoration and agroforestry and reforestation strategy based on climatic risk information to be developed by the GCF project.

The project will develop strategies and actions for catchment rehabilitation, including selection of new species, methods of rehabilitation and maintenance that are fully

## Green Climate Fund (Funding Proposal), Page 33 of 99

Informed by the hazard maps to be developed under Activity 1.1, I will delineate the priority hazardous areas for a phased rehabilitation strategy that will assist in the identification of plant species that will provide the soil, water and nutrient protection properties needed to address degradation of catchment. Several varieties of tree species have already been identified as being suitable for agroforestry systems in the target catchments. These varieties provide proper catchment ecosystem functions in order to safeguard infrastructure and climate proofing investments from long-term environmental changes. The project will also develop a strategy for the sustainable management and maintenance of infrastructure in the future. The agroforestry activities will be fully aligned with the relevant policies, especially those related to land use planning, forest policy, and water resources management. This will ensure that the markets and linkages being developed by GoSL ensure the sustainability of the agro-forestry activities.

## 95. GCF resources combined with co-finance from the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries (MAF) and the National Directives for Forest Management (NDFM) will be used to develop a long-term strategy and action plan for rehabilitation strategies, including agroforestry, for catchments upstream of target infrastructure assets. The project will develop analysis of the current state of the catchment in terms of soil erosion, sedimentation, land degradation, water quality maintenance that are fully informed by the climate hazard maps to be developed under Activity 1.1. The maps will delineate priority hazardous areas for a phased rehabilitation strategy that will assist in the identification of plant species that will provide the soil, water and nutrient protection properties needed to address degradation of catchment. Several varieties of tree species have already been identified as being suitable for agroforestry systems in the target catchments. These varieties are growing trees that is typically cultivated for timber. Acacia gumwood that is increasingly being used for agroforestry it projects for its nitrogen fixation properties, but also for shade it creates for other trees, such as wood. It is a resistant tree species to climate change and can be planted in areas where the soil has been degraded. Other Suwari are also used for their timber and fast burning properties. Teak, mahogany, sandalwood and coconut have also been identified as being suitable for agroforestry systems in the target catchments. The project will adopt a multi-stakeholder approach to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the process.

The project will asset MAF and MCIE in developing a long-term strategy and plan for agroforestry as a catchment-wide ecosystem service, which will be aligned with the national forest policy and the NDFM. The project will also work with each municipality, based on the ability to address soil erosion and land degradation. The main benefit of this activity will be the reduction in sedimentation and soil erosion in the catchments that are targeted for rehabilitation activities. This includes: gradual improvement of the geo-physical and hydrological condition of the catchment, reduced incidence of flooding and landslides, improved water quality, increased biodiversity, provision of timber and non-timber forest products, provision of short and long term and sustainable economic benefits to project beneficiaries, and the promotion of the creation of new jobs.

## 96. Actual catchment rehabilitation works will be conducted in the catchment areas that are particularly hazardous across the target districts. These areas will require reforestation in order to stabilise the soil, limit erosion, and stem degradation of the ecosystem services provided by these catchments. The project will also involve a series of management works will involve convergence of existing government reforestation programmes with collective action by the local communities. This will be achieved through the development of a community-based agroforestry model and the proposed project will also follow the same established practice. The project will support the MAF directors on the ground to ensure that they are able to provide technical assistance in the catchments where they operate, on areas of high exposure to climate risk. Such climate risk informed prioritization will be based on hazard modeling and mapping of a range of climatic change scenarios. Based on hazard risk information developed during the feasibility study, the project will identify priority sites for rehabilitation works and develop appropriate agroforestry schemes. The project will work with the MAF to design and deliver this support to their existing reforestation and catchment management programmes in order to ensure that they are aligned with the national forest policy and in line with MAF SDP (2011-2030) objective 3.4 which aims to ‘To develop capacity for improved decision making in planning, implementation and monitoring of forest rehabilitation activities’. The project will also work with a team of rehabilitation experts agroforestry will be used as an element of catchment management. The MAF forestry and MCIE staff and local experts will engage community leaders to ensure the development of cooperatives for sustainable agroforestry.

## 97. At the local level, and based on the agroforestry strategies to be developed, the project will support farmers in developing their own agroforestry systems through opportunities offered by identifying agricultural cooperatives and cooperatives which will serve to facilitate members or groups in developing community-based agroforestry. These activities will be carried out with the involvement of local communities and will be organized according to type of agroforestry (e.g. coconut growers association) and by geography (e.g. on an Administrative post level) to provide logistical support to farmers. In addition, these groups will include women’s associations as well as youth

### C.4. Background Information on Project | Programme Sponsor (Executing Entity)

#### 100. The main project sponsor is the Ministry of Commerce, Industry and Environment (MCIE). The MCIE mandate gives it the responsibility for the implementation of the project. The MOIE will be responsible for overall coordination and implementation.

The MOIE, and more specifically the National Directorate for Climate Change (NDCC), will be the project’s implementing agency. The NDCC is a technical body under the Ministry of Social Solidarity (MSS) and Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries (MAF) will serve as additional resource partners.

#### 101. The project is implemented in line with established Government of Timor Leste and UNDP procedures in Timor Leste. The NDCC will be responsible for technical guidance and oversight on all project inputs, activities and outputs. The project formulation process will be used to further define management, coordination and communication arrangements between the MOIE and the NDCC. This will include a detailed plan for project implementation including the validation of project activities, preparation of project outputs, and participation in trainings. The NDCC will be involved to every as many and as early as possible.

The 2015-2019 NDCC budget is US$83,605,000. Between the Country Programme focal areas, resources are distributed as follows: Resilience Building (22%); Inclusive and sustainable development (43%); and Democratic governance (35%). In the last four years (2015-2019), the average of country office delivery in NDCC projects was 87%. The NDCC has a strong track record for project implementation. Its implementation include: Leadership UN Resident Coordinator and UNDP Resident Representative (1); Country Director (1); Deputy Country Director (1); Project Officer (1); Programme Officer (2); Climate Change Analysis/Heat of Communication and Management Support Unit (1); Operations; Head of Hillfort Analytical Unit (1); Head of NDCC Office (1); Senior Technical Advisor (1); Climate Change Analyst (1); Common Premises Manager (1); Field Security Associate (1).

The NDCC was formerly known as the National Directorate for International Environment and Climate Change (NDEACC).

### C.5. Market Overview (If applicable)

103. Agriculture dominates economic activities in Timor Leste (IV Constitutional Government Program, 2007-2012). It is subsistence agriculture with low inputs and outputs, and comprises crops and livestock, fisheries and forestry. It is estimated that over 95% of the population depends on agriculture for their livelihoods. Crops such as rice, cassava, potato, mung bean, peanut, and soya bean are widely grown in farmlands on steep slopes. The exception to this is the lowland areas where maize, sorghum, millet, and wheat are cultivated. In addition, coconut, palm oil, cocoa, coconut and candlenut are important and are grown on specific slopes and bands of elevations. For example, coffee plants are found in the coastal areas of some districts such as Baucau and Viqueque. Low agricultural production combined with a high population density has led to food insecurity in Timor Leste. The World Bank (2013) estimated that 74 percent of the rural population suffers moderate and severe food insecurity. Annual food deficits also contribute to malnutrition, especially among children under five years old.

104. Despite the heavy reliance on agriculture for subsistence and for income generation, there is a lack rural infrastructure to support agricultural productivity including access to markets and what infrastructure exists is at a considerable distance from the areas of production. The World Bank (2013) estimated that in 2010, the Gini coefficient measured the living standards across the country against the asset index. The sucos with highest living standards are concentrated on the coast and in the central highlands where there is better access to infrastructure. These are also the most remote and have small population. Access to infrastructure is higher in sucos with higher living standards, and there is a strong correlation between access to infrastructure and living standards. In 2010, only 35% of the sucos had average share of households with electricity is over 90%. This compares with an average share of 60% in the 80 sucos with the highest living standards. Access to improved water and improved sanitation is also much higher in sucos with high living standards.

105. The project is using agroforestry as a strategy to improve catchment management practice and stabilize the land. Degraded and deforested watersheds significantly exacerbate disaster risks and associated risks of physical damage and economic loss, especially for smallholder farmers who are often unable to afford insurance or other forms of infrastructure. Potential of such a strategy is enormous in Timor Leste. Over 30% of its land area is under-utilized and it could be used as agroforestry.

106. FAO (2010/2013) reports that 264 ha of trees were planted by 2010 for both productive purposes (timber, pulpwood, biofuel) and protection purposes (carbon, soil erosion, water regulation). These areas span a continuum of forest types from degraded to primary forests. In 2010, the total area under forest was 354,786 ha. By 2010, the annual increase in planted forests was 5M ha annually. Globally, FAO further suggests that this for future world food security and poverty reduction is a critical strategy to meet the growing demand for wood products. It seems clear that the role of forest plantations as source of food and wood products will continue to increase.

107. In Timor Leste today, nearly all urban and rural households use fuelwood for cooking. This predominance of fuelwood in the domestic energy sector is a major contributor to deforestation and degradation of forests which are critical to the economy and the general poverty of the population. In 2006, the World Bank (2007) estimated that total annual fuelwood use was 15 million cubic meters, with an average consumption per household of about 3.4 m³ of biomass/ha/year. As part of this study, sawnwood (Eucalyptus udaya at 2.5 x 2.5 bundles) was purchased in the Dili market for a national selling price of US$0.17/kg. If all the fuelwood consumed in Timor Leste was monetized, the value would be approximately US$34 million per year. The current methods used to harvest and process sawnwood are processing practices using chainsaws are extremely wasteful and inefficient, capturing only an estimated 15% of the volume of wood cut. This project will address this issue by providing training in sustainable harvesting techniques and become actively involved in establishing, harvesting and processing domestically-grown forest trees for local use and for export. The project will be the first such targeted effort to facilitate the community-led and development in the sector of agroforestry.
