# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (Funding Proposal), Page 38 of 99

### MPTs

108. Based on the geomorphological condition of the target sites the proposed design of the Agroforestry intervention is a Agro-forestry System (AGS). This design has three major components: Multi-Purpose Trees (MPT), Cash Crops (CC) and Fruit Bearing Trees (FBT).

109. MPTs will be planted as hedgerows to address soil erosion and control water flow. The MPTs will also serve as local inoculants for the agroforestry system, providers of natural pesticides and insecticides and alternative fuel wood source.

Leaves of MPTs are also excellent feed source for poultry and livestock. CCs will be the immediate source of income of the project beneficiaries. These trees will be planted in a way that they can be intercropped with other crops or can be cultivated along the alley in between the hedgerows of MPTs. Any CCs that will thrive on the area can be grown if it is not affected by the presence of MPTs.

110. The selection of MPTs was done based on the type of soil and climate conditions, as well as the type of CCA that will produce simultaneously each planting season to increase the volume of production and facilitate intercropping with other crops. The species were selected from the most common agroforestry trees in the municipalities.

The species were selected based on the most commonly available and widely grown species in the municipalities.

The 1971 Forest Act prohibits cutting down trees, which is a major practice for land clearing prior to agricultural cultivation. The intervention will also serve as a precursor in the development of it culture of planting and caring for trees alike for among the project stakeholders.

### D.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

Without GCF investment, vulnerable communities (mainly engaged in smallholder farming) will continue to be susceptible to climate-related disasters and their associated impacts.

### D.2 Exit Strategy

134. The project’s sustainability and exit strategy is rooted in the key elements of design and implementation. The following assumes long-term benefits beyond the project implementation period:

Investment in human resources and institutions: This element is focused on developing knowledge institutions that have skilled human resources as well as adequate information, tools and technologies to effectively pursue their mandate in climate risk management. Project investments will improve the availability of risk information and create effective institutional arrangements for managing climate risks. The project will also establish a robust system of short-to-long term personnel learning and training programs at all scales – including community, Administrative Post (AP), district, provincial and national levels.

Investment in systems: This element is focused on developing knowledge institutions that have skilled human resources as well as adequate information, tools and technologies to effectively pursue their mandate in climate risk management. Project investments will improve the availability of risk information and create effective institutional arrangements for managing climate risks. The project will also establish a robust system of short-to-long term personnel learning and training programs at all scales – including community, Administrative Post (AP), district, provincial and national levels.

135. Investment in human resources and institutions: This element is focused on developing knowledge institutions that have skilled human resources as well as adequate information, tools and technologies to effectively pursue their mandate in climate risk management. Project investments will improve the availability of risk information and create effective institutional arrangements for managing climate risks. The project will also establish a robust system of short-to-long term personnel learning and training programs at all scales – including community, Administrative Post (AP), district, provincial and national levels.

136. Most importantly, cost-benefit assessment methodologies and a socio-economic risk model will be embedded into the planning processes for the Integrated Municipal Development Planning (PIMD) and National Village Development Plans (PNVD). This will ensure that climate risks are considered in all development plans and that financial resources are available after GCF assistance ends for the government to better analyze climate risks associated with investments, identify opportunities for adaptation, and implement measures to reduce costs or finance cost-effective risk reduction measures, and maintain infrastructure investment over time. The cost-benefit modeling initiated as part of this effort will be used to support efficient use of financing.

137. Investment in social and economic assets of vulnerable communities: The damaging effects of extreme events and changes in weather patterns exacerbated by climate change will unravel the potential for socioeconomic

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 42 OF 99

### Development in vulnerable and isolated communities. However, investing in small-scale infrastructure in a way that is resilient to climate change impacts can help reduce the vulnerability of these areas. Infrastructure that is resilient to climate change impacts will be more likely to provide long-term benefits for the community. The provision of infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and pipes will, in turn, help secure essential services such as water supply, electricity generation, and health care. In addition, investments in resilient infrastructure can reduce the impact of climate impacts while providing protection against multiple hazards that dominate the country’s hazard profile (flooding, droughts, landslides). The project will provide a framework for long-term resilience through adaptation and implementation cycles by embedding climate risk information and enhancing the capacity to prioritize, budget, locate and implement resilient infrastructure. This will ensure that investments in infrastructure are aligned with the sustainability of infrastructure climate proofing efforts for the long-term. In addition, this will provide the potential to mobilize co-financing while absorbing the additional cost of climate proofing in the future. Through municipal and private sector finance mechanisms.

#### 138. Investment in natural capital: The functional capacity of the landscape plays an essential role in maintaining long-term resilience for communities by providing ecosystem services that help buffer against the impacts of extreme weather changes in weather patterns, especially in island community of complex and harrn terrain. For example, stable adjacent slopes and healthy vegetation will keep the soil from climate proofing infrastructure proposed above. A natural capital approach is critical for the project to be able to provide a long-term resilience framework that also accounts for, and promotes the maintenance of, the ecosystem services provided by the natural environment in the project area. The project will therefore include an investment in natural capital through the establishment of areas dedicated to climate protection, such as sea defences or flood walls, interconnections in water supply, or retrofitting of all infrastructure to ensure that it is resilient to climate change impacts. This will be a critical component for long-term sustainability of social and economic assets, such as those that the GCF project will fund.

#### 139. Post-project GCM: In addition to protecting investments via maintaining the natural surroundings, the government co-financing supporting district municipal and village investment mechanisms will also include budgeted GCM costs for a period of five years after the end of the project (please refer to Annex XIX (b) for details). This will be as well as locally defined roles and responsibilities for control, protection and maintenance to sustain the GCF investments.

#### 140. In terms of maintenance of infrastructure, MSA is committed to provide GCM to 130 infrastructure units to be implemented by the project, during and after the end of the project (please refer to MSA’s co-financing letter, Annex XVII). The project will also include a maintenance plan for all infrastructure units that are part of the water supply network to be operated and maintained by relevant line ministry practitioners sitting under MSA at the municipal level. (please refer to GCM and Annex XIX (b)).

#### 141. In addition, the project will assist relevant government institutions in developing long-term GCM financial planning to ensure that all infrastructure units implemented within the project area are maintained over time. The project interventions have been designed to strengthen financing and implementation of GCM in the long-term. This is achieved by embedding climate proofing into the design of infrastructure projects, as well as embedding CBA, asset management and portfolio risk management methods that ensures the systematic identification of risks associated with climate change impacts on infrastructure. In addition to ensuring that GCM is integral to climate proofing of infrastructure, the project will also implement a strategy for the harmonization of GCM across all relevant government institutions through a training programme with regular budget allocated for maintenance, thus enhancing the service life of the infrastructure units.

#### 142. Specifically, the project will provide the following safeguards for financial sustainability:

- **Risk management:** The project will develop climate proofing and adaptation interventions based on climate risk information to be developed by the project, and development of a financing model for the long-term maintenance (unlike current practice).
- **Strengthening multi-disciplinary approach to GCM for a harmonised GCM framework for rural infrastructure**
- **Advising the government on optimal/efficient allocation of funds for climate proofing and GCM through financial planning, budgeting and investment strategies.**
- **Enhancing community-government dialogues around local infrastructure needs, risks and risk reduction strategies and their financing**

### E.1 Impact Potential

#### 143. The project will increase resilience and enhance livelihoods of the most vulnerable segments of population, particularly women and children, in the target municipalities of Timor Leste that are among the most vulnerable in terms of poverty indices, infrastructure deficits, gaps in basic public services and social vulnerabilities, and high levels of community exposure to climate induced disasters. Selected rural districts encompass a diversity of terrain, including mountainous zones, river valleys, coastal plains, and low-lying areas. The degree of vulnerability to climate change impacts and disaster development are varying.

Under Output 2, the total number of direct beneficiaries will reach approximately 175,840 people (93% women) in 67 villages across 14 municipalities. In addition, an estimated 175,840 people (93% women) and some 300 ha of land will benefit from agroforestry and reforestation activities.

The main areas of adaptation are: i) flood risk management systems; ii) irrigation systems; iii) water supply systems; iv) drainage systems; v) improved irrigation systems (45.19km); vi) food reserves (14.83 km2) - will be made more resilient to climate change induced disasters; and vii) 300 ha of land will be under a combination of agroforestry, reforestation and catchment management measures.

In the 67 target municipalities climate-induced hazards currently have the following impacts on people, infrastructure and agriculture:

- Dwellings (number of homes affected): 14,663 homes affected by floods; 87,139 homes affected by drought; 57,919 homes affected by erosion; 84,853 homes affected by landslides
- Water supply systems: 20,000 people at risk from water supply sources; 66,027 homes at risk from floods; 75,046 homes at risk from drought; 76,049 homes at risk from erosion; 75,186 homes at risk from landslides
- Rural roads (length of rural roads impacted): 323.69 km impacted by floods; 2,143 km impacted by landslides
- Agricultural land: 10,000 ha affected by floods; 75,000 ha affected by landslides; 10,000 ha affected by floods; 40,598ha affected by erosion; 40,598ha affected by drought.

The 20 flood control projects identified will have the effect of averting $37,000 USD in annual economic damages to the target communities, impacting 13,336 beneficiaries and providing a total of 1.6 million USD in benefits. The 50 slope stabilization projects will have an impact on 24,987 other services for 50,297 beneficiaries and will substantially increase their economic activity from current values of $1.68 million USD combined. The 30 water supply project identified will have the effect of averting $10,000 USD in annual economic damages to the target communities, impacting 4,000 beneficiaries and providing a total of 250,000 USD in benefits. The 60 agroforestry and reforestation schemes will reach 50,246 beneficiaries and will enhance income from crops, currently a value of $248,485 USD.

Monitoring & Evaluation: Balance participation to make M&E more effective

#### 150. Total number of beneficiaries and reduced annual economic losses under baseline and CC scenarios were calculated using a GIS-based socio-economic risk model that was developed for the project area (the same as the one used in the feasibility study) and also for all of Timor-Leste (official statistics of the 2017 Census), and a GIS-based socio-economic risk model that was developed for the project area. The number of beneficiaries per infrastructure unit was determined using a polygon layer that was used to define "catchment" areas of beneficiaries. The risk map layers were used to first identify whether the road falls within the High Risk Zone (HRZ) or not. Using a series of criteria, the beneficiary households were determined based on the 2015 census. For flood defences, the location of the proposed projects was identified using Google Earth imagery into digitise polygons of beneficiary areas and combined with the Flood Risk Map. The number of beneficiaries per infrastructure unit was determined by identifying the number of households within a polygon whose centroid falls within the HRZ. The total number of beneficiaries was determined if the project is meant to benefit the whole community, the total household and population in the irrigation scheme area were used as the denominator for determining the number of beneficiaries. It should be noted that the population groups benefited by individual infrastructure works do not overlap; therefore, the sum of all infrastructure units will give a higher number than the actual number of beneficiary households in the sucos in which the infrastructure unit is being implemented. With linear infrastructure projects such as roads and water supply systems, the start and end points are defined using road names or river names as start and/or end points of the infrastructure; but beneficiary households are different[46].

#### 151. GIS data on people, property and agricultural land enabled the enumeration and, in some cases, the quantification of the number of beneficiaries per infrastructure unit. The socio-economic risk model was used to determine a climate change projection. Findings of GIS-based socio-economic risk mapping (e.g., vulnerable regions, population affected, infrastructure, property, and agricultural land damaged) and related economic losses form the basis of the calculations.

### E2. Paradigm Shift Potential

#### Degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project/programme investment

**E2.1. Potential for scaling up and replication (Provide a numerical multiple and supporting rationale)**

The total population of the six target districts of Baucau, Ermera, Atau, Viqueque, Lautem and Liquiçá is 522,000 people, or 43% of the total population of the province. The proposed project also has replication potential in the remaining 7 municipalities which would benefit the remainder of the population.

The paradigm shift lies in the proposed strategy to address increasing cost of maintaining functional longevity of assets and infrastructure in the face of climate change. Baseline pathways will be progressively increasing costs of infrastructure, necessitating changes in construction requirements, addition of protection features, and increases in operations and maintenance costs. The alternative pathway is a proposed paradigm shift that would reduce these costs by introducing new skills and methods for generating climate risk information and enhancing engineering capacities.

The steps necessary to achieve this paradigm shift are as follows: First, the project is introducing new skills and methods for generating climate risk information and enhancing engineering capacities. By introducing new modeling techniques and tools, the project will provide a more comprehensive understanding of climate change impacts on infrastructure. This will enable better decision-making in terms of design and implementation of infrastructure projects that can withstand future climate conditions.

Secondly, the project is enhancing the enabling environment for POM and PONES funding sources for rural infrastructure development. The project will be supported by longer term investment plans and improvements in broader policies of climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.

The limited nature and decline in investment in rural infrastructure through the PDGR and PNGS rural planning processes as well as the perceived high cost of including climate resilience into infrastructure development and investment planning. This project approach to community engagement through the PDGR and PNGS rural planning processes will result in greater transparency and therefore buy-in, greater community ownership and consequently greater efficiency in project implementation.

Thirdly, the project will bring about both improvements and restoration activities in catchment ecosystems to improve ecosystem function and resilience, which will reduce the vulnerability of communities and infrastructure in all catchments in TL. This degradation has increased exposure of residents and infrastructure to climate-induced hazards such as flooding, landslides, droughts and heatwaves. The project will also promote a shift towards promotion of climate-smart agriculture and forestry activities. Not only will these efforts improve ecosystem function and enhance water availability, but they will also provide significant social and economic benefits to the communities.

In addressing climate change impacts, the project is further building resilience in infrastructure development and reducing the long-term cost of infrastructure maintenance.

160 The project has particularly strong emphasis on knowledge generation, learning and information sharing/dissemination. First, the project will introduce hazard and risk assessment modeling and mapping tools and generate a climate change vulnerability map for all provinces in the country. This will be used as a basis in climate risk management and to train MoIE and MSS staff in the use and maintenance of these new tools and technologies. The project will also develop a set of guidelines, manuals, training materials, and other technical documents as well as asset inventory and management databases. Project activities will provide a series of trainings to the municipalities on hazard and risk assessment modeling and mapping techniques, climate change vulnerability assessment, and economic valuation. Under Output 1, the project will facilitate dissemination and sharing of common and definitive climate risk information needed by all sectors of central government to embed climate risk considerations in their planning processes. The Climate Change Coordination Committee (CCCB) under the previous GEF-funded SBIIR project, has a mandate to provide climate information services across all government institutions. This output will build on this mandate and strengthen it through training and capacity building for government practitioners. The proposed GCF project intends to build the capacity of the CCCB to embed necessary skills in its operations.

At the municipal level, targeted trainings will focus on engineering methodologies that can help climate-proof infrastructure development. Through these trainings, the project will also provide technical assistance to municipalities to create the conditions for new engineering practices for more resilient infrastructure development in the country. Through capacity building and training of local government staff at the national, provincial, district, municipal and sub-national level will be strengthened to embed such knowledge and skills for continuity of the learning process. To this end, the project will provide technical assistance to municipalities to create conditions for the application of findings and findings of SBIIR to institute the required capacities, provide tools, methods, engineering skills and other enabling conditions that will allow municipalities to apply these methodologies in their planning processes. The project will also provide training municipal staff in the application of these methods, the project is embedding climate resilient infrastructure management practices into the PDIM and PRIDS frameworks. The project has also embedded training and TOI materials on climate change adaptation for rural infrastructure development and CAMP - Community Action Management Plan processes. In addition, socio-economic data to be collected (Activity 1.1) and updated using local labour-intensive methods will be used in the design of interventions. The proposed GCF project will implement a Gender Engagement Plan will include community engagement and capacity development on topics to include climate risk management and adaptation. This will ensure that women’s voices are heard, their needs met and their participation improved. These interventions will improve ownership of these interventions while implementation of the Gender Action Plan will ensure that interventions are gender-sensitive and inclusive. The project will also provide training in gender analysis and the strengthening of cooperatives through the development and implementation of the agro-fisery strategy will embed gender considerations into all aspects of the project.

162 The project will implement a knowledge management (KM) framework (See Chapter 12 of the FR for detailed description of the KM framework). It will have the following key aims:
* To ensure that all relevant information is captured, shared and disseminated as well as long-term access to data on which stakeholders’ essential institutional functions rely and other data and information that can be used for evidence for policy making.
* Connect key stakeholder groups, practitioners and experts to ensure that key learning and experience is shared with the wider community of practice.
* Ensure staff in the stakeholder institutions know about effective and relevant KM techniques so that knowledge is shared, captured and retained by the institutions and shared within and across the sector (institutional KM improvement).
* By developing and promoting KM as a tool for continuous and sustainable improvement and ensuring that KM tools are embedded into institutional processes and maintained within the stakeholder institutions.

### E2.3. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The project is creating three main categories of enabling conditions for climate-resilient communities and their physical assets: First, innovative tools and methods will be introduced for hazard, risk and vulnerability assessment, modeling, and mapping. Second, new approaches in cost-benefit analysis methods and tools will be introduced to embed climate-risk-informed, appraisal-based strategies of investment into the project cycle. Third, a comprehensive loss and damage database will be developed and linked to municipality staff and field officer data collection systems. This will enable more accurate reporting on damages and losses from disasters and will address a current lack of data on climate impacts and disaster losses. Enhanced efforts to collect loss data, coupled with improved risk assessment tools, will allow for better decision-making in the future as needed. Asset Management Systems linked to the damage and loss database and utilizing climate-risk data will enable more informed decisions regarding infrastructure investments and maintenance strategies.

Data sharing systems across key sectoral institutions concerned with infrastructure development will enable a seamless flow of information between these institutions and facilitate more efficient and effective resource allocation. Furthermore, technologies will be utilized to create efficiency in this process. For example, use of drone technology for risk assessment and a long-term observation of change at broader geographical scales (of coastal and inland areas) will allow for the creation of high-resolution vulnerability maps that can be used by local communities to participate in local risk assessments by recording coordinates of vulnerable infrastructure as well as other relevant data. This will also enable the development of hazard and vulnerability maps that will underpin land use decisions, risk reduction and infrastructure development investments. This will also determine how climate-resilient infrastructure is prioritized within the project cycle and how it complements institutions in their functions of adaptation planning and implementation. Decentralized investment mechanisms such as PDRS and PMDS will be used to ensure that local communities are involved in decision-making processes. By embedding climate-risk criteria into PMDS and PDRS planning manuals and guiding documents, the project will create planning tools for disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation at the community level. This will benefit resident vulnerable populations and their essential economic assets (including water supply, irrigation and transport infrastructure) by ensuring that investments are made in a way that is both cost-effective and sustainable for scalable investments into local development and climate-risk reduction. Municipal investment plans based on cost-benefit analysis methods will be developed to ensure that investments are made in a way that is both financially viable and socially equitable. This will enable evidence-based advocacy for public and private sector financing of infrastructure projects, which will lead to more efficient use of resources. This creates benefits for catchment management and provides an enabling environment for entrepreneurship, thus lifting livelihood pressures off the land and addressing current financial access restrictions in rural communities.

### E.2.4. Contribution to regulatory framework and policies

164. The project will create enabling conditions by addressing the current gaps in the disaster and climate change policies and respective subsidiary legislation concerning their enforcement. It will specifically focus on the regulations and procedures that are required for the implementation of the proposed adaptation strategies and resilience building strategies. The project will develop the construction and building codes (in relation to water supply, flood defense and transport infrastructure) as well as the land use planning guidelines in line with the national disaster risk management strategy and Environmental and Social Impact assessment laws and regulations will be adjusted to integrate climate risk management requirements into these laws and regulations. These measures are designed to ensure that the project is socially acceptable. Strongly considering the customary law and traditions that influence land use decisions, the project will integrate adaptation policy measures into the land and forest laws. These measures will be devised to facilitate such climate change adaptation strategies as the development of water harvesting systems and the sustainable management agro-forestry schemes.

165. Existing institutions, policies and regulatory frameworks significantly influence the lives of the rural population. Supporting the strengthening of existing national, and especially local public institutions in serving the rural communities and reorienting the existing policies of institutions in favour of the poor is an increasingly expected result of development projects. The project will support the government to develop a policy framework that changes the current national policies affecting the rural communities and their exposure. In addition, the degree of decentralization, which allows devolution of power to the latest level of governance, will be strengthened.

167. The project is attempting to strengthen the PDMA and PNDS processes. As such, the project has the potential to have a long-term impact by effectively embedding climate risk considerations and resilience into the planning design and construction phases of infrastructure projects. This will ensure that the infrastructure projects are more resilient and community capacity to address these risks, implement climate resilient infrastructure, and strengthen livelihoods (Agriculture, Livestock, Forestry, Fisheries) in a manner that is consistent with national disaster risk management policy, and the project will leverage the evidence it gathered from the SSRF project to inform the wider policy framework.

168. The GCF contribution to policy level work includes long-term catchment management strategies for risk reduction and improvements in implementing CDM and DRM Frameworks. It also includes the introduction of knowledge tools, guiding documents, investment planning, and/or information into sectoral strategies.


### E.3. Sustainable Development Potential

169. Main economic benefits from the project investment are derived from avoided economic losses due to climate change-induced disasters. Under climate change, economic losses by mid-century from single extreme hazard events are estimated to be $203 Million, $37 Million, $10 Million and $12.5 Million USD for landslides, floods, erosion and drought respectively. The project will contribute to the reduction of these risks through the construction of resilient infrastructure that deploy infrastructures such as resilient water supply systems, flood defense structures, and transport infrastructure. Deploying these infrastructures will be able to reduce economic losses by 2050 by $183 Million USD. This will also provide livelihood opportunities for the communities that will benefit from functioning water supply and irrigation networks, stable roads and bridges, and improved access to markets. The project will also contribute to the creation of employment as 30 engineers will benefit from newly acquired skills and job opportunities to design and deploy climate resilient infrastructure.

#### 170. Social benefits of the project relate to increased safety and protection of the local population from climate related disasters. Thousands of people in Timor Leste remain isolated during disasters such as floods, flash floods or landslides. Unpredictable weather patterns have led to a decline in agricultural productivity and food security for many families. This has led to basic from basic transport services and unable to access local markets, health centres, or schools. Women are particularly vulnerable because they are responsible for fetching water and collecting firewood.

A damaged water supply network also forces women to walk long hours to reach the nearest water source to collect water for their households. The project will provide a safe and reliable water supply system that will allow these women from away any available support. GCF investment will therefore safeguard local communities and their assets from climate-related disasters, while providing them with access to clean drinking water.

The project will also include climate resilient projects as part of future PDM and PNDS processes. This will empower communities and help them adapt to climate change by ensuring that they have the necessary resources and infrastructure in place for a sustainable livelihoods that meets their needs and addresses some of their vulnerabilities. It is doing so in the following ways: Deliberate targeting of women, youth and other vulnerable groups; inclusion of gender considerations throughout the project and during feasibility study. It considers roles of women in customary social roles and family responsibilities and the ways they are affected by climate change. The project will also ensure that all new infrastructure projects and infrastructure units secured their input on current water supply issues which requires them to walk long distances daily to fetch water for household use.

In addition, women have been consulted on their farming practices and their input in the design of the relevant irrigation schemes. This has ensured that the project is gender-sensitive and inclusive. The project will also ensure that families were fully taken into account in developing the agro-forestry requirements which will address current crop yields issues and provide a sustainable livelihood for women, youth and other vulnerable groups. The project will also ensure that basket's requirement of the households and the saleability of surplus product, in each municipality. Furthermore, in undertaking the socio-economic cost benefit analysis of the project, we have included gender-specific benefits of each infrastructure project and ensured that these are reflected in the overall project design.

The project will be embedding in the long-term PDM and PNDS process for the identification and prioritisation of projects in the future so that they can be implemented with the necessary resources. The project will also ensure that it uses responsive community consultation approaches that were developed under the SRIK project into the PDM and PNDS processes to ensure that women, youth and other vulnerable groups are fully involved in decision-making for choices to be made by women (e.g. fetching water) and providing access to better hygiene and health through the supply of clean drinking water. The project will also ensure that all new infrastructure projects and units secured are not only being used for drinking water, but for kitchen' vegetable gardens as well. In addition, road stabilization projects will be designed in a way that they can be used by women and youth to access markets and engage in cooperation. With regard to food security, irrigation projects have the potential to provide increased annual productivity and yield of crops which are important for women's livelihoods.

In undertaking the project design and implementation, the project will embed gender-differentiated aspects of climate risks into community-based risk mapping processes, disaggregating data collection procedures with infer infrastructure prioritization, design and implementation.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## UNDERLYING VULNERABILITIES

The degree of geographic isolation/access to urban areas coupled with socio-economic conditions demonstrates that most rural settlements in the country are highly vulnerable and susceptible to climate change induced risks. An ADB study on vulnerability assessment of Timor-Leste found that 80% of the population lives below the poverty line.

The success with higher living standards is concentrated around the capital Dili and close to district centers. Successes with lower living standards are concentrated in the north, east, and south of the country. The areas with lower living standards have a greater proportion of people living on less than $2/day compared to those with higher living standards, and there is a large gap in access between groups. For example, of the 89 success with the lowest living standard, only 10% had access to piped water, while for the 66 successes with the highest living standard, 75% had access to piped water. The same applies to sanitation: 24% of those in the lowest success group have access to sanitation compared to a share of 66% in the 89 successes with the higher living standards. Access to improved water and improved sanitation is also more prevalent among those with higher living standards.

In addition to asset, social and infrastructure indices, capacity to cope is also an important index of vulnerability. When coping capacity is considered, the analysis showed that the impact of climate change is dependent on the size of the household.

#### Change (the increase in % of the municipality which will be affected by the highest category of the particular hazard) as well as the coping capacity for that community. This points to the need to enhance the coping capacity of communities and provides a means of identifying where the enhancement of components of coping capacity, namely infrastructure assets, can have the greatest impact on reducing vulnerability and enhancing resilience in the face of climate change. The analysis shows that the climate risks to infrastructure and livelihoods is increasing, and those who are most affected by these risks are women and girls.

#### Women play an important role in Timor-Leste’s rural communities. In particular, they are active in agriculture as cultural traditions dictate. However, women face a number of challenges when it comes to accessing resources necessary to control and respond over such land, credit and capital as well as access to agricultural inputs and technology, training and information. This is particularly the case for women living in remote rural communities and those living in urban communities. Women and girls in Timor-Leste are particularly vulnerable to food insecurity. Consequently, they suffer from malnutrition and poor health outcomes (Sawaya et al., 2013). The lack of access to land and credit by women is a key barrier to attaining food security through higher agricultural productivity is disproportionately affected by these low social empowerment, weak community influence and lack of control over and access to income, resource and information (Soeiro et al. 2014). Women are also more vulnerable than men in the face of climate change.

#### Eliminating gender gaps in rural communities is thus paramount to achieving productive rural communities in Timor-Leste.

### E.4.2 Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

#### 81. When the impact of climate change with coping capacity considerations is assessed for each receptor type (road infrastructure, water sources, houses) and all hazards into consideration, the following municipalities emerge as the most directly affected by climate change: Bobonaro, Ermera, Lautém, Manatuto, Viqueque and Vila do Conde. These are also the municipalities that have the lowest coping capacity to deal with these climate-induced hazards.

#### 82. Timor-Leste is a post-conflict low-income economy with the poverty rate measured at 40%. Over 70% of the population, and over 80% of the female workforce, is engaged in subsistence agriculture, where low crop yields persist. According to to the World Bank (2013), Timor-Leste has a high vulnerability index for climate change, with a score of 95 out of 100. This is largely due to high poverty rates, severe deficiencies in infrastructure and public services, but also due to poor institutional capacity and extremely limited access to education and training services are currently not up to standard to build a productive and skilled population. This makes the country’s vulnerability index understated and not staffed by skilled personnel.

#### 83 Since gaining independence in 1975 Timor-Leste has faced great challenges in rebuilding its infrastructure, government institutions and providing funds for young people entering the workforce. The development of offshore oil and gas resources has greatly supplemented government revenues. This technology-intensive industry, however, is not well suited to Timor-Leste’s economic conditions because there are no production facilities in Timor-Leste, though there are plans to develop domestic processing capacity.

#### 84. The government has focused significant resources on public infrastructure, including electricity and roads. But despite expenditure of over $1 billion per year since independence, the underlying approach policy challenge the countries remains how best to use oil-and-gas wealth to lift the non-oil economy onto a higher growth path and to reduce poverty.

E.5 Country Ownership

Beneficiary country (also) ownership of, and capacity to implement, a funded project or programme

E.5.1 Existence of a national climate strategy and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAMAs, NAPAs and NAPs

The project is fully in line with the adaptation priorities expressed in National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPA). The NAPA priorities promote climate disaster risk reduction to protect local populations and physical infrastructure. Protection of forest and watershed forest rehabilitation linked with issues of food security are also underlined as important.

The project is aligned with SDGs 5, 6, 10, 11, 13, 15. Specifically with regard the SDG 1 the project will:

- Strengthen the resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in all countries;
- Integrate climate change considerations into national policies, strategies and development processes;
- Improve education, awareness-raising and human and institutional capacity on climate change mitigation, adaptation, impact reduction and early warning;
- Promote mechanisms for raising capacity for effective climate change-related planning and management in least developed countries, small island developing States, including focusing on women, youth and local and marginalized communities.

The government’s Strategic Development Plan for 2011 onward offers a vision, targets and indicators for the next two decades. The plan is structured around three priorities: (i) Economic foundations: targeting sectors of economic growth such as transport, telecommunication, power, water supply and sanitation; (ii) Economic foundations: targeting sectors of economic growth such as transport, telecommunication, power, water supply and sanitation; (iii) Institutional framework: focusing on macroeconomic management and improving the capacity to generate public revenues beyond oil; and (iv) Institutional framework: focusing on macroeconomic management and improving the capacity to generate public revenues beyond oil. The draft National Climate Change Policy is structured around these priorities and offers a direct contribution towards the strategic directions of SDP. The proposed project also contributes to SDG 1 most directly through its contribution towards the access to public services.

The Draft National Climate Change Policy intends to provide policy guidance to mainstream climate change into the development process, with particular emphasis on poverty reduction and sustainable development.

### Expected Performance Against Investment Criteria

#### Climate Resilient Infrastructure

192. The policy aims to:
   - Promote climate resilience and climate proofing approaches and concepts in small, medium and large scale infrastructure.
   - Identify and map all appropriate and effective climate proofing measures that have been practiced in the country and internationally.

Commission a comprehensive cost-benefit analysis to assess the benefits and losses incurred by climate change and associated extreme weather events on integrated climate resilient infrastructure development projects. Integrate climate resilience into the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) regulations and other relevant provisions to make EIAs mandatory for all infrastructure development activities.
Develop guidelines for integrating climate resilience in the design, construction, and operation of infrastructure development projects.
Identify the most climate vulnerable communities as well as the underlying factors contributing to their vulnerability. Recognize that these communities are often located in areas with high exposure to natural hazards such as floodplains, irrigation canals, roads, or health facilities etc., so help enhance community resilience.

Develop guidelines for integrating climate resilience into the design, construction and operation of infrastructure development projects in the government as well as private sector to integrate climate resilient infrastructure.
Integrate climate resilience into the existing monitoring and evaluation templates used by government authorities such as Ministry of Public works, Transport and Communication.

#### Loss and Damage

Collaborate with existing vulnerability assessments, natural disaster risk assessments and complement the information on existing and potential loss and damage incurred by the climate change, disaggregated type of loss and damage, sector, region etc.
Present a comprehensive report on the information on the existing and potential loss and damage, disaggregated by sector, magnitude, gender, and geography.

Establish a Task Force led by the Multi-Stakeholder Committee for Climate Change specifically for Loss and Damage.

#### Disaster Risk Management

Make provisions of updating climate risks and vulnerability zones in collaboration with the NMDO to prepare risk sensitive land use planning.
Implement the National Land Use Plan to ensure that human settlements and infrastructure are not built in critical climate risk zones or disaster prone areas.
Collaborate with local authorities particularly to develop local level disaster risk reduction action plans that can be used to prioritize and implement disaster risk management interventions at the community level.
Integrate disaster risk management into existing national, regional and sub-national disaster risk reduction guidelines.
Coordinate with national and subnational disaster operating units and centres to help make informed decisions. Integrate climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction in all disaster preparedness programmes such that there is an integrated approach to managing disaster risks at the community level.
Create platforms for coordination and collaboration between climate change adaptation and disaster risk management interventions.
Make provisions of community shelters to evacuate people at risk during the event of climate induced disasters. Prioritize the use of participatory approaches, sustainable infrastructure development and community-based approaches to disaster risk management.

### The Draft Disaster Risk Management (DRM) Policy underpins the need for robust, systematic and reliable climate data to inform decisions on DRM and the linkages between the climate change adaptation measures and DRM measures. The alignment and complementarity with climate change actions and priorities are made visible through the objectives of the DRM policy.

- **The DRM Policy will create an integrated national capacity to identify, assess and monitor disaster risks (including hazards, exposure, vulnerability levels and economic effects together with prevailing capacities), including the effects of climate change.**
- The DRM Policy is designed as a framework for planning and programming into which disaster risk management is integrated along with climate change adaptation.

### To ensure that the disaster risk management aspects are well integrated into different levels of planning, budgeting and execution in social and economic development, the DRM policy proposes key areas of intervention including Risk assessment and identification; b) Preparedness; c) Planning and d) DRM communication. Proposed areas of intervention under Risk Assessment and Identification are:

- **Identification of disaster risks**
  - implementation of risk assessments
  - access to assessment data at all stakeholders in disaster risk management,
  - comprehensive use of this data for planning, programming and ongoing programme risk assessment

### The Revised Draft National Forest Policy emphasizes the importance of conservation, production, and management of forest resources. It also highlights the need to address other related sectors such as watershed management, agriculture, biodiversity, land use management, and cross-cutting aspects (such as capacity development and climate change), thus facilitating a more integrated approach in policy formulation. Areas that have been identified to facilitate implementation of the Policy are:

- community-based management of forests and watersheds
- participatory restoration and land restoration
- forest conservation and protection
- market access for forest products
- extension, training, research and information dissemination

### Direct reference to climate change is made under the Policy Instrument of the Forest Conservation and Protection and climate change adaptation under the community-based management of forests and watersheds. Forest resource based models that are being promoted in the policy such as agroforestry, silvopastoral systems and mixed farming draw speak to the need for control of land degradation, sustainable land use management, combatting desertification, reforestation and afforestation. The instruments on forest resources and forest management also have strong climate change adaptation and mitigation connections.

### MAF’s Strategic Development Plan 2014-2020 (MAF SDP) was developed based on the priorities for the agriculture, farming and forestry sectors in the country. The main objectives of the MAF SDP are: a) to focus on rural development; b) the main objectives are c) to focus on agricultural and rural development that supports small farmers and promotes improved markets in order to reduce poverty; d) to ensure food self-sufficiency, nutrition and sovereignty; and e) to promote economic diversification by reducing dependence on oil revenues. The MAF SDP also takes into account the costs of climate change.

### MAF commenced in 2015 a review of the National Forest Law with support from the Food and Agriculture Organisation (FAO) and is currently developing a long-term forestry investment plan to diversify from the largely oil-dependent economy towards more sustainable economic development.

### Furthermore, in June 2016 the GoT sent to the National Parliament the draft new Land Law which will inter alia, provide the required legal framework for agroforestry; giving more security and ownership to local users (i.e. land and trees); ensuring that communities’ rights are protected and how to create an enabling environment that would sustain agroforestry development.

#### E.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

1. UNDP is one of the largest targets of climate change grant support to developing countries focusing on the main sub-themes: (i) building resilience in livelihoods; (ii) enhancing disaster risk management in vulnerable communities; (iii) ecosystem-based adaptation; (iv) fostering resilience for food security; (v) climate resilient integrated water resources and coastal management; and (vi) promoting climate resilient infrastructure and energy. UNDP globally has an extensive network of country offices, regional hubs, and a global headquarters that support national programmes and projects as well as highly competent technical team at HQs and regional offices.

2. UNDP provides a vital coordination role for catalyzing enhanced capacity to adapt to climate change risks and impacts across the UN system in Timor-Leste through its presence in the country and its strong links with the entire UN system serving to consolidate all efforts across multiple UN agencies under the unifying framework of "delivering as one".

3. UNDP in Timor Leste has a long-standing relationship with the key ministries and departments and in close partnership delivers projects that are aligned with the National Development Plan (NDP) and the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP). The UNDP Country Office in Timor Leste is a record of implementing large scale projects, both soft and hard infrastructure. In 2017, UNDP in Timor-Leste was recognized by the United States Agency for International Development, has recently successfully completed the Small Scale Rural Infrastructure, or SSRI, project. This project was implemented with the support of the Government of Timor Leste, the World Bank, and USAID. The project is supported by Regional Technical Advisors at the UNDP offices in Bangkok, Thailand, as well as by policy adaptation, ecosystem-based adaptation, and climate change resilience experts from the United Nations Environment Programme, Ministry of Commerce, Industry and Environment using UNDP's National Implementation Modality, which is designed to ensure delivery of projects that are aligned with the NDP and NCCP. The project was implemented in accordance with ESS and compliant with stakeholder consultations. UNDP has a three-tiered quality assurance system: Services that UNDP provides to its clients; services that UNDP staff provide to each other, and procurement for the project. UNDP's services will be provided by staff at the UNDP Country Office (HQ), UNDP Asia Pacific Regional Hub (Bangkok) and UNDP Headquarters (New York). UNDP will act as the Quality Assurance partner for the project.

4. While the overall execution/implementation of project will rest upon the MCE as an implementing partner, concrete outputs and activities/sub-activities will be implemented by a combination of experts from a number of parties. These will include technical assistance provided to the MCE by UNDP (technical support in areas such as coordination between UNDP and responsible parties), as well as consultant teams and organizations which will be procured through open competitive tendering for proposals. More specifically, this project will engage the following respective parties in achieving project outputs:

#### Ministry of Commerce Industry and Environment (MICE)
This agency houses the National Directorate for Climate Change (NDCC) which is responsible for developing climate change policy and legislation. It is comprised of three national directorates - National Directorate for Climate Change (NDCC), National Directorate for Planning Control and Environmental Impact (NDCPE), and the National Directorate of Projects and Budgets (NDPB). The NDCC has a key role in the development of the National Development Plan (NDP) (planning application) and NDCPE undertakes biodiversity impact assessments for infrastructure projects. The NDPCE directorate is responsible for the implementation of the NDP, including the identification of priority areas for completion. The role of MICE includes the development of methodologies for addressing climate risk in all sectors, collaboration with other government agencies to ensure that climate change considerations are integrated into projects. The GEF, GCF and UNFCCC focal points and Designated National Authority for Timor Leste also sit within the MICE.

#### 2.3.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

In terms of cost-efficiency, the inclusion of interventions related to natural capital and physical infrastructure has been shown to lead to significantly higher improvements in resilience than if the project included only a single approach. Based on previous experience and lessons consolidated during the feasibility assessment, the project addresses the fundamental issues that are causing vulnerability by addressing both the underlying causes of poverty and the laws and ordinances. This will enable climate-proofed infrastructure development as well as investment in watershed restoration and rehabilitation to be more effective in terms of cost-efficiency and resilience of natural capital assets. Investing in natural capital is more effective in the long-term as it will help decrease the cost of climate-proofing of infrastructure by reducing levels of exposure to climate hazards.

#### E.8.3. Financial viability

##### 224.
This project could have focused on implementation of climate resilient infrastructure for the target municipalities as the only intervention. While this would benefit the target municipalities and might have had a trickle-down effect on other municipalities, it would not result in the fundamental change in how small scale rural infrastructure projects are identified, prioritized, financed, implemented and monitored. By addressing the root causes of vulnerability through integrated approaches, the project will lay the foundation for all infrastructure developed to be made climate resilient in the future. In addition, by addressing the root causes of vulnerability, the project will contribute to the development of a national strategy on climate proofing of infrastructure becomes a requirement within a suitable legislative and enabling environment in the future. The project will also contribute towards the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) through adoption of this holistic and integrated approach that addresses all the root causes including land use management, which have long-term effects and effectiveness.

##### 225.
Overall, the proposed solution is expected to be in line with best practices and community ownership, as well as present synergies across the two outputs and interrelated activities. In addition, they builds on ongoing efforts to ensure their efficiency and cost-effectiveness. The proposed activities and interventions will reach 45% of the total population (excluding both direct and indirect beneficiaries) over six municipalities.

##### E.8.4. Application of best practices

#### 226.
Without PDSG funding, the economic losses from a single extreme event or combined hazardous consequences (e.g. inundation, landslides, droughts, etc.) could be as high as $114 Million per year. The proposed project’s PDSG budget of $12.9 Million over 5 years, on its own, will therefore not mitigate against this potential size of economic losses and the associated social costs.

##### 227.
The public goods nature of this project's outputs doesn't entail significant revenue generation or cost recovery from the project. Where income generation opportunities exist, these apply directly to the beneficiaries (for instance, improved agricultural practices) rather than to the project itself. The financial viability analysis for each output is detailed in Annexures XIX(a) and (d). The project cost-benefit analysis is provided in Annexes XXIIa and XXIIb.

##### 228.
Financial viability of the project investments is assured through a combination of elements that build ownership and the capacity to manage infrastructure. These include: (i) the involvement of local communities, including women and local communities to maintain and derive economic, social, environmental benefits from the proposed investments; The project will also contribute towards the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs); (ii) community-level infrastructure investments are subject to local arrangements for maintenance and operations, except for rural roads which are maintained by the Ministry of Public Works, Transport and Communications (MPWTC) regular road maintenance operations of the Ministry of Public Works, Transport and Communications (MPWTC).

#### 229.
The proposed project is based on a set of best practices and lessons learned emerging from past projects and from experience derived by sectorial ministries and development partners in Timor Leste. Key lessons are derived from the UNDP-supported project "Strengthening Small Scale Rural Infrastructure in Timor Leste – SSRi project", which has piloted the approach to climate-resilient infrastructure in several municipalities, including Bobonaro, Ermera and Liquiçá. Other lessons are derived from the UNDP-supported project “Dili-Alcano Road Corridor (DARC) Project”.

#### 230.
Under the SSRi Project critical small scale rural infrastructure was designed to be climate-resilient and implemented through participatory approaches. In addition, the project strengthened local governance systems at the municipal and Administrative Post level, which will ensure that future investments in infrastructure are made with consideration for the design and implementation of PDSM projects in a manner that makes them withstand risks and impacts of climate change. ## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 65 OF 99

The project successfully completed the climate-resilient design and implementation of small-scale infrastructure projects in rural communities, following a participatory approach. The project included bio-engineering catchment management approaches, such as planting along drainage routes or creating vegetative barriers to reduce runoff and erosion. It also involved community engagement practices like engaging communities in planting (e.g., Owsika with NGOs, Talmiron). Additionally, the project supported the establishment of the Centre for Climate Change and Biodiversity (CCCB) within the University of Timor-Leste, which was achieved through the provision of technical assistance (expertise and training), computer equipment, GPS equipment and GIS training, CC software for climate mapping, mapping exercise, and facilitation of experts in climate mapping.

The following approaches have been tested to develop climate resilience infrastructure in an SSRM project, which will also be applied in this project:

1) Water Supply systems
   - Identification of sources at risk from reduced supply due to droughts.
   - Calculating requirements for upgrading water supplies and increasing dependency of supply.
   - Protection of water sources by revegetation and ground sources, formulating informal sources (pumping wells), and providing environmental protection and supply dependencies.

2) Roads and Bridges
   - Rehabilitation of bridges that are usually washed away in the rainy season using climate resilient materials and protection of bridge openings with bioengineering methods.
   - Vegetation of road corridors with bioengineering material such as Vetiver grass, re-sizing of road drainage systems to reduce runoff and erosion due to climate change.
   - Engagement of local communities in the vegetation of road embankments, as well as for water catchment re-forestation on project road works.

3) River defences
   - Building of flood defences to protect communities currently at risk using bioengineering methods to protect flood embankments from erosion.
   - Construction of river banks that take account of climate change.

4) Irrigation systems
   - Construction of new irrigation schemes which include water storage systems to store water for use in the dry season and against drought.

The GCF project will build upon the lessons learned from the SSRM project to further embed climate resilient infrastructure planning and management by:

By ensuring the project impact on rural communities includes enhancement of the value and derived benefits from existing land uses, such as agriculture, livestock and livelihoods. The project will increase the capacity of local communities to exploit potential economic opportunities and to develop stronger links with markets and external partners in order to improve their resilience.

Existing institutions, policies, and regulatory frameworks significantly influence the lives of rural poor. Supporting the capabilities of existing national, and especially local public institutions in servicing the rural communities and reorienting the existing policies or institutions in favour of the poor is an expected outcome of this project.

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 17 OF 99

### F.1 Economic and Financial Analysis

#### Summary of Economic Analysis

237. The economic analysis of the proposed project was carried out in accordance with the Guidelines for the Economic Analysis of Projects under the United Nations Development Program. The economic efficiency of the investment was determined by computing the economic net present value (NPV) with an assumed 10% discount rate, and the economic cost-benefit ratio (C/B). The NPV is defined as the difference between the present value of benefits and costs. UNDP/GEF have opted to use a 10% discount rate, in line with the existing practices of multilateral development banks.

238. Economic values (costs and benefits) are all measured in real terms in 2017 values. Economic costs of the project are estimated at USD 54.9 Million. The economic benefits are estimated to be USD 60.1 Million, giving a C/B ratio of 1.08 for unskilled and semi-skilled labour in Timor Leste. Provided that the economic cost of labour in Timor Leste is expected to increase by an average rate of 5% per year (with a standard deviation of 2%, and a confidence interval of approximately 60%), we expect this assumption leads to significantly overestimating the economic cost of the project, and underestimating the true net economic value of the project.

239. As a common practice in the analysis of the economic costs and benefits of investment projects, numerous assumptions were used to delineate the “with project scenario” from the “without project scenario”. These assumptions are presented and discussed below. Assumptions were made to underestimate the true net economic value of the proposed investment project.

#### Overall Project Economic Analysis

240. As part of the development of the project feasibility, a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) was undertaken for the 130 infrastructure schemes to be implemented. Implementation prioritization was based on this CBA. The CBA analysis has taken forward to determine the economic net present value (NPV) and cost-benefit indicators.

241. The total project capital costs are estimated at USD 59.6 Million. GCF’s contribution is USD 38.7 million, which represents 65% of the total project cost. The Government of Timor Leste will contribute USD 20.6 Million, which includes Operational and Maintenance costs of USD 7.1 Million during project implementation. Other co-financing from UNDP/GEF is estimated at USD 9.3 million (see Annex XIX for details). Co-financing from the World Bank is USD 45.8 million (see Annex XXII) and USD 20.6 million (see Annex XXIII) for more detail). Capital investment takes place over the five-year period of project implementation, with a total of USD 13.9 million in year one, USD 7.1 million in years two to four, and USD 5.8 million per annum from year six and year ten are presented in year 7 which will enhance the present value of costs when compared with the present value of benefits.

242. Following the identification of flood-prone areas and meteorological hazards (floods, landslides, erosion and drought) in all Municipalities and watersheds in Timor Leste, 6 Municipalities were selected and structural measures were identified in each to create resilience to four components of public infrastructure: Rural Roads, Water Supply and Sanitation, Health Services and Education. The project will be implemented with a view to increase the introduction of agroforestry options to minimize the effects of erosion on agricultural land. As described above, the project will be implemented in 144 proposed villages selected by UNDP/SSR (Small-Scale Rural Infrastructure) team in the 6 Municipalities.

243. Capital, operational (where appropriate) and maintenance costs are provided for projects and the Internal Rate of Return (IRR) is calculated to determine the performance of economic efficiency. The Sustainable Urban Communities (SUCo) assuming no resilience measures were put in place (The Do Nothing option). Clearly these resilience measures will be implemented as part of this project, so that the IRR is a conservative hypothesis. For the broad analysis of feasibility, it is assumed that residual losses after completion of the projects are not included.

244. Analysis of Climate Change Risks, Impacts and Vulnerability for baseline and projected Climate Change Scenarios: Dr John Chatterton Project Deliverable 1 December 2016


---
**Page 70**
---

244. The identification of high and very high risk for all 4 hydro-meteorological hazards did not include an assessment of their probability of occurrence, so under Do Nothing it is impossible to predict both the timing and frequency of a damage event. This makes it difficult to assess the cost-effectiveness of any proposed adaptation or mitigation measures; an assumption that a landslide event, for example, in a very high risk location will occur every 10 years on average will give a wholly different internal Rate of Return for the project than one is expected to occur every 2 years.

The objective of creating structural resilience to infrastructure is not just to ameliorate the effects of major hazards in high and very high risk areas but to resist the degradation of infrastructure over time as increasingly adverse weather events become more frequent. For example, a road in a low or medium risk area may degrade (under Do Nothing scenarios) over time; reducing access or inhibiting access altogether. This being so, it enables comparison of the relative priority of the cost benefit of all 4 hazards and their associated impacts on infrastructure. The IRRS are not absolute and not absolute giving a ranking to projects. They are indicative of the relative cost effectiveness of an appraisal-led design once GCF funding has been allocated. In this analysis costs are broad brush as are benefits.

For simplicity, the time horizon for each project is 10 years. It is quite possible that in reality no economic loss would occur during a 10-year period and that the project would be cost-effective even if it were not included in schemes. For others, economic loss may be more frequent. What is certain is that the propriety of damaging hydro-meteorological hazards will increase over time. The frequency of damage events from these hazards has increased (almost certainly more frequent than in reality) will enable priority ranked projects both nationally for all projects and by project type. This is based on a combination of hazard risk, vulnerability and opportunity areas which will require further detailed modelling and investigation once GCF funding is secured.

248. Though IRRS were prioritized for each of the 130 schemes, at this stage the benefits for all 4 project types (rural roads, water supply, irrigation and flood control) were added together. Benefits are summarized as follows:

- **Avoided loss of total livelihood by improving irrigation systems**, access to water supply, and road quality and connectivity. The benefit is derived from 2015 survey data and the assumption that no improvement in livelihoods would occur without infrastructure.
- **Reduced flooding to land and property.** Property values are derived from 2015 surveys; agricultural land at risk of flooding is estimated using a flood hazard map.
- **Social benefits:** Impact studies have found that following rehabilitation the roads not only had year-round access but also provided better economic opportunities for the communities living in the area, with services improved along rehabilitated roads, including 24-hour ambulance service and mobile health services. A conservative estimate of the social benefit is US$10 per person per year or US$350 million over a 10-year period. This is assumed to be US$1 per person/year for a total of 28,671 people in the road network area.

In environmental project appraisal, benefits may be regarded as monetary benefits expressed in USD, or non-monetary benefits expressed as beneficiaries of the projects (households protected, population benefiting, services area benefitted). In theory, all social and economic benefits should be monetized to express them consistently as monetary benefits. In theory, all social benefits can be monetized, but not without considerable survey and field investigation.

250. Subsistence farming may be blinded in areas of high erosion with commensurate loss of crop income and general livelihood. An analysis was done within the 6 Municipalities to indicate the potential sites for agroforestry to enhance soil conservation and improve livelihoods. The assumption is that all households are engaged in subsistence farming at this stage. The assumption is that every household has an average size (for subsistence farming) of 1,000 sqm and will use 50% of the land for crop production while the other half will be used as pasture land. This will yield a higher income while yielding some wider catchment management benefits. It is not envisaged that large enough economic gain

## Summary of Project Economic Viability

The base line analysis gives an Internal Rate of Return of over 16.2%.

Some explanation of the cash flows is required:

- Annual O&M is included for all projects
- Additional more comprehensive maintenance is included for rural roads in Year 6
- It was assumed that in alternate years there would be no disruption to these services and benefits of improved infrastructure.
- social benefits due to better access to markets, education, health, commercial and administrative facilities
- disaster risk reduction

The four infrastructure bundles were added together to provide a single IRR as each complement the other in safeguarding communities at risk from hydro-meteorological hazards. Cross subsidization of costs between projects is also included.

Applying sensitivity tests to the base case as follows in all cases gives an IRR as follows:

- Increasing Costs by 15% gives an IRR of 13.9%
- Decreasing Benefits by 15% gives an IRR of 13.1%
- Increasing Costs by 15% and Decreasing Benefits by 15% gives an IRR of 10.1%

## Summary of Financial Analysis

In accordance with the Guidelines for the Financial Analysis of Projects of United Nations Development Program the financial analysis has been carried out only for activities and sub-activities that can produce direct and quantifiable benefits in monetary terms, either to the beneficiaries or to the capital providers. As shown in Table 1 above, only the activity level (Activity Level) 2.2 is included as it relates directly to revenue generation by beneficiaries and hence only for these activities, the financial analysis has been conducted.

The financial analysis is conducted both at the sub-activity level and at the activity level. For instance - the financial analysis of the sub-activity level (Sub Activity Level) 2.2.1 is carried out independently from the financial analysis of the sub-activity level (Sub Activity Level) 2.2.2. The financial analysis shows the Financial Analysis for Sub Activity Level 2.2.2 (Rural Roads, Water Supply, Irrigation Systems, Flood Protection) and at the activity level (Activity Level) 2.2.

Financial Analysis is carried out as two distinct scenarios: Scenario 1 describes a case when the GCF does not participate and when the proposed GCF's capital investments are replaced with loans from Development finance institutions (DFIs). The second scenario describes a case in which the co-financing is in the form of grant investments and supports the co-funding provided by the Government of Timor Leste (GoTL).

## Analysis and Recommendations

- Under scenario 1, where the Grant participation of GCF is replaced with debt funding from commercial financial institutions, it could be noted that the project investment results in a NPV of -$9,584,770. This negative NPV is due to the fact that the financial return of 13.19%, as measured by the FIRR, is higher than the Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC) of 4.91%. When the commercial loans are replaced with the participation by the GCF in the form of Grant, the project investment results in a NPV of $40,834,588, which is much higher than the NPV in Scenario 1 and the FIRR on 13.14% is more than twice as high as that in Scenario 1.

- In view of this analysis, it is recommended that in spite of the positive FIRR profile of the project when funded by development assistance, the project should be supported with non-commercial capital such as commercial loans to ensure financial viability and operational sustainability. Our recommendation is based on the below-mentioned rationale:

257. NO incremental earnings – The benefits from the project accrue to the project owners (GOTL) not in the form of increased income or saving but in the form of expected / prospective savings. The absence of "incremental earning" benefits to the project owners necessitates that the project be supported with non-commercial capital as much as possible for ensuring viability and financial viability over the longer-term.

258. Benefits are indirect – The benefits from the project accrue to the project owners (GOTL) in the form of potential savings. These savings accruing to the project owners over the long-term and in the form of losses avoided / spending avoided such as transportation losses avoided due to Climate resilient Rural roads, avoided spending on a large scale for maintenance of rural roads, etc., are indirect benefits. The project is expected along with the project owners enjoy these benefits. This way of realizing benefits necessitates that the project be supported with non-commercial capital as much as possible for ensuring viability and financial viability over the longer-term.

259. Pre-mature economic profile; social and environmental background and challenges – Timor Leste is Asia’s youngest nation and is one of the world's poorest countries. It has been heavily sevently pained with several violence incidents, including civil war, uprisings, communal violence and inter-communal violence. As much as 70 per cent of the country's infrastructure was destroyed. Hundreds of thousands of people were left homeless after the conflict ended in August 2006. The UN has estimated that 15% of the population lives below the poverty line. In Indonesia territory of West Timor, in 2006, tensions between the national police and the armed forces resulted in open conflict between the two institutions, a breakdown of law and order and the displacement of more than 150,000 people.

260. The country has been under peaceful democracy since 2008 and is focused on infrastructure re-building. Owing to the pre-mature economic profile of the country and viewing the project from the perspective of social and environmental background and challenges, it is recommended that the project be supported with non-commercial viability and long-term operational sustainability for this project.
