261 Regulatory constraints on availing commercial loans by GoTL - The Public Debt Law setout by the GoTL allows only concessionary loans to be considered for the purpose of financing strategic infrastructure projects.

262 Practical difficulties in tapping development / commercial finance though GoTL - Though commercial loans have been presented as an alternative to GCF grants, the ground reality could be starkly different. The concessionary loans that are available under the GoTL are subject to a number of conditions and restrictions which may impose several riders. These partners could be focused on financing large-scale infrastructure projects of national scale such as Road networks, power generation, etc., and not necessarily small-scale projects or projects for climate resilience. Moreover, these projects may contain terms mandating the presence of certain implementation partners. For these practical challenges in availing development / commercial finance, it is recommended that this project be considered under GCF funding.

263 Lack of borrowing program - The financial analysis had explored the possibility of GoTL replacing the GCF grants with more exotic form of the public market borrowing. However, Asia's youngest nation has not established a borrowing program yet and does not have a currency of its own.

## Financial Analysis Conclusion

265. The financial analysis was conducted in accordance with the Guidelines for the Financial Analysis of Projects of United Nations Development Program. The financial analysis has been carried out only for activities and sub-activities that are directly related to the project. The financial analysis does not include any costs incurred by the capital providers. Activity 2.2 qualifies as such activities with defined and quantifiable costs and benefits. In monetary terms, therefore, the financial analysis is a cost-benefit analysis (CBA). The CBA has been conducted on a comprehensive indicative basis of financial analysis on the project itself. Two scenarios were considered - Under scenario A, it was assumed that the project would be funded by the United Nations Development Programme and other institutions; it could be noticed that the project resulted in a substantially lower NPV of $9,584,770 in comparison to Scenario B where the project is funded by development partners. The difference between these two scenarios can be seen as nature of project benefits, economic profile and situation of Timor Leste, regulatory and fiscal constraints which affect the financial viability of the project.

266. The project supports the integration of climate change considerations into the development of climate resilient small-scale infrastructure that will, in turn, provide essential services to local communities. It is embedding such climate change considerations within the current infrastructure planning framework and strengthening the climate resilience of the country's infrastructure. The project also aims at building capacity for the identification of field proven methods and practices and implements at a scale that is important for transformation of small-scale rural infrastructure development in the target municipalities, while providing a strong basis for national scaling up and replication.

267 Risk Knowledge. A key foundational activity of the project will be strengthening of the climate risk knowledge base and the role of Tims Leste for the major hazards. This will be done through the introduction of vulnerability assessment, modeling and mapping techniques and tools not currently available in Timor Leste. Climate hazard risk is a complex issue that requires an integrated approach to understanding and managing it. The project will provide the level of detail required for making risk informed decisions, nor for designing climate resilient infrastructure. The project will also support the National Meteorological and Hydrological Service (NMHS) of Agriculture and Fisheries (MAFI), establish 10 FADIS weather stations in Timor Leste. Information from these stations is collected and used for climate variable reporting. In addition, Seeds Life (SIL) maintains a network of 25 automatic weather stations across the country. The project will collaborate with the National Directorate for Meteorology and Geophysics (NDMG) and maintain a hydrodynamic database. The installation of FADIS stations is expected to be completed by the end of 2018. The project will also support other entities that are supporting the government to enhance the observation network. Given that the main objective of the GCF is to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, increase resilience and adapt to climate change, the upgrade of the hydrodynamic network falls outside of project's scope. However, the project is putting into place the necessary infrastructure for the collection of data which will be used in the development of risk assessments. The project is thus supporting the generation and use of the hydrometric data by developing the risk data and information systems. This will also support the NMHS to develop climate resilient small-scale water resources and maintenance of climate resilient small-scale rural infrastructure. Furthermore, the project will introduce modern and proven methods for the assessment of climate risks in Timor Leste. These include the use of remote sensing, hydrometeorological and socio-economic data available for Timor Leste. Modern modeling software will be introduced to support a review of data availability for Timor Leste confirmed that the methods are appropriate for the data available.

268 Hazard, risk and vulnerability mapping. Hazard, risk and vulnerability maps are essential for the assessment of current and future climate risks. The project will develop hazard, risk and vulnerability maps based on current considerations and the identification of receptors such as infrastructure at risk. The strategic assessment of risk to population, livelihoods, economy activity and to local ecosystems is a key element in the response to change. A government priority to understand and guide development and use land and water resources sustainably. At provincial levels, GCF resources will be used to introduce modeling technologies to develop hazard maps under current and Climate Change scenarios. The project will also support the NMHS to develop risk assessments for all aspects of risk-informed decisions for all aspects of development and risk management in the future. This will include zoning of development areas based on climate change vulnerability, as well as the identification of critical infrastructure at risk to economic activity. In addition, the hazard maps will be used as the basis of the multi-hazard early warning system to be developed by the project. The hazard maps will also provide a basis for the development and implementation of emergency preparedness and response plans. Furthermore, these maps could be used in the establishment of different types of insurance schemes that would help reduce economic losses from climate change events and enhance prepreparedness. The visual maps will benefit decision makers and all involved in natural hazard risk management at the national level as well as at local levels. These maps will also provide a basis for the development and dealing with hazards in the basin in the future. Important the hazard maps will be the basis for the management of climate change risks.

269 Disaster Risk Management and asset management. The implementation and maintenance of modeling systems and the full adoption of the new technologies to be introduced will require technical capacities in all areas of hazard and risk management. These capacities are currently not available at national level, although some capacities exist in governmental and non-governmental institutions in areas that contribute to risk reduction and disaster management. In addition, there is a need for capacity building on climate change adaptation strategies and plans in various sectors. This however is mostly the case at central level; rather than decentralized levels (regional, municipal and local). While technical expertise exists in various sectors and for specific technical areas, knowledge of climate change adaptation strategies and plans is lacking across all sectors. Technical capacities related to hazard identification, risk assessment and identification, prevention, risk reduction, risk mitigation, disaster preparedness and response are also lacking at the national level as well as weak across institutions and governance levels. In certain sector, there are insufficient human resources; in many cases, knowledge of climate change adaptation strategies is not available or is limited to a few individuals. As part of the SSRi project, an assessment was made of the existing gaps in institutional capacity for all aspects of hazard

and risk management in Timor Leste. A capacity development plan will be specified based on the needs identified.

In addition to hazard maps for individual hazards, a multi-hazard map (MHP) will be developed for all major river basins in Timor Leste. This is an excellent tool to create awareness in mitigating multiple inter-related hazards and for assessing vulnerability of communities and infrastructure. The MHP will also provide a basis for prioritization in infrastructure design and construction, particularly in a country like Timor Leste where small-scale infrastructure in a given area can be susceptible to multiple hazards, all of which can be triggered by the same set of long-term/seasonal drivers as well as short-term events.

## 270 Damage and loss recording

The project is also introducing modern approaches to assessment of economic damages (through socio-economic risk modeling of the underlying hazard impacts) and damage and loss assessments. This will provide a more accurate picture of the extent of damage and losses, which can further enhance the identification of disaster impacts and speed up mobilization of resources in order to reduce causality.

The use of drones for disaster response is also being introduced as part of the project. The drone will be used immediately after a disaster. There are concerns at National Disaster Operations Center (NDOC) regarding the reliability of drones, which can be affected by weather conditions and other factors such as "flying for many months"). Reasons given for such delay are remoteness, distance, and inadequate communication systems through which the drone is controlled. The project will introduce a system to ensure that the drone is reliable in communication services and poor sharing and quality of data. Double entry and adequate auditing to filter out malicious data will be introduced as part of this system.

The project will also introduce a system for disaster risk management, which will be based on a robust quality assurance system. Based on this, the support of drones in disaster management was fully recommended by the NDOC. The drone will be used for monitoring and mapping of infrastructure damage; enhance risk and damage assessments and revolutionize disaster preparation, response, and management capacities. It is also expected to provide early warning systems for potential disasters, including flood activities and strengthen the preparation for and response to disasters including the assessment of physical damage and loss, as well as the identification of priority areas for rehabilitation and reconstruction of livelihoods. Data generated from the drone flights will also be useful in the long-term monitoring of hazards, which will feed into the development of early warning systems and disaster risk management plans, design of climate resilient infrastructure, and environmental monitoring. Drones equipped with photogrammetry and navigation equipment will be used to allow rapid and reliable assessments of important hazard parameters.

## 271 Climate proofing infrastructure

The 130 infrastructure projects were subject to a cost-benefit analysis. Capital, operational (where appropriate), and maintenance costs were provided for each project; and the Internal Rate of Return (IRR) was calculated using the Discounted Cash Flow Analysis (DCF). The IRR is defined as the rate of return (Sucess) assuming no resilience measures were put in place (The Do-Nothing option). The time horizon for each project is 20 years. The cost-benefit analysis was conducted by comparing the Internal Rate of Return (IRR) for each project including total costs (capital and periodic annual maintenance) with the benefits as defined above. The IRR represents a measure of profitability, which can be used to compare projects and represent- ing elimination of potential losses associated with improved climate resilient infrastructure. On this basis, the project will provide an indication of the cost-benefit for each sub-parallelipiped and for each individual project type to indicate where project investments are worth value for money.

## 272 Evaluation of the cost of Climate-Prooing Infrastructure

In certain countries of the region with complex terrain, it is difficult to assess the costs associated with climate proofing infrastructure. However, the additional costs would result in 7 to 9 years due to lower maintenance costs over the long-term. This means that the project will be more cost-effective and provide better value for money.

The project has identified three types of projects where climate proofing is required: (i) water supply systems; (ii) 3 climate resilient rural road rehabilitation projects were implemented in 2016 at an average cost of around $65,658 USD per km. The total investment cost for these projects was approximately $4 million. The cost-benefit ratio between 3 – 5 % of the context cost. Assuming a value of 10% of the cost, it can be estimated that the additional cost of climate-proofing roads is $1,474.152 to $6,890 USD per km. For water supply systems, the total investment cost per project was approximately $3 million. The cost-benefit ratio between 3 – 5 % of the context cost. Considering only the total investment cost, this yields a simple average of $5.0 per person for 127,775 USD. Since most of these projects are gravity-fed, operational expenses are minimal, as are maintenance costs.

273. Ecosystem based catchment management: In addition to the climate proofing measures that will be implemented for all types of infrastructure in the target priority locations, the project is also implementing an ecosystem based approach to address the vulnerability of these areas and to improve their resilience against climate change impacts within these catchments. This includes the use of agroforestry. Agroforestry is a combination of agricultural and forestry practices that are integrated on the same land management unit. While agroforests are typically less diverse than native forest, they do contain a significant number of plant and animal species. They provide many ecological functions similar to plantation forests. Agroforestry systems can also provide valuable ecological functions. Similar to plantation forests, agroforests can help relieve some of the pressure to harvest natural resources in these areas. In addition, agroforestry systems are often more diverse than monoculture plantations (trees, shrubs, palms, bamboo, etc.) are deliberately used on the same land-management units as agricultural crops and livestock. Agroforestry systems provide a wide range of ecological functions that can be used for any important role in conservation of natural resources, especially soil. Adoption of agroforestry practices enhances the productivity of the land by increasing its capacity to retain water, nutrients and organic matter. Among the productive functions, fuel wood, fodder and fruit are the most important besides construction wood, game animals, honey, etc. Agroforestry systems provide a wide range of ecological functions that can be used for include shade; reduction in wind speed; control of erosion and maintenance and improvement of soil fertility. Agroforestry systems also provide many ecological functions similar to plantation forests. They provide shade for other horizons. They reduce nutrient leaching losses through tree root and mycorrhizal systems. Agroforestry systems recycle nutrients through decomposition, litter, pruning and root residues. In agroforestry systems, there are both ecological and economic interactions between the components of the system.

274. Agroforestry and reforestation will be implemented in the catchments upstream of the infrastructure to reduce soil erosion and landslide risks downstream, thus adding an additional layer of protection and lengthening the service life of the infrastructure. The project will also implement a monitoring framework for agroforestry systems and specific implementation procedures for the sub-catchments within which the infrastructure projects will be located. Agroforestry systems are being implemented in areas that have been identified as priority areas for agroforestry and reforestation intervention, specifically targeting areas that will compromise the infrastructure due to climate change related hazards.

### F.3. Environmental, Social Assessment, including Gender Considerations

275. This project has completed the UNDP social and environmental screening procedure (see SESEP attached as Annex VIa). 276. The project is expected to have some short-term, but medium- or long-term environmental impacts particularly in relation to the rehabilitation of existing infrastructure but will have significant long-lasting environmental benefits (see Box 1). These include improved water quality, roads and bridges, and flood control structures and will introduce agro-forestry into degraded areas to allow for resilience.

277. The project will also improve the capacity of government and communities to plan, design, and deliver rural infrastructure projects. This will be achieved through training manuals, checklists, and standards will be reviewed and updated to support improved planning and implementation.

During implementation, UNDP will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with the agreed Memorandum of Agreement (MOA), which includes an Assurance Management Arrangement (AMA) and project confirmation to be agreed with the GCF. This may include, but is not limited to, monitoring missions, quarterly financial reports, annual performance reviews, semi-annual implementation progress reports, annual implementation reviews, and audits at project level or at implementing partner level on the resources received from UNDP.


288. The Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT) framework consists of four processes, namely: i) macro assessments; ii) micro assessments; iii) cash transfers and disbursements; and iv) assurance activities. Assurance activities will be carried out by UNDP in accordance with the AMA and include quarterly financial reports and special audits. During micro-assessment, there can be weaknesses identified for which actions are required to address these weaknesses. The macro assessment is a review of the project's overall performance. Some of the assurance activities will have to remain higher and modalities of engaging with that implementing partner will have to be agreed.


289. The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and together with any specific requirements agreed in the AMA. UNDP will also review the financial statements of the implementing partner against budget plans, on the basis of the implementing partner's risk rating and UNDP's guidelines. A scheduled audit is due to determine whether the project is being implemented in accordance with the approved programme activities as set out in accordance with the work plan. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit or an internal control audit.


290. All GCF resources will be provided to the executing entity, less any agreed cost recovery amount. Under UNDP's normal procedures, the implementing partner will provide a quarterly financial report on programme activities and the implementation of agreed and approved programme activities, in accordance with UNDP standard policies and the NIM Guidelines. The implementing partner will also provide a financial report on quarterly basis to UNDP. Any additional requirements will be as in accordance with the AMA.

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

## SAFEGUARDS RELATED

291. Environmental and social risks relate primarily to the potential impacts of construction activities. These include: the potential disturbance or damage of habitats; sediment and erosion; catchment-wide impacts of agro-forestry; and conflicts with local communities.

292. Agro-forestry activities can have potential negative impacts if poorly planned and managed e.g., if incorrect species are used, over-harvesting occurs, if soil erosion is not managed, if natural areas are cleared for agro-forestry or other uses, etc. These risks also exist both in terms of social capital (methods) and eco-system services/management.

293. Women in Timor Leste are particularly vulnerable and there is a risk that this is not adequately addressed. The project will provide training to women on the use of agro-forestry techniques, as well as on income generation and education. Providing opportunities for livelihood enhancement to women is a valuable aspect of the project.

# G.2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

Please describe financial, technical and operational, social and environmental and other risks that might prevent the project/programme objectives from being achieved. Also describe the proposed risk mitigation measures.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Political instability or regional conflicts | Other | High (>20% of project value/High (>40% of project value) | Low |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project will develop and implement an emergency management contingency plan in line with UNDP CD's crisis management requirements. This may reduce the level of impact of the risk to medium to low level.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Project implemented infrastructure is destroyed by catastrophic hazardous event | Social and environmental/social and environmental | High (>20% of project value/High (>40% of project value) | Low |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project will develop and implement an emergency management contingency plan in line with UNDP CD's crisis management requirements. During the design and construction of relevant infrastructure, disaster risks will be taken into consideration to ensure that the infrastructure is resilient against such events. These activities will reduce the level of impact from hazardous events and lower the probability that the infrastructure will be destroyed by such events.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 79 OF 99

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Reduced government priority for climate change adaptation and DRR due to political, financial and technical constraints, lack of institutional capacity, and limited awareness of the need to fully embed infrastructure climate proofing and DRR resilient measures into policies and enabling framework. | Technical and operational | Medium (1-20% project value/Medium (5-120% of project value) | Low |

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

* This project will have constant consultations with high-level government representatives and will carry out lobbying and advocacy campaigns in support of CC adaptation and DRR. This will reduce the impact of the risk to the minimum level.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Absorption and operational capacities of project responsible agencies are insufficient to adequately implement climate-proofing of infrastructure and management of disaster risks beyond the project. | Technical and operational | Medium (1-20% project value/Medium (5-120% of project value) | High |

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

* The project will pay close attention to the capacity building of all relevant agencies through carrying out a training of trainers, conducting on-the-job end line training for staff in these agencies, strengthening/reinforcing internships within responsible parties and particularly municipalities, and developing technical guidelines and methodologies for sustainable design, construction and maintenance of climate-resilient infrastructure. Capacity building throughout the project will reduce the probability and impact of this risk to the minimum level.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Due to poor financial performance of the government, and particularly ministries and agencies engaged in the project, there is a high probability that budget and staff cuts occur in these state organizations. | Financial | Medium (1-20% project value/Medium (5-120% of project value) | Medium |

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

* The project will assist government authorities to develop and implement sustainable long-term financial planning for the design and implementation of climate resilient infrastructure, including identification of potential private sector contributors and accessing international donor financing.

---

Selected Risk Factor 6

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Local communities are not interested in being engaged in community-based biodiversity and reforestation activities for enhanced catchment management | Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value or <1% of project value) | Low |

Mitigation Measure(s)

- The project will conduct extensive awareness campaigns at the grassroots level on climate-induced natural hazards, vulnerabilities, and risks. In addition to the rehabilitation of infrastructure by implementing agroforestry, reforestation and catchment management will be highlighted.
- Awareness raising will be based on tried and tested community engagement established by the existing SSRi project.
- The project will also make significant efforts to mobilize communities for the implementation and adoption of community-based agroforestry. This will reduce the impact and probability of the risk to the minimum level.

Selected Risk Factor 7

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Agro-forestry implementation on land previously used primarily for agriculture. | Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value or <1% of project value) | Low |

Mitigation Measure(s)

- Stakeholder consultation will be undertaken prior to the final selection of agroforestry sites within the infrastructure catchments to minimize conflicts with pasture land use.
- The project will also ensure that communities are implementing agroforestry from protecting infrastructure and agricultural land to the environmental and ecological benefits of overall catchment management. This is expected to be higher than opportunity costs related to current agricultural yields from the already degraded land.

# H.1: Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund Level

## H.1.1: Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund level

### Increased climate-resilient sustainable development

The project objective is to safeguard vulnerable communities and their physical assets from climate change impacts, including extreme weather events, sea-level rise, and increased frequency of droughts. The project will increase the climate resilience of vulnerable small-scale rural infrastructure, strengthening the capacity of these communities to adapt to climate change by improving access to water resources and basic infrastructure services. It will embed new skills, technologies, and innovative methods in climate risk identification and mitigation processes, enhance monitoring and recording of climate risk information at the community level, and improve the availability of climate risk information for small-scale rural enterprises.

### Fund-level impacts

| Expected Result | Indicator | Means of Verification (MoV) | Baseline | Mid-term Target | Final Target | Assumptions |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A1.0 Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of people, communities, and regions | AT 1 % reduction in losses of lives and economic losses (USD) due to climate-related disasters in the GF intervention | MDA GIS databases | Economic loss equivalent to 2% of GDP | Mid-term: 25% reduction in economic losses in 6 target municipalities | Final: 75% reduction in economic losses in 10 target municipalities | Climate proofing, asset management and disaster risk management (DRM) interventions result in a target reduction in economic losses |

## Notes
* Information on the Fund's expected results and indicators can be found in its Performance Measurement Frameworks available at the following link: <https://www.greenclimatefund.org/documents/0016237278431_1_Performance_Measurement_Frameworks_PMF.pdf>

# H2. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

## 294. Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP POPP and the UNDP Evaluation Policy.

### Oversight and monitoring responsibilities:

**295. The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation rests with the National Project Manager.** The Project Manager will develop annual work plans to ensure the efficient implementation of the project. The Project Manager is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are implemented in accordance with approved implementation, including the implementation of the Monitoring & Evaluation (M&E) plan, so that the appropriate support and resources are provided by the UNDP Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser to maintain a high level of transparency, responsibility and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results.

**296. The UNDP Country Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through annual supervision missions, quarterly visits, and other technical assistance.** This support will be provided in accordance with the terms outlined in the UNDP POPP. Additional M&E implementation quality assurance, and troubleshooting support will be provided by the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser to ensure that all project groups and stakeholders including the NDA Focal Point will be involved as much as possible in project-level planning.

**297. A project inception workshop will be held after the UNDP project document is signed by all relevant parties: a) re-define project objectives, b) review the project results framework and discuss progress towards achieving project implementation; c) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the project team, including reporting and communication lines and conflict resolution mechanisms; d) review the results framework, re-assess baselines as needed, and discuss how to collect data for monitoring and evaluation purposes.** The Project Coordinator will ensure that all participants are aware of their roles and responsibilities in relation to the project’s M&E plan. All participants will be required to sign a confidentiality agreement. Participants will also be required to sign an attendance sheet. The workshop will include discussion on reporting requirements and mandatory reporting, and agree on the arrangements for the annual audit: a) plan and schedule the audit; b) discuss the audit process and methodology; c) review the audit report template. **The results of CTA will prepare the inception report no later than one month after the inception workshop.** The final inception report will be shared with the UNDP Country Office, the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser, and will be approved by the Project Board.

**298. An Annual Progress Report (APR) for the proposed first year of project implementation: The Project Coordinator, and UNDP Country Office, all project RPs and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser will review the APR. #### Project Impact Monitoring and Evaluation:

**302. To examine the impacts of the project on rural communities**, the review will examine whether the interventions implemented by the project have enhanced the value and derived benefits from existing community assets such as land, water resources, forests, etc., and how these assets are being used to improve livelihoods. The areas to be examined while improved skill or health, education, and socio-economic conditions will be key indirect benefits to be considered in this review. The project will also examine the extent of its contribution towards enabling rural communities to develop stronger link with the markets and external partners, through the risk reduction and adaptation interventions implemented by the project. The role of local private sector actors such as agricultural input and engineering companies in the implementation of similar projects in the future will be a key aspect in this will reflect whether the project has contributed to creating an enabling environment for the private sector in the long-term. Likely contribution of the project to food security will be examined. Since the project is introducing agro-farming as a means of safeguarding livelihoods, it will be necessary to directly assess how the external objective is achieved.

**303. Environmental impacts and adaptation measures**: The review will examine the extent to which climate change and other risk factors like disaster related disasters. The extent to which the project contributes to the rehabilitation of the environment (particularly of the agricultural resource base and watershed management) in areas currently affected by land degradation and at high risk of desertification, erosion, landslides, floods, etc., will be examined. The review will also examine environmental impacts of the project, as well as any environmental consequences of the project. It is also concerned especially with the extent to which the project contributes towards reducing vulnerability of rural communities. Environmental impacts may be negative as well as positive, intended or unintended, and all of these will be considered in this review. The review will examine whether the project has implemented appropriate measures to protect infrastructure from climate-related hazards such as erosion and landslides. To directly measure the extent to which the project contributes towards reducing vulnerability of rural communities, it is observed. Counting the number of trees that survive and measuring the lives of hedgerows established is possible; however, this will be difficult in areas where there are no trees or hedgerows. The review will use aerial imagery and use of UAVs. The comparison of physical ground measurements due to the agroforestry intervention will be done using a combination of field visits and remote sensing data. The review will examine whether the project has reduced erosion, sediment-ripples will be laid-out in strategic locations. The Municipal Suco Extension Workers (MEEW) and the agricultural extension workers will be trained on how to use the tool. The MEEW will also be trained on how to undertake a training course on basic farm record keeping and the MEEW will ensure that AFs are using this tool. The team will then visit the field and collect data. The consolidated information gathered from farm records will then be useful during the project-year end economic evaluation.

**304. Existing institutions, policies and regulatory frameworks significantly influence the lives and resilience of the rural poor in terms of their access to resources and services, as well as their ability to adapt to climate change and other risks communities to hydro-meteorological hazards. In addition, the degree to which the project impacts local-level decision making capacity is also a relevant consideration and important to this project. The review will examine the extent to which the project has strengthened the institutional framework for rural development in general. Institutions to implement climate resilient infrastructure that affects the lives and livelihoods of rural communities.**

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 86 OF 99

### 305. To monitor and measure the changes brought by the project, impact evaluation will be designed to assist the project team to collect baseline information/data; final survey can gain insights into developmental and adaptive impact of the interventions that will be carried out during the project. For this purpose, before any interventions take place, a household survey targeting beneficiary households at least two times (baseline and final) during the project period will be conducted. The survey will consist of:

- **Analysis of the survey data:**
  - Follow-up survey which will be used by project staff;
  - Training on the use of follow-up survey methodology.

### 306. The methodologies for monitoring and reporting for the project outcomes are outlined in the means of verification in Table 11.2 through which progress which on each indicator from the baseline to the mid-point and end-point will be measured.

#### More specifically, the results of Output 1 will be monitored through the administration of qualitative scorecards to measure awareness and adaptive capacity. A series of target surveys will reveal the level of knowledge and awareness among project beneficiaries about climate change and its impacts on their livelihoods. The surveys will also identify whether the PDIM, NPSD and other relevant investment frameworks and policies have embedded the multi-hazard approach in their design and implementation. The surveys will be used to assess the impact of climate proofing production and use of quality risk information in relation to the design and deployment of small-scale rural infrastructure.

#### For Output 2 detailed HH surveys will be undertaken to monitor physical stability of the infrastructure units and the quality of their services, as delivered by the project through the implementation of climate proofing measures. The surveys will capture changes in household livelihoods and well-being, especially for women and vulnerable groups with improved access to water on their wetting and livelihoods. Additionally, HH questionnaires will be designed to elicit information among other aspects, on access, ease of use, maintenance, durability, and the overall satisfaction level of beneficiaries towards the infrastructure units and issues around GBM of the infrastructure units. The tailored surveys will be conducted in relation to all categories of infrastructure units and their respective components. The surveys will also capture the impact of climate change capacity of target municipalities. The questionnaires will also capture the benefits of agro-forestry on household livelihoods, including changes in income from agriculture and related activities (changes in income should take into account the effect of climate change hazards); (ii) changes in food security; (iii) changes for production yield and quality; (iv) changes for seasonal migration; (v) land fall left behind; (vi) freshwater availability for household use; (vii) change in family savings.

#### The impact indicators will include but should not be limited to (i) extent to which the project interventions such as improved access to water, agro-forestry and other climate proofing measures have reduced exposure to hazards (ii) changes in income from agriculture and related activities (changes in income should take into account the effect of climate change hazards); (iii) changes in food security; (iv) changes for production yield and quality; (v) changes for seasonal migration; (vi) land fall left behind; (vii) freshwater availability for household use; (viii) change in family savings.

#### As part of the community survey a section will be included to monitor community involvement in the design and implementation of community-based agro-forestry schemes – tracking participation in paid work opportunities, as well as ongoing involvement in resilience building through in-kind commitment of time to maintenance and enforcement of the agro-forestry scheme. The survey will also capture information on the number of respondents’ estimations of approximate number of hours per month spent on local resilience building activities. Finally, monitoring of the implementation and results of site-specific structural protection measures at 130 sites will be ensured as outlined in the ESFM.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP109

## Proposal name: Safeguarding rural communities and their physical and economic assets from climate induced disasters in Timor-Leste

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

#### Country/ies: Timor-Leste
#### Project/programme size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of Caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project is in a Small Island Developing State and Least Developed Country and has a strong climate rationale and theory of change. Most of the budget is aimed at direct on-the-ground interventions that will enhance the resilience of small-scale infrastructure and other assets. | Good technical oversight through partnership with key agencies will be required for successful implementation |
| The comprehensive approach links climate information to planning and investment decision processes. It utilizes innovative eco-disaster risk reduction and management solutions that integrate multi-hazard early warning system approaches and are based on robust scientific evidence. | Management of the multi-stakeholder process will require proper attention |
| The proposal includes significant investment in capacity development in the target areas for a least developed country and small island developing state. It features a high level of co-financing from different government ministries (over 1.5 times the GEF grant). | Water supply interventions should be accompanied by improved sanitation and hygiene services to ensure that they are parallel by a number of partners coordinated by the Government of Timor-Leste |
| The proposal includes extensive community involvement that will help create ownership, efficiency, and sustainability. | Currently there is a local technical capacity deficit in some core areas of the proposed interventions |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XIII titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".


---
**Page 107**
---

## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### Project background and climate rationale

1. Timor-Leste is a small island developing State that is highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate variability and change and is currently experiencing increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather and related disasters.
2. It has fragile socioeconomic and ecological systems, which combined with poverty and limited access to basic services, including small-scale rural infrastructure like water supply and drainage systems, emboldens vulnerability.

3. The proposal has undertaken a comprehensive assessment (feasibility study) in the areas of intervention and demonstrated robust results that are aligned with the GCF investment criteria and priorities.
4. The main multi-hazards of floods, droughts and drought cause an average yearly economic loss of USD 283 million, USD 37 million, USD 10 million and USD 12.5 million respectively. As climate change gathers pace, mid-century projections for these are expected to increase by a factor of two or more. Floods account for about 58 per cent, and droughts for about 11.5 per cent of the gross domestic product. Rural communities are disproportionately affected as they have limited access to basic services, including disruptions in their daily lives, isolation, and lack of basic services.
5. The situation is further complicated by the fact that rural communities face multiple hazards with high vulnerability, which have become major barriers to adequately integrating adaptation and risk management considerations into development planning and investment decisions, particularly those relating to infrastructure, social protection, land planning and housing.

6. The project aims to improve the resilience of rural communities by addressing the underlying causes of vulnerability of social and physical rural infrastructure. These vulnerabilities are caused by climate change impacts on water resources, soil erosion, climate variability and extreme weather events, as well as inadequate response capabilities and financial constraints in the rural areas.
7. This project will directly benefit 175,840 people from rural communities who will gain access to improved livelihoods through the provision of resilient infrastructure and climate-resilient infrastructure such as water supply and irrigation systems, roads, bridges and flood defenses. A total of 23,690 hectares of land are planned for rehabilitation and will be designed and implemented with financing from GCF and the development partners of Timor-Leste with the active participation of the rural communities.
8. Communities will benefit from the improved land use management practices such as soil conservation, agroforestry, reforestation and catchment management measures contributing to the protection of a wide catchment area and the improvement of water resources.

9. In addition to direct on-the-ground interventions, infrastructure will be made more resilient by mainstreaming/embedding climate resilience standards into the broader development planning process. The project will ensure that all infrastructure is designed, constructed and maintained.
10. The technical capacity to be established by the project for generating and analyzing climate risk and early warning information (activity 1.1) is expected to contribute significantly in supporting the integration of climate change considerations and risk reduction measures in the planning and budgeting cycles at the village and municipal levels (activity 1.2), and the creation of a knowledge database for managing climate-induced disasters (activity 1.2).


---
**Page 108**
---

## Project financing

The proposal provides a good financial proposal for an adaptation project from a least developed country and small island developing State with significant socioeconomic development challenges. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has requested a USD 21.9 million contribution from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). This includes co-financing contributions from the Government of Timor-Leste (to be obtained through budgets of a number of ministries) and a USD 0.4 million contribution from UNDP to cover project management costs.

The project will finance:

* **USD 12.8 million** for climate-proof small-scale infrastructure.
* **USD 3.6 million** for operation and maintenance (O&M) costs for the 130 resilient infrastructure projects.
* **USD 5.4 million** for co-financing of O&M costs through the UNDP Small Grants Programme (SGP).
* **USD 2.7 million** for climate-proof small-scale infrastructure.

The project will also cover:

* **USD 1.8 million** for operation and maintenance (O&M) costs.
* **USD 0.4 million** as UNDP co-financing for project management costs.

GCF funding will cover activities 1.1, 1.3 and 2.1, which are all associated with capacity-building for climate information and early warning services, risk management, adaptation planning and implementation, and infrastructure maintenance. The remaining activities (activities 2.2 and 2.3, which together account for over 80 per cent of the project cost) will be jointly covered by GCF funding and co-financing from the Government of Timor-Leste.

Infrastructure maintenance costs during the lifetime of the project, which will be covered by co-financing from the Government of Timor-Leste, is subject to an accredited entity (AE) in the process of securing a commitment letter that extends O&M coverage by the Government to 20 years.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Climate risk information generation and integration into planning and management (total cost: USD 7.72 million; GCF: USD 3.45 million, or 46 per cent)

Activities under output 1 seek to address gaps in the capacities of sectoral institutions to generate climate risk information and integrate it into their decision-making processes. This is a fundamental step in integrating adaptation into wider decision-making processes throughout the administration.

Activity 1.1 will develop and deliver climate risk information services and vulnerability mapping for all sectors, including water resources management, agriculture, health, and other economic data - as well as technical staff training in climate risk modelling, mapping and vulnerability assessments, cost-benefit analyses, and project appraisal with a focus on infrastructure development.

Activity 1.2, largely financed by the Government of Timor-Leste, will establish a database for climate information and early warning services. This addresses a key knowledge gap (in combination with activity 1.1) that will facilitate the understanding of climate risks in the country and their integration into sectoral planning and decision-making.

Activity 1.3 will reform ordinances, regulations and building code standards, which will enable the climate-proofing of infrastructure projects. Climate-resilient construction practices are mainstreamed as part of new resilience planning for new infrastructure development in the country. Specific interventions are described in good detail in the funding proposal.


---
**Page 109**
---

# Component 2: Climate risk reduction and climate-proofing measures for small-scale rural infrastructure (total cost USD 56.18 million). GCF cost USD 18.41 million, or 32 per cent)

This output focuses on building resilience at the municipal and village levels in six priority districts, with a combined population of 522,000:

- Building the capacity of local governments to incorporate adaptation measures into the regular planning and budgeting processes of the village and municipal development plans.
- Directly undertaking on-the-ground infrastructure resilience and ecosystem-based adaptation projects at the village and municipal level.

Activity 2.1 seeks to mainstream adaptation considerations all day long within the village and municipal level, where critical planning related to land use and small-scale infrastructure is undertaken. This activity will also provide technical assistance and information on climate hazards and risks generated in activity 1.1 to identify projects and enhance the ability to incorporate adaptation measures into project design and implementation processes, tools such as socioeconomic cost-benefit analyses, optimising, appraisal methods, and environmental impact assessments under climate change impact scenarios.

Activity 2.2, which has USD 33.8 million constitutes 59 per cent of overall investment cost including co-financing from the GCF. This activity is divided into four categories:

- Protection of rural water supply systems through land revegetation, formulation of informal sources of water, installation of sandpits, and other climate-resilient approaches.
- Rehabilitation of rural bridges and slope stabilisation of rural roads cored with bio-engineering methods (e.g. vetiver grass) for the protection of bridge opening and slope stability against erosion and landslides.
- Installation of rural flood defences to withstand climate change impacts, with biomechanical engineering methods where possible to prevent erosion; and
- Formation of water harvesting systems and irrigation systems to mitigate against intensified droughts that are projected to occur with climate change.

The four types of activities have a clear climate rationale and a direct positive impact on the rural poor. The GCF Secretariat has commissioned an economic analysis provided by UNDP, which indicates that while some irrigation activities have a very high return on investment (ROI), the overall ROI for water harvesting systems have a very small ERR (1 per cent). It should be noted that the economic analysis of such small-scale infrastructure on an asset-by-asset basis is complicated and that synergies and the key role of these interventions in the broader context of climate change adaptation are not fully captured by this analysis. The GCF Secretariat considers that the overall ERR is more relevant in informing the merits of intervention.

Activity 2.3 captures catchment management and rehabilitation measures following an ecosystem-based adaptation approach, with communities actively participating in agroforestry. Restricted to the six priority districts, this activity will be implemented by local authorities, comprising a total of 1,500 ha (of which 20 per cent will have interventions financed by GCF) will be undertaken over five years. The activities include the development and implementation of an agroforestry strategy development, the purchase of agricultural inputs, rehabilitation works, and O&M during the lifetime of the project. This activity, in addition to its potential to reduce vulnerability, also has relevant potential adaptation co-benefits in terms of generating economic


---
**Page 110**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The project targets the most vulnerable districts of the country that have been identified based on poverty indices, infrastructure deficits, gaps in basic public services and social vulnerability indicators. The proposed interventions are expected to significantly improve the resilience of physical assets to climate variability and change through direct on-the-ground interventions. These include 120 water storage units (47 road dams) with a combined catchment area of 236,849 hectares; 38 irrigation systems with a combined catchment of 216,458 hectares; 38 irrigation systems with a combined catchment of 216,458 hectares; and 20 flood defenses along 14.15 kilometers of river, for a total investment cost of $79 million. The project is expected to benefit approximately 10% of the land will benefit from agroforestry and restoration activities (3,100 ha of reforested land), 1,000 ha of improved livestock grazing areas, and 25,000 ha of agricultural land leading to improved livelihoods for 23,412 households. A further 1,200 ha will be improved through reforestation and agroforestry with co-financing from MAI.

In addition to the direct benefits, the project is expected to contribute towards developing new, robust systems (including water storage) to secure water for prolonged consecutive dry days/seasons. The project will help reduce impacts of the projected intensified droughts. The proposed improved sanitation and hygiene interventions are expected to significantly improve the resilience of local agricultural livelihoods and well-being. More predictable rice production is expected with an increase in rice production tonnage per year, as the impacts of the dry season/droughts will be mitigated by irrigation.

The collective impact of the interventions on the population will be significant in terms of livelihood improvement (e.g., increased income from improved agricultural activities and livestock activities in output 21% of the total population). The broader community in the six districts targeted for intervention is expected to benefit through improved access to health services, and informed decision-making. Broader benefits include overall avoided costs, job creation, and revenues from the effective use of climate information and early warning to manage risks to lives, livelihoods, and assets.

In order to realize the benefits from water supply interventions, they will be implemented with improved sanitation and hygiene systems in place. There are a number of partners involved in the project implementation. The Government of Timor-Leste has presented a plan by which the Governor of Timor-Leste, through his relevant Directorate General, will coordinate the activities of the different partners, ensuring that they go hand-in-hand with the water supply investments from the GCF project.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift Scale: High

The proposal clearly articulates and responds to the broader capacity gaps that exist in Timor-Leste to enable it to manage the climate risks to its low-emission, climate-resilient development. These gaps include:

(a) Limited knowledge and information on climate risks, damages and vulnerability;


---
**Page 111**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale High

Overall, the implementation of a proposed business case for weaving community lives, livelihoods and assets addresses some of the core elements of the Paris Agreement, and is well-aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals and Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (2015-2030). Specific annotations are provided below.

### Social benefits

Revegetating land around rural water supply systems will strengthen community access to a more climate-resilient water supply system. The proposed intervention will provide 10.3 million cubic meters of water per year for the communities in the project area that currently have no potable water or experience erratic access and are impacted by a number of drought-related climatic extremes. This would create benefits for local communities in terms of reduced water-related diseases and improved overall well-being and productivity of communities.

Road rehabilitation would result in an improvement in year-round motor vehicle access of road and bridge infrastructures, reducing estimated travel times by half and creating


---
**Page 112**
---

opportunities such as 24-hour emergency services (fire, ambulance, security) that are currently lacking in the region.

### Economic benefits

* UNDP estimates that the economic losses from a single multi-hazard event (e.g., involving floods, landslides and/or erosion) could be up to USD 141 million in the six target municipalities, which would not be fully covered by existing budgetary allocations of USD 1.25 billion over five years.

* Rehabilitation of rural bridges and slope stabilization of rural road corridors using ecosystem-based approaches will increase their availability year-round and reduce overall operational costs. This will lead to more sustainable land use management, environment-friendly forestry and last longer than most traditional approaches. More importantly, access to critical rural infrastructure will improve safety of life, productivity and sustainable livelihoods.

### Environmental benefits

* The proposed ecosystem-based rural flood defense approach has been proven to be resilient to the estimated increasing frequency and intensity of climate-induced floods and paired Tornadoes in the region. This project provides a replicable model that creates incentives for local communities to rehabilitate degraded watersheds and pursue livelihood practices that ensure environmental sustainability.

### Needs of the recipient

* Timor-Leste is a small island developing State with a high level of vulnerability to climate change-related landslides, floods, erosion and droughts, as shown by the current and expected economic losses under climate change scenarios described in paragraph 5. Furthermore, the country has a population density that is among the highest in Southeast Asia, with over 10% of its total population living in poverty. It has a fragile socioeconomic and political situation, and food security is an issue for many communities. The country also faces challenges related to inadequate public services, as well as climate change impacts. Financial resources for adaptation are scarce, and as such, GCF intervention is critical to make the project viable and sustainable.

* The proposal has undertaken extensive consultations at the national, municipal and community levels, and the specific needs of the multiple recipients were considered in the design. These include the need for improved access to basic services, livelihoods development and broader development challenges at all levels. The interventions are also designed to be scalable and replicable.

* However, the narrative could be further strengthened by exploring what benefits could be derived from considerations of demographic dividends in designing the activities so as to maximize impacts of the interventions in the communities.


---
**Page 113**
---

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The proposal articulates the strategic vision of the country’s climate change strategy, its national development plan and its broader disaster risk reduction theme which is clearly aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (targets 1, 2, 3, 6, 12 and 13) as well as the Sendai Framework and hence demonstrates the effectiveness of the interventions in addressing a comprehensive range of climate change related challenges and contributing to sustainable development challenges.

The proposal builds on the experience of a number of earlier projects in the country that have built capacity at the community and institutional level, most relevant of which is the “Strengthening the Resilience of Small-Scale Rural Infrastructure and Local Government Systems” project. This project has demonstrated the importance of involving communities through community-based O&M financing. Using and expanding upon this experience is critical for the implementation of a complex project in often remote settings that rely on the participation of communities to ensure sustainability, particularly at the level where the project will be implemented.

An extensive stakeholder consultation process, with specific attention to gender needs/priorities, was an integral part of the project design, informing aspects ranging from the presentation of local evidence interventions to the design of water irrigation and agroforestry schemes.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 22 953 million to finance a project with a total cost of USD 40.04 million. The proposal features a significant contribution from national and local government institutions and ministries, amounting to USD 16.487 million (of which USD 1.09 billion is provided by the Ministry of Water Resources and Irrigation). This contribution comes in the form of project management costs, for total co-financing of USD 1.64 for each USD 35.453 million invested on the ground. The project will be implemented through three activities: 1.1, 1.3 and 2.2. The costs for on-the-ground activities 2.2 and 2.3 will be split, with GCF financing covering 70% of each activity’s total cost. The total co-financing amount was reduced from the original USD 35.453 million request by splitting the cost of on-the-ground interventions between two separate activities.

Most of the investments funded through the project will provide public goods such as climate information services and flood protection, which clearly justify grant funding. For public infrastructure, such as rural roads, water supply and irrigation, grant funding is justified because it allows for a more efficient use of resources by avoiding losses and expenditures due to climate-related disruptions. Although the UNDP financial analysis indicates that the project could be financed through concessional loans, given the nature of the interventions and their high risk profile, it is not clear whether these loans would materialize without robust oversight, given the nature of the interventions and their high risk profile. Therefore, it may be appropriate to consider concessional loans for the purpose of financing strategic infrastructure projects.

The proposed financial structure is considered adequate, and inclusion of a well-articulated budget and strategy – with long-term commitments from government and community stakeholders – will ensure that the project remains on track politically and helps guarantee the sustainability of the project in the long term.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

1. The project aims to improve the resilience of vulnerable communities and their physical assets against the impacts of climate change.
2. It will support capacity-building of the mandated government agencies in implementing a range of small-scale rural infrastructure in six priority municipalities, including water supply systems, road construction, and other livelihood activities such as reforestation and agroforestry.
3. The project is categorized by GCF as having a low environmental risk category (EAC) due to its generally localized nature, limited scale, and readily manageable through the application of standard mitigation measures.

#### Environmental impacts of agroindustry and revegetation/landscaping restoration

1. Without these activities, those actions could inadvertently result in the introduction and spread of invasive plant species and/or new pests.
2. Also, primary forests could end up being converted to secondary forest or other types of vegetation that may not be suitable for native species or species that are already present in the area and have been proven to be non-invasive.

# Land tenure-related conflicts

The potential for conflicts, particularly those associated with land tenure, is a concern because of the prevailing land tenure system issue and the post- conflict context in which the project will be implemented. These issues would be addressed through the subproject screening. Any potential land issues would be identified and formal documentation of land-use agreements (i.e., owner's consent, deed or donation, etc.) would be obtained before any activities are undertaken.

## Indigenous peoples

The Indigenous Peoples' Standard of the Hii is applied to this project because the population at the project site may consist of members of one or several of Timor-Leste’s indigenous ethnic groups. The Hii requires that all projects involving land-based, and prior consent and broad support are assured through consultation and voluntary participation of indigenous peoples. This will be achieved by ensuring that all indigenous residents of the subproject, including with people who are not members of the beneficiary communities, have been consulted on the project’s potential impacts and provided with a substantive voice in decision-making processes. The Hii also requires that the National Commission for Indigenous Peoples and Ethnic Minorities Planning Framework describing the processes that will be undertaken to protect the rights of indigenous peoples pursuant to the Indigenous People's Standard of the Hii is followed.

The Hii requires that all projects involving land-based, and prior consent and broad support are assured through consultation and voluntary participation of indigenous peoples. This will be achieved by ensuring that all indigenous residents of the subproject, including with people who are not members of the beneficiary communities, have been consulted on the project’s potential impacts and provided with a substantive voice in decision-making processes.

## Impacts on protected areas of natural habitat

The ESMP has ruled out any subprojects in protected areas. To ensure that none of the subprojects will fall within any protected area, the AII has agreed to screen its subprojects so that it includes activities inside protected areas.

### Potential involuntary resettlement impacts

Involuntary resettlement is defined as the construction or rehabilitation of small irrigation canals and water supply systems. In addition, economic dislocation is not a remote possibility in the event of the implementation of any of these projects, particularly given the management strategies, which may result in loss of income, food gardens or pasture.

Consultations and community consent will minimize these impacts but would be inadequate to adequately address them. The AII has agreed that all subprojects must avoid the creation of new involuntary resettlement impacts (i.e., involuntary loss of land, homes, structures and/or trees, and institutionalization of existing involuntary resettlement impacts). All activities in agriculture, including participation in agroforestry, shall be strictly voluntary and require the owner’s consent, a waiver or deed of donation.

### The AII

The fact that high-risk subprojects are not funded, the AII has agreed to adopt a simple screening checklist form that shall, among other things, be used to determine eligibility/non-eligibility as well as the level and scope of the assessment study to be undertaken. The following subproject screening criteria will be applied:

### Gender policy

The proposal contains a gender analysis and therefore complies with the operational guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. It also contains a gender action plan.

The assessment indicates that Timor-Leste has a favourable environment to promote gender equality through its constitution and the various strategies and strategic development


---
**Page 117**
---

### Gender Assessment

The gender assessment provides detailed gender-related information based on literature reviews and on-the-ground consultations with communities. It recognized that women face multiple barriers to accessing resources and services which prevent them from engaging actively in development work and reduces their participation in the labour force and formal employment. Further, there are gender disparities in access to and control over resources, including water, land, and other natural resources. Women also have limited access to property ownership, due to patriarchal norms and customary systems. These poor challenges for women are compounded by the fact that women’s work is often unpaid or underpaid.

### Gender Action Plan

Women have to spend on average two to three hours a day on fetching water either directly from the source or from the nearest communal tap. In addition, the project has identified several other gender-related barriers which prevent women and girls from engaging in and benefitting equally from the project. Through consultations with women, the project has developed interventions that address the practical needs of women. Securing water for households is a key challenge for women as they are responsible for collecting water, considering considerable opportunities for improving women’s daily lives, health, and livelihoods.

Furthermore, women face challenges in accessing land and other resources such as roads and damaged bridges. As women mainly stay home and do household chores and home gardening, they suffer from the lack of access to these resources which further limits their economic activities. This creates a high level of risk for the lives and health of women and children. Therefore, in addition to addressing gender-related barriers through the project’s core interventions, the project also addresses the issue of mobility by installing climate-proofed and stabilized roads and bridges. The need to implement this intervention as a priority has been verified with the support of the Ministry of Transport and other relevant government groups.

The gender analysis has identified various interventions that would allow for increased participation in the project and the mainstreaming of gender issues in the various policy documents and processes.

# Risks

## Overall program assessment (medium risk)

### GCF requested to provide a grant of USD 22.9 million to safeguard rural communities and their livelihoods in Timor-Leste from climate change impacts by strengthening local institutions and improving small scale rural infrastructure in the target areas.

The GCF funds will be complemented by co-financing of USD 71.1 million from the Government of Timor-Leste and the ADB. The project is expected to contribute to the achievement of Small-Scale Rural Infrastructure and Local Government Systems to Climate Risk & Dili Area Resilience Project (SRI-DR) outputs, especially for the identification and cost estimation of 130 infrastructure interventions.

The success of the project depends on the political will to consider climate risks in cross-sectoral policies and legislation. In addition, capacity of relevant government ministries to provide necessary support during implementation of the project, including the EE (output 2.2), the main output with 63 per cent of the total project budget and 74 per cent of the total GCF financing), will influence the output.

## Capability of accredited entity/executing entity to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The AE (UNDP) has been operating in Timor-Leste since 1998. In addition, it has been collaboratively implementing the projects with the executing entity (EE) in the areas of environmental management and climate change.

The MGE is the EE for this project. It has a track record in implementing the Global Environment Facility's Least Developed Countries Fund project and other donor-funded projects. However, most of the projects recently implemented by the EE are micro-scale (less than USD 10 million) and do not have the same level of experience as the majority of the EE and provide necessary support during the implementation of the project.

## Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Erratic internet connection and power supply: the funding proposal and feasibility studies indicate that the EE has a stable internet connection and available power supply. This may interrupt the operation of the database management system, which requires internet connectivity to function properly. The EE is aware that the existing database already has internet connectivity and backup power systems.

In terms of infrastructure, while most of the water supply and irrigation systems are gravity-fed systems, there or no electricity connections in the project area. The feasibility studies have confirmed that GCF resources not be used to finance fossil fuel-powered pumping systems. According to the information provided by the EE, the EE has a backup power system for the pumps required for the design of the pumping systems, and training for maintaining the solar electrification systems is included as part of the project.

### Absorption and operational capacities of responsible entities: the EE will coordinate closely with relevant ministries and experts to implement subactivities and specific outputs. This will include MSA, MSS and MAF. The result of the capacity assessment shows a risk rating

### 4.4 Fiduciary

The EE for the project is the General Directorate for Environment at MGE. The MGE is also the project sponsor, whose mandate is the responsibility for environment and climate change policy in Bulgaria. Therefore, MGE will be responsible for the implementation and success of the project and will establish necessary planning and management mechanisms to oversee the project.

As the AE, UNDP will require the EE to implement the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations and policies and procedures, including its National Implementation Manual (NIM) and other relevant guidelines on procurement, environmental and social safeguards, and other performance standards which will be legally binding upon the signing of the UNDP Standard Basic Assistance Agreement by the Government of Bulgaria and UNDP for the implementation period of up to 12 months.

MGE will be accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achievement of project outcomes and effective use of UNDP resources. MGE will also be responsible for providing all necessary support for direct project services for this project. These services cover both technical and administrative support as well as other related services. The EE will ensure that these services follow UNDP policies on the diversion of direct project costs relating to GCF-funded projects.

UNDP will disburse funding (received from GCF) in accordance with the funded activity agreement disbursement schedule to the MGE, as the EE, for the purposes of implementing the project. UNDP will also provide technical and administrative support services to the named Responsible Parties, which are responsible for delivering particular project activities in accordance with your institutional mandates (to be detailed during the formation of the project document).

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

As an adaptation project, the proposal targets the project is expected to impact an anticipated 173,840 direct beneficiaries (89,643 men, 86,197 women; 19,751 households), which in turn will have a positive impact on 2.5 million people. These include increased climate resilience for small-scale infrastructure as well as 300 ha of reforested and rehabilitated land to buffer against climate-induced disasters.

The timetable of implementation in section C has been revised as per earlier comments and conforms with the standard GCF format.

## Legal assessment

The accreditation master agreement with the AE was signed on 5 August 2016 and became effective on 23 November 2016.

The Fund has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and thus has capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Timor-Leste, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileged and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected from civil or criminal jurisdiction of the country's courts unless it is further assessed. The Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the government of Timor-Leste for its consideration.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with privileged and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursement of GCF are suspended until GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

## List of proposed conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.
