# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP109

## Proposal name: Safeguarding rural communities and their physical and economic assets from climate induced-disasters in Timor-Leste

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Adaptation impact

##### 1.1.1 Adaptation impact

**Timor-Leste is a least developed country with a heavy reliance on agriculture for subsistence and income generation (approximately 75 per cent of its population is employed in the agricultural sector). The country is highly vulnerable to climate change, which include higher rainfall variability (predicted to decrease in the dry season and increase in the wet season) and more frequent extreme weather events. These changes will result in an increase of soil erosion, flood occurrence, landslides, crop failure, water shortages and damage of public and private infrastructure in a business-as-usual scenario.**

The proposed interventions aim at reducing climate change vulnerability by enhancing the climate resilience of small-scale rural infrastructure, including water supply systems, irrigation systems, roads, bridges and ferries and irrigation systems in six more vulnerable municipalities of the country.

1. The project consists of two main components: Component 1 includes the development of climate information services (CIS) for climate change adaptation; a monitoring system for monitoring, recording and accounting for climate-induced damages; and the integration of climate change considerations into district level planning processes to implement small-scale rural infrastructure planning and management. Component 2 focuses on the implementation of interventions in six vulnerable municipalities of Timor-Leste, which would include re-vegetation of land around rural water supply systems; formalization and enhancement of existing new water supply and rural irrigation systems; rehabilitation of rural bridges and ferries; construction of small-scale rural flood defences; and implementation of agroforestry and reforestation as ecosystem-based adaptation measures. The proposed interventions under activity 2.1 would be integrated into the ongoing District Investment Development Plan (DIDP) at the municipal level and will be guided by cost-benefit assessments, vulnerability mapping, and other tools to be introduced by the project.

2. The design and implementation of rural infrastructure under activity 2.2 would be guided by cost-benefit assessments, vulnerability mapping, and other tools to be introduced by the project.

3. The proposed interventions on rural infrastructure (activity 2.2) would directly benefit approximately 119,000 people (almost 15 per cent of the population of Timor-Leste).

**Funding proposal, paragraph 28.**


---
**Page 123**
---

Agroforestry and reforestation activities (activity 2.3) would cover approximately 300 hectares and benefit 58,000 people. When questioned by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) on the possibility of double-counting beneficiaries of individual infrastructure works and ecosystem services provided by agroforestry and reforestation activities, such as water supply schemes, flood defenses and/or agroforestry activities at the same time), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) responded that it would be possible to estimate for each activity, based on community consultations, to avoid double-counting.* The total number of people who will benefit from the proposed interventions in the targeted district, which is 522,000 and represents 44 per cent of the total national population.

The proposed interventions will reduce the climate risk vulnerability of targeted populations by increasing water availability for domestic use, livestock watering and irrigation purposes during drought periods, better and more diverse crop yields, increased water quality*, reduced damages to private and public infrastructure due to flooding and extreme weather events (activities 1.2 and 3).

Regarding the strengthening of institutional and regulatory systems, the project will develop a set of guidelines and specifications for rural infrastructure development and climate-induced disaster risk management (activities 1.1 and 1.3). The proposed interventions are part of the Government of Timor-Leste’s efforts to invest in developing a risk management application for the storage, analysis and management of disaster data, allowing users to estimate the economic damages caused by climatic events and evaluate the effectiveness of climate change adaptation measures.

Although the project recognizes that the availability of hydro-meteorological data is limited, and that it is not possible to identify with certainty the areas most exposed to climate hazards*, the project does not include interventions directed towards supporting hydro-meteorological data generation. When questioned by ITAP on this matter, the proponent responded that the issue was being addressed through a separate project, which is also being addressed by other organizations and entities that are supporting the Government of Timor-Leste.

The preliminary design of water supply and irrigation systems included the identification of water sources and the determination of their capacity to supply water to the communities. The project will also identify areas where it would be possible to establish practices of lands that could potentially be irrigated, types of agricultural activities and crops that farmers were engaged in or could engage in.

All project interventions are coupled with capacity-building activities. This would help to create awareness of climate threats and risk reduction processes among public institutions and the communities involved.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium

#### 1.2.1 Potential for knowledge and learning

The project is not the first effort to support the implementation of community-led agroforestry activities in Timor-Leste. The feasibility study in section 7.1 describes previous government efforts.

The monitoring and evaluation plan would comply with the UNDP Programmes and Operations Policies and Procedures, the UNDP Evaluation Policy and the GCF additional requirements for monitoring and evaluating project impacts, as described in the funding proposal, are deemed adequate.

The damage database system planned under activity 1.2 and the consequent capacity to evaluate the impact of climate change on agroforestry activities will also facilitate the sharing of climate risk knowledge so that such knowledge can be used in other projects. Other than this, there is no indication that specific activities aimed at disseminating lessons learned throughout the country or region.

#### 1.2.2 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

To ensure the sustainability of outputs beyond the project lifespan, the funding proposal includes the following components: (i) capacity-building for public institutions on climate risk management and adaptation; (ii) development of a legal framework for agroforestry-related assessment tools in legal frameworks, policies and planning frameworks, and specifically for the proposed activities; and (iii) strengthening the institutional capacity of the proposed agroforestry activities.

The increased diversity and volume of agricultural production due to the implementation of agroforestry activities has the potential to expand the market for agricultural products. The project would reduce disruption to product transportation thanks to rehabilitated and climate-resilient infrastructure, as described in section 4.5.1 of the feasibility study framework. This framework describes actions aimed at strengthening market opportunities through inclusive and efficient value chain development and improved community and private sector participation in all subsectors.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

#### 1.4.1 Vulnerability of the country, vulnerable groups and gender aspects
30. Timor-Leste is highly vulnerable to climate risks such as rainfall variability, increased temperatures, and sea-level rise, which can be exacerbated by land-use change. The degree of isolation and the socioeconomic conditions of the rural population make them even more vulnerable.

31. Women are considerably disadvantaged in relation to access to resources such as land, capital and credit, as well as access to agricultural inputs and technology, training, information and markets.

32. The project targets the most vulnerable in terms of poverty, infrastructure and basic public service deficits, social vulnerabilities, and levels of exposure to climate risks. The identification of these groups was based on the results of the baseline survey and the Analysis of Climate Change Risks, Impacts and Vulnerability for Baseline and Projected Climate Change Scenarios* carried out in 2016.

#### 1.4.2 Economic and social development
38. Timor-Leste is a post-conflict low-income country, with a poverty rate estimated at 89 per cent. Over 75 per cent of the population is engaged in subsistence agriculture (80 per cent for women). Due to high poverty and defective infrastructure and public services, 20 per cent of the population suffers from food insecurity, and an additional 13 per cent are vulnerable to food

# Uncertainty: Increased climate variability and extreme events are expected to worsen the situation.

## Timor-Leste gained its independence from Indonesia in 1999 and since then has been facing great challenges in rebuilding its infrastructure, strengthening institutions and generating economic growth.

### The villages with the lowest living standards are the most remote and least populated. Between 2008-2013, only 45 per cent of households had access to improved sanitation while electricity is only 3 per cent, while in villages with higher living standards the average share is 66 per cent. Access to improved water and sanitation is also much higher: in villages with high living standards, 98 per cent have access to improved water and 75 per cent have access to improved sanitation. In contrast, only 20 per cent of households in remote areas have access to improved sanitation while 31 per cent have access to improved water. The share of drinking water from a well or spring (25 per cent) and 25 per cent has access to improved sanitation is even lower for remote villages compared with other areas in the dry season.

#### Alternative of alternative sources of financing

The value of the indicator "domestic credit provided by financial sector" is negative for this country (-5.63 per cent of gross domestic product as per the funding proposal, -7.2 per cent as per World Bank data for 2017). This indicates a weak domestic financial framework and reflects limited access to finance.

### Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The proposal recognizes and addresses a series of barriers to the implementation, operation and maintenance of climate-resilient small-scale rural infrastructure, including insufficient institutional capacity at national level, lack of coordination between the Ministry of municipal and village-level institutions. Approximately 15 per cent of the total project budget of USD 9 million is allocated to activities aimed at overcoming these barriers (activities: 1.1, 1.2, 1.3 and 2.1). All in all, the proposal has a strong focus on institutional strengthening and capacity-building.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to High

#### Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

Ecosystem-based adaptation interventions are more effective in the long-term than hard engineering measures and would help to reduce the cost of maintaining new and rehabilitated infrastructure by diminishing its exposure to climate hazards.

The project was designed with a focus on institutional strengthening, risks and vulnerability assessments, infrastructure development and ecosystem-based adaptation measures. The project is not considered as a stand-alone project.

Given that rural infrastructure activities involved 130 individual subprojects in various sectors (roads, irrigation, drinking water, etc.), it was not possible to assess the expected cost and benefits of each intervention. The cost of infrastructure was estimated based on the unit costs of the SSB project.

#### Amount of co-financing

The Government of Timor-Leste would provide financing for 62 per cent of the total project cost.

Based on the assessment of the funding proposal at R22, ITAP concluded that "the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed interventions are reduced to a low rating due to uncertain operation and maintenance costs and the lack of resources to secure co-financing". # Financial viability

## Being of a public goods nature, project outputs do not involve cost recovery. Indirect revenue generation would come from the enhanced income of households implementing agroforestry practices and reduced household expenditure and income loss.

### Project costs are shared between the GCF and the 130 infrastructure subprojects from year 3 to year 20.

## Financial viability

### Being of a public goods nature, project outputs do not involve cost recovery. Indirect revenue generation would come from the enhanced income of households implementing agroforestry practices and reduced household expenditure and income loss.

#### Project costs are shared between the GCF and the 130 infrastructure subprojects for the estimation of avoided damage, the same frequency was used for all four considered scenarios.

### Due to the lack of historical data on damage caused by climatic events and to allow for the comparison of costs and benefits of all rural infrastructure, losses were assumed for each scheme based on a 50 per cent probability. For this reason, the internal rates of return (IRR) are only useful for ranking scheme viability.

#### Approximately 50 per cent of all flood control infrastructures are not reported to avoid damage by floods or crops. It is not clear if this assumption is based on lack of data and conservativeness.

### The financial analysis includes costs saved by avoiding economic losses, transportation losses, direct damages, and crop loss on an annual basis. It showed an IRR of 13 per cent and a net present value (NPV) of US$ 41 million.

## Best practices

### The designs for the small-scale rural infrastructure included under activity 2.2 (food defences, water harvesting and irrigation systems, and roads and bridges) were based on tested climate-proofing prototypes from the GEF-funded SRI project. For this reason, the description of these measures does not present detail on the technical aspects or design processes. For example, to be able to design a food defence system that can withstand drought periods, it would be necessary to analyse spring and ground water resources, soil moisture retention capacity, crop water requirements, irrigation systems, and sizing of storage tanks. However, ITAP considers that the large number of small-scale interventions will provide sufficient experience for this.

### The incorporation of physical protection, settlement chambers and filter membranes in intakes structures of springs and the stabilization of catchment area erosion and control through tree planting were considered as best practices by the SRI project team. The inclusion of these features was responded to as a response to ITAP questions.

### The project builds upon the lessons learned during the implementation of the SRI project and will be implemented in conjunction with other climate change programmes in order to fully achieve the integration of climate resilience considerations into infrastructure design, implementation, and operation. This is consistent with the GCF’s policy guidance and the public and involved a series of rural infrastructure interventions, including water supply and irrigation components, food defences, road defences, and bridges. The planning elements of the present project would be guided by the capacity development needs assessment and


---
**Page 130**

capacity development strategy formulated under the SSH project would scale up and complement the trainings under the SSH project.

Other best practices come from the lessons learned through the UNDP-supported Dili-Amuro Road Development Corridor Project which focused on strengthening the institutional and technical capacity of local authorities to plan, design, finance and implement ecosystem-based adaptation approaches, specifically agroforestry projects.

The TAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal. To make sure the whole set of benefits deriving from water supply schemes from this funding proposal materialize, the AE must meet the following:

Condition precedent to all disbursements for drinking water supply interventions under action 2.2:

For the avoidance of doubt, evidence must include, but not be limited to, a feasibility study including a description of the project, availability of funding, budget, project schedule and any other relevant information.

(ii) The accredited entity shall ensure that the GCF Processes will not support or finance directly or indirectly, any activities in relation to drinking water supply intervention in targeted municipalities that do not satisfy the requirements of the condition precedent to all disbursements for drinking water supply interventions under action 2.2.

With this condition, the TAP considers that both project impact and contribution to sustainable development can be rated as high.

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan GREEN CLIMATE FUND FULL PROPOSAL

## I. INTRODUCTION

### Background

The proposed project is designed to safeguard vulnerable communities and their physical and economic assets from climate change induced disasters in Timor-Leste. The proposed project aims to address the existing barriers and shift the baseline scenario towards climate resilience, with a focus on women, children, and elderly as well as the most vulnerable part of the total population. The direct beneficiaries of the project are 175,849 (89,643 males, 86,197 females) and the indirect beneficiaries are 346,160 (176,473 males, 169,687 females) living in the six municipalities. The proportion of men and women benefited by the project is almost the equity balance.

Previous assessments undertaken to understand the gender roles and the differential impact of climate induced disaster risks and climate shocks have underscored the established notion that women, children and elderly are more vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change. To assess if this widespread notion holds true in case of Timor-Leste or not, UNDP had undertaken a four way gender analysis in six municipalities to assess:

a) the existing barriers to access information on disaster risks and existing coping mechanisms and adaptive capacity in six municipalities on men and women;

b) impact of climate change on livelihoods, infrastructure and community based adaptation measures on the lives and livelihood of men and women;

c) role of women in contributing towards gender responsive planning and building resilient communities; and

d) policy gaps that need to be addressed to strengthen and foster mainstreaming gender into climate and disaster risk management interventions.

This gender assessment provides an overview of the gender situation in Timor-Leste in the context of the impact of climate change, coping mechanisms, climate vulnerabilities, policy gaps, information access and existing barriers. The assessment also explores both gender based differential challenges and opportunities. The assessment explored both existing and potential mechanisms to aid in mainstreaming gender into the overall development activities, with emphasis on climate resilient development.

The gender assessment was based on primary data collection through site visits, where key informant interviews and focused group discussions were conducted along with consultation workshops with the key stakeholders at both national and sub-national levels. This was done to ensure that the findings are representative of all groups in Timor-Leste.

### a) Primary Data collection:

With the insights from the secondary information, primary data collection was prepared, such as checklists for interviews, identification of appropriate & context specific gender assessments tools, and other participative approaches to support the gender analysis.

Throughout the community engagement, the study team undertook several initiatives to ensure integration of women such as holding women-only focus group discussions, or separating women and men. These enabled women to express their views on climate change adaptation and disaster risk management. The discussion workshop held between 23rd-24th March 2017 also served to obtain some additional information that was not possible to obtain during the field visit and consultations with stakeholders.

### b) Desk Review:

The assessment made use of available resources and insights from primary data collection to inform and strengthen the analysis with specific focus on the core components of the climate change (adaptation, resilience and disaster risk management). The desk review sources for this component of the study consist of:
- i) Literature (international) on gender and climate change and DRR;
- ii) Literature on climate change and disaster risks on Timor-Leste; and
- iii) Key documents such as national policies, strategies, and gender mainstreaming instruments and tools adopted in Timor-Leste.
- iv) Programme documents, narrative reports, country report on risks analysis and other documents and various materials produced within the scope of the two projects.

The following sections illustrate the sector-specific gender disparities. For example, in the economic sector, the existing patriarchal system in Timor-Leste undermines women vis-à-vis men and limits their access to resources because they are educated or come from wealthy households. Women’s contribution to the economic sector is significant, yet their contributions are not fully acknowledged and made ‘visible’.

• **Violence against women and girls in Timor-Leste**, is a serious issue that largely prevented women in participating actively in all aspects of development. This is evident through the high prevalence rates of gender-based violence and specifically domestic violence, which was the most frequently reported crime across all districts of the country between 2000 and 2009.

• The **2009-2010 Demographic Health Survey** (DHS) found that 43.8% of women aged 15–49 years in Timor-Leste had experienced physical or sexual violence by a current or former partner at some point during their lifetime, compared to the national average of 37%.

Health Survey (DHS) found that 31% of women aged 15-49 have experienced physical violence in their lifetime, with 29% of women experiencing physical violence often or sometimes in the past 12 months. The DHS survey also reported that about 3% of women aged 15–49 have experienced sexual violence. Social acceptance of domestic violence also impacts the implementation of the law.

### Participation in Labour Force within the existing patriarchal system in Timor-Leste

Undermines women vis-à-vis men and subsumes their contribution to society even when they are economically active. The economic role of women with respect to the economic sector is significant, yet their contributions are not fully acknowledged and made visible. This is exemplified by data showing gender disparities in labour force participation rates among different age groups.

* Among men, the participation rate is above 80% for those aged 30–49, and it is also higher for those in urban areas than those in rural areas.
* Among women, the participation rate is higher for relatively older age groups: those aged 55-69 in urban areas and those aged 45-49 and 55-69 in rural areas.
* Compared with men, labour participation among women aged 30–34 and 35–39 is lower than that of men. This suggests a gender gap in labour force participation.

This large gender gap suggests that women of prime childbearing and childcare ages including women's household responsibilities, especially child care, are likely to constrain their engagement in the labour market.

The disparity in labour force participation with lower participation among women illustrates a standard feature of the pattern in adoption of modern fertility surveys: for example, women engaged in work solely for household consumption, such as tending to the family garden, are not counted as "employed" to capture primarily market-oriented jobs and roles in the labour market.

Gender disparities in the urban areas where only 39 percent of females are employed or available for work, compared to 69 percent of males. Whilst over 55 percent of men aged 10 years and above are employed compared to only 28 percent of females. Women are less likely than men to be self-employed or employees, as men hold most jobs in both the private and public sectors. While women in decision-


---
**Page 139**
---

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FULL PROPOSAL

making positions had a comparatively higher level of education than their male counterparts. Most of the unemployed were male (63%).

A general observation noted that of all women primarily economically inactive, more than one-third (42%) were making some sort of economic contribution. Predominantly in unpaid farming activities (29.5%) or through making ties (weaving), tablecloths, kurtis and other garments (10%). In addition to this, 3% of women who were making an economic contribution, doing unpaid farm work (7.8%).

According to the 2010 Business Activity Survey, the private sector employs almost 47,000 people across the country, with nearly one third (30%) of those being women. The most profitable sectors are agriculture and livestock, which employ more than 56% of women, while USD18 million in added value to the economy in 2010. Women hold 38% of jobs in this industry and it employs 4,600 (3%) of the 139,000 women who work in the private sector.

Construction is another major employer for women, with 57% of all workers being female. The concentration of women in the private sector is in the accommodation, food and services industry where they represent more than half of the workforce (62%).

In case of access and control over the resources, including property and assets, women face serious economic constraints. The prevailing patriarchal norms, and customary law system (commonly referred to as 'sacar in Tigray), greatly influence the acquisition and control over resources such as land, livestock, etc., and these norms translate further into rights to inheritance and property, or obtaining business finance, etc. This is despite provision in the 2011 Civil Code for women and men to have equal rights to inheritance of land, as well as succession; *customary law prevails*.

The inheritance system continues to follow traditional matrilineal and patrilineal lines, especially in rural areas. In Timor-Leste, 'matrilinial' communities do not exceed 12.5% of the population. Even where there is no formal communal property, most traditional patriarchal norms still apply, with all assets (land, house etc.) belonging to a husband, and being inherited by male descendants only.

Furthermore, as stated in Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), while there are no reported legal restrictions on women's access to financial services, including credit, women have virtually no access to it. Moreover, Women often need references, husband’s signature or face difficulties filling out the forms due to illiteracy and, as such, experience barriers in applying for loans.

• The gender (inequality) scenario and gender dynamics is similar in the case of climate change and disaster risk realm. Climate change and disaster risks have been found to have a significant impact on women’s lives and livelihoods, which are often more vulnerable due to the gender-based roles. The magnitude of the impact is usually a factor of level of vulnerability of the communities in addition to the level of dependence on the natural system. Women are more likely than men to be affected by climate change because they face different vulnerabilities and coping capacities resulting from gender-based political, cultural and socioeconomic differences and inequalities that persist throughout the world. The magnitude of impact is expected to increase proportionally with the level of human development, coping mechanisms and policy instruments that contribute towards creation of enabling environment for women.

In rural Timor-Leste, the burden of agricultural work, coffee harvesting and caring for home gardens is generally shared between men and women. However, domestic responsibilities such as child-rearing, cooking, cleaning and overall family wellbeing, reflects traditional gender roles. This implies that women’s vulnerabilities to climate change and disaster, while similar to men, include specific additional concerns such as:

- Access to water and firewood;
- destruction of land and damage to the home gardens;
- damage to seeds;
- hindered access to markets and hence sale of products/ generation of cash;
- disruption in access to clinics; and
- closing of schools.
- Post-disaster health care.

Due to the traditional gender roles of women, the lack of access to water is a critical limitation imposed on women by climate change. Women have to spend on average 2–3 hours a day on fetching water either directly from the source or from the nearest communal tap. Therefore, securing water for household and farming purposes

### Green Climate Fund

Through climate resilient water supply systems will bring considerable opportunities for improving women's daily lives, health, and livelihood. The need and priority of this intervention has been verified during consultations, interviews and community meetings with all women groups.

In general, male-headed households have higher incomes than female-headed households and overall there is a considerable difference in the income of male-headed households compared to female-headed households. Pregnant and nursing mothers are particularly vulnerable because of their increased need for food and water and their decreased mobility. As the primary carers of children under five years old, women are also more vulnerable than the disabled. This increases their workload and reduces their mobility in cases where quick evacuations are required or where they live a long distance to water supply facilities.

It is important to note that there is often a gender balance between productive and reproductive activities of women is severely altered before and after flood events and has impacts on the resilience and income of a household.

- **Education** in Timor-Leste has succeeded in achieving gender-parity in primary and secondary school with girls' rate exceeding boys: In 2010, 94% of primary school age girls are enrolled compared to 87% for boys. At the tertiary level, female enrollment was 34% for girls and 27% for boys.* Thus in Timor-Leste, the difference between females' and males' education in terms of numbers is not significant; but the largest gender gap in education attendance is at tertiary level with almost 60 percent of university students being male.

As for fields of study at tertiary institutions, most women get their qualifications in areas such as economics and management (54% of graduates in 2010 were women) or teaching (41%). In 2010, fewer women graduated from the fields of engineering (21% of graduates were women), law (29%), public administration (31%), and agriculture (17%). The proportion of women graduating with degrees in economics and public administration, whereas their representation has fallen in law and agriculture.*

**vulnerable**. *Early marriage can be a factor for the high drop-out rates as also other consequences including participation in productive activities, and health. Wide disparities in education results in different abilities for women and men to acquire and process information and to communicate in face of disasters as well as to CCA measures.*

- **Early marriage and reproductive health**: Early marriage continues to take place in Timor-Leste, mainly in cases where a marriage has been arranged at birth or because of extreme poverty where "baraka" (bride-price) can be paid. As per the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA), 50% of women aged between 20 and 49 years were married, divorced or widowed (down from 10.6% in 2003); compared to 0.4% of boys in the same age range (down from 0.6% in 2003). Traditional attitudes and limited access to education and health services are some of the factors that contribute to a relatively young leading, to high fertility rates. In terms of reproductive health, high fertility, limited access to health services, safe water, and improved sanitation facilities are among the major challenges faced by women in Timor-Leste.

- **Health indicators**: The country has seen a declining trend; maternal (decreased from 660 per 100,000 live births in 2003 to 557 in 2009–2010), the highest in the world) and child mortality (decreased from 60 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2003 to 48 in 2009–2010) are key issues for women; and have been identified as the top public health priorities by the Ministry of Health (MoH)[1].

- **Decision making**: Timorese women face significant inequality as compared to men in respect to decision-making, and are restricted both at private and public sphere. Women's participation is low in the decision-making process on family planning; gender stereotypes and patriarchal attitudes. In daily life, cultural norms in Timor-Leste assign the dominant role to men (patriarchal system) in planning or decision-making regarding family size, child spacing, and reproductive health. The husband of the household makes decisions especially those having economic implications while women about their health & minor purchase; senior women hold a higher status than other women. Thus, in households all major decisions pertaining to economic decisions are made by men.

Similarly, the public sphere too is dominated by males with limited participation of women in decision making process [details in Chapter III under Women's participation and Leadership]. The common barriers to enter leadership position in Timor-Leste include:123
- lower education and literacy compared to men;
- discrimination in recruitment;
- negative stereotypes and attitudes towards women's participation in public life;
- attitudes to women's traditional role in the home;
- little support from families and political parties, especially little support for women who engage in politics as independent candidates without political party support;
- decision making;
- low interaction between women;
- low civic awareness; and low confidence to put themselves forward as candidates.

### Agriculture

Over 80% of Timor-Leste's rural population depends on agriculture and natural resources for their livelihood.14 Their agricultural land in Timor-Leste make it less than ideal for agricultural production with rugged, erosion-prone terrain, poor soils, and varying, often unpredictable rainfall.15 With more than 90% of the agricultural land being small plots, the majority of farmers are women due to a predominance of small subsistence farms with low levels of production and productivity.16

Households rely heavily on the cultivation of staple crops such as maize, rice, cassava, and sweet potatoes. Crop yields are well below the average for East Asia, leading to problems of food security in many communities, especially during the country's dry season when there is a high demand for food.17 The majority of households live at or near poverty line levels. As reported in a study, poorer households are more vulnerable to increased food insecurity because their livelihoods are more reliant on a staple food crop that is sensitive to climate hazards, such as maize production for food and income than other households. This clearly demonstrates a linkage between poverty and vulnerability to climate change.18

# III. MECHANISMS TO ADDRESS GENDER INEQUALITY IN TIMOR-LESTE - LEGAL AND ADMINISTRATIVE FRAMEWORK

The government of Timor-Leste's consistent political commitment to gender equality is demonstrated through the priorities given to the gender equality and gender inclusion at political, legal, administrative, and constitutional levels. Article 17 of the constitution as well. Article 17 of the Constitution calls for equality between sexes in all areas of family, political, economic, social, and cultural life. While Article 16, calls for nondiscrimination on grounds of sex or gender. The government also adopted formal strategies to advance gender equality, namely:

- Gender mainstreaming is mandatory across all government laws, policies, programs, and processes at national, as well as local level;
- Policies and programs to be designed to empower women socially and economically through livelihood support measures;
- Basic services to protect women from risks to be improved;
- Mechanisms to provide financial support to women leading households strengthened;
- Education strategies that promote the retention of girls at higher levels, particularly at the secondary and tertiary levels;
- More women to be employed across the civil service, also in senior positions;
- Policies and programs to be designed to support women in taking on decision-making roles in the public and private sectors.
- In addition, national zero-tolerance policy for violence in schools and homes will be strengthened.

The government's commitment is also reflected in Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan (SDP) 2011–2030. The SDP acknowledges women’s contribution to the country’s communities, farming-sector, economy and homes, despite lacking in proper recognition and without adequate support. The SDP sets out a vision for Timor-Leste's development with emphasis on social capital, infrastructure development, and economic development, focuses on achieving gender equality goals through the adoption of the following strategies:40

Some of the national gender considerations are influenced by the international commitments that Timor-Leste has made in a capacity of a signatory or party to international conventions and protocols.

The internationally agreed Hyogo Framework for Action (HFA 2005–2015) makes special references to the following as priorities and general considerations:41 A gender perspective should be integrated into all disaster risk management policies, plans and decision-making processes, including those related to risk assessment, early warning, information

More generally, the UN system has taken actions to achieve these gender equality, such as by adopting the Beijing Declaration and Platform of Action (1995) at the Fourth World Conference on Women in Beijing, which established global mainstreaming as a global strategy for the promotion of gender equality.

The Incheon Declaration on Disaster Risk Reduction in Asia and the Pacific 2010** recognizes the need to protect women, children and other vulnerable groups from the disproportionate impacts of disaster and to empower them to promote resilience within their communities and workplaces. The declaration also calls upon governments to mainstream gender equality into disaster risk reduction and adaptation, that is, whether and how women, men, boys, and girls from all minority, marginalized and diverse groups are affected by and respond to climate change and disasters.

As a party to the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and ratifying Paris Agreement, Timor-Leste has recognized the importance of involving women and men equally in UNFCCC processes and in the development and implementation of national climate policies that are consistent with the goals of the Paris Agreement. The country is committed to addressing issues of gender and climate change by including overarching text in the Paris Agreement National Determined Contributions (NDCs) document.

- Improving gender balance and increasing the participation of women in all UNFCCC processes, including in delegations and in bodies constituted under the Convention and its Kyoto Protocol.
- Increasing awareness and support for the development and effective implementation of gender-responsive climate policy at the regional, national and local levels.

Under the Lima work programme on gender, parties agreed to review progress towards achieving gender equality and women’s empowerment as a key element of their NDCs. The Heads of Governments, the Ministers, and the Heads of Intergovernmental organizations of countries in Asia and the Pacific, including Timor-Leste, met at the 2015 Asia-Pacific Climate Change Conference (APCCC) in Busan, Republic of Korea, on 29–30 October 2015.

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND: FULL PROPOSAL

Though the gender equality is enshrined in the Constitution and given adequate consideration in the SOP, gender disparity and need for a better social inclusion is felt across the sectors. One of the fundamental causes of gender disparity is gender stereotypes. Existing gender stereotypes are reflected in the distribution of women and men within the labour market economy, including developing skills in non-traditional areas, access to productive resources for women and men (girls and boys). Men, for example were more concentrated in occupations such as protection services, construction, transport, manufacturing, and agriculture, while women were more often working as housekeepers, weavers and gardeners. Gender stereotypes are also reflected in the types and levels of work that men and women take and the trainings that women and men receive. The Government has introduced a number of Vocational Education and Training (VET) Programmes, which in turn channels women and men into different jobs.

### Institutions:

In Timor-Leste, the Secretary of State for the Promotion of Equality (SEFP) was established as an institution and legal agency and a central Government body with the objective to promote equality between women and men. The SEFP is also the implementing body of the National Action Plan on Gender Equality (NAPGE). The National Committee for the Support of the Social and Socio-Economic Promotion of Women (SEM) is the overarching national mechanism that coordinates gender mainstreaming efforts across all sectors, as well as to monitor the implementation of the commitments. SEM also coordinates with the Gender Working Groups (GWGs), which are established in each ministry to provide technical support for gender mainstreaming in government activities. The GWGs’ mechanism is implemented both at the national and municipality levels, to its ensure increased inclusion of gender priorities in Ministerial Annual Action Plans.

The SEM and the GWGs have the basic competencies on general gender issues, but require technical support to integrate gender in project implementation. have the following shortcomings:

- Lack of technical capacity to mainstream gender specific to CCA and DDR agenda or related projects. This is especially in the use of gender analysis tools, in combination with climate vulnerability risk assessment and providing policy support to projects pertaining to gender mainstreaming in CCA and DDR initiatives.
- Lack of institutional coordination between SEM and GWGs. When not being consulted on project design, planning or implementation of projects.

So far SEM’s support to CCA and DDR related work has been limited. Similarly, the GWGs in each ministry has not been instrumental in gender mainstreaming because of their weak technical capacity specifically on gender related CCA and DDR work.

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Administration Framework

Timor-Leste has recently endorsed Decentralization Decree Law, which has strengthened and empowered sub-national administration units, mainly municipal authorities, municipal administrations, administration posts, sucos (villages) and aldeias (sub-village). With the additional authority and decision-making power, the sub-national administration units have a bigger role in implementing development projects.

Hence, in addition to the national level, government authorities at municipal and sub-levels are best placed to provide the necessary support for a gender responsive resilience-building agenda that has communities at its centre. There are two existing mechanisms, namely: (i) Municipal Integrated Development Plan (PIDM) or Plano Desenvolvimento Integrado Municipal; and (ii) National Programme for Village Development or Programma PIDOS (nacional Desenvolvimento Suku).

### The PIDM process

PIDM is one of the Government programme implemented throughout the country aimed at harmonizing the implementation of programmes conducted at the district level. The most notable change in terms of climate impacts and disaster risk reduction that PIDM will be faced with is how to increasingly address the underlying causes of vulnerabilities in face of climate change and disasters. It is important for the PIDM to explicitly link the vulnerabilities seen in climate adaptation and disaster response with the vulnerabilities that present challenges in development.

The PIDM has a strong gender mainstreaming integrated in its organisational structure and process established, it was implemented starting from Aldeia to Suku, Administrative post and Municipality level; the numerous stakeholders involved are: (i) Community members (ii) Suco Councils (iii) territorial developments (iv) Sub-district Development Commission (v) The Ministries responsible for local Administrations (vi) Line Ministries and (vii) The National Development Agency.

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FULL PROPOSAL

During the field visits, as well as KIIs held on mainstreaming gender in the PDIM process, government representatives in the municipalities visited underscored that the project proposal review process based along the line of its organisation structure ensures that gender is integrated into the project. However, there were concerns about the level of gender integration in the PDIM process. There were others who argued that despite the appearance of participation of women in the proposal review process there are certain gaps:

- It was revealed during discussions that proposals submitted by women's groups - mostly related to water and livelihoods - often get eliminated at the end of the process by the technical committee, which is made up of members from the PDIM team. This is to support women on developing good proposals, based on sound planning including identifying their need;
- There are no provisions and guidance on how females' and males' needs and concerns take place in consideration in project design, planning and implementation and tracking impact.
- Allocation of budgets.

## PDINS process

The PDINS is a new, nation-wide community development programme of the Government of Timor Leste. The PDINS channel funds directly from the Government to communities which will plan and implement projects in their own villages (sub-districts) or districts (provinces).

The types of infrastructure to be built or refurbished includes irrigation canals, local bridges, motorcycle paths, market places, kindergartens and sports facilities, water supplies, schools and health posts.

It was expressed that PDINS has more focus on gender from planning to implementation. Women from the village council submit proposals that are based on community discussion; although there is no specific fund for proposals from women's groups. Generally, proposals from women's groups include: irrigation canals (for water supply), local bridges (for connectivity [coffee fruits and fuelwood]), raising poultry, producing "taxis", setting up kiosks, and etc.

### IV. GENDER ISSUES IN RESPONSE TO IMMEDIATE CLIMATE INDUCED DISASTERS AND CLIMATE RISKS

In Timor-Leste, rural populations are greatly exposed to a range of hazards, including flash floods, landslides, soil erosion, coastal flooding and drought due to unfavorable terrain, socio-economic conditions, and the lack of infrastructure in many cases. Disasters, related risks and vulnerabilities have social as well as physical dimensions. Climate change and disaster risks have been found to have differential impact on men and women, mainly because of their unequal access to resources and opportunities.

The differential impact is usually a factor of level of vulnerability of the communities and the level of dependence on the natural system for livelihood. The impact of disasters and related risks are different for women and men as they have different roles in society and possess different social and economic capacity to respond to disaster. Gender inequality and women’s disempowerment are the determining factors that make women more vulnerable than men, especially when it comes to climate change and disasters; and at the same time their skills and life experiences are not identified as resources, and therefore, are not incorporated into risk reduction and disaster preparedness planning or recovery efforts. Unless these inequalities are adequately assessed and incorporated into climate change adaptation and DRM policies, the disparities are likely to be exacerbated.

Irrespective of gender, the causal effect of climate change and disaster risks is expected to increase proportionally with the level of human development, coping mechanisms and public vulnerability. Women in rural communities are more vulnerable than men because in the case of countries like Timor Leste where the staple source of household income is still agriculture-based activities, women have a greater responsibility for agricultural production as most infrastructures are either non-existent or need major overhaul; the lives of rural communities, especially women and girls are more sensitive to climate change and disaster risks.

As cited in a report: "Gender serves as an important dimension of both vulnerability and adaptation, that is, whether and how women, men, boys, and girls are affected by and respond to climate change." While 'vulnerability' is commonly understood as social, economic or environmental factors that make individuals, households or groups more susceptible to adverse change. Adaptation is understood as "the ability to change in response to the impacts of an event to minimize adverse consequences."

There has been considerable evidence that attention to gender in disaster planning and recovery is essential, but limited attention paid to disasters and family violence; worldwide literature suggests that increased violence against women is characteristic of post-disaster

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FULL PROPOSAL

### Recovery
Gender-based violence is found to increase due to disaster-induced stress and the temporary breakdown of law and order.[1] Of the little available research on the gender implications of climate change and disaster related impacts, it was found that it can exacerbate existing inequalities[2] and create new vulnerabilities as well as leading to increased gender-based violence. This is interesting because women are more likely than men for women and girls having to travel further to access water sources/fuels; or because of migration and community displacement in areas affected by climate change – there are increased incidences of harassment, sexual abuse and domestic violence during the migratory cycle etc.[3] Women also face poor access to health services, lack of access to impassable roads and damaged bridges. As women mainly stay home occupied with their household chores and home-gardening, and their children mostly remain isolated after extreme climate events or disasters. This creates a high level of risk for the lives and health of women and children.

For effective mainstreaming of gender considerations into Climate Change projects it is important to have gender-focused approach (instead of a woman focused approach) so that gender relations in CCA and in DRR are analyzed within a socio-economic and cultural context of Timor-Leste, leading to a challenging intervention on an already fragile society. In this norms and expectations for women and men and how this might affect the projects, will enable to achieve long term sustainable outcomes. Therefore, in addition to water supply infrastructure and livelihoods (through agroforestry interventions), the project also addresses the mobility of people including access to health services, improved roads and bridges.

These issues were identified and identified during the FGDs and community dialogues conducted. Most women in the municipalities visited for this study are engaged in agriculture. In general, women spend more time performing household chores than men during both normal times and after disasters. Women are responsible for cooking, cleaning, washing, fetching water and firewood whereas men are engaged in agricultural activities, because of additional reproductive care and work cares; women have less time for leisure, unlike men who may have more time for socialization and relaxation. In the case of disaster, women are responsible for feeding their families, as well as cooking, washing, fetching water and firewood were regarded as women's roles. Though not obligated, men and children (boys and girls) help in fetching water and firewood. Men, for example, may fetch water from a distance away; in such instances women will stand in the queue and men help to bring the water home.

To underscore the importance of developing and implementing gender responsive climate resilience programmes and projects in addition to ascertaining that gender is effectively mainstreamed into the climate resilient development, Timor-Leste drafted two related policies: National Climate Change Policy and National Disaster Risk Management Policy.

# Annex XIII (c) – Gender Assessment and Action Plan GREEN CLIMATE FUND FULL PROPOSAL

## V. GENDER ANALYSIS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

The gender analysis undertaken at the onset and design of this project acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout implementation. Stakeholder consultations took place in May 2017. Results from the consultations are detailed in the Stakeholder Engagement Report and Stakeholder Action Plan annex included in full as an additional annex as part of the proposal.

The gender analysis, through stakeholder engagement and consultation considered the following:

- Ensuring men's and women's equal participation in its activities under projects components;
- Identifying the socio-economic, cultural and institutional barriers that deterred women's and men's equal participation and their ability to equally benefit from its activities;
- Identifying the differences in men's and women's ability to access and control the benefits derived from its activities;
- Assessing the impact of the project on women's empowerment and well-being through the collection of high quality sex-disaggregated data.

The consultations have identified the following areas as particularly important dimensions to be reflected upon during the implementation phase:

- Tremendous improvements in increasing women’s active participation in the decision making, particularly during the PDM and PNDS formulation process to ensure that climate responsive infrastructures are gender response too;
- Gender analysis must be included as part of risk and vulnerability assessments for the national and local governments to effectively collect sex and age disaggregated data.
- Implementation of the recommendations from the GCF Gender Action Plan (GAP) on Gender Mainstreaming in CC and DRR developed as part of this assessment;
- Relevant analytical tools and frameworks must be simplified and made available in a user-friendly manner;
- Systematic capacity building and empowerment of the Gender Focal Points appointed by the government in each line ministry. In order to enable them to contribute in mainstreaming climate responsive gender priorities into sectoral plans and programmes;
- Critical to improve evidence informed knowledge generation, requires support in developing tools and methodologies for data collection, analysis and capacity for systematic sex-disaggregated data collection, analysis and storing on climate change and disaster impacts, capacities and other relevant information.

- Foster the creation of women's groups that contribute to diversifying local and specialized livelihoods to increase their resilience. Strengthen women's access to the following livelihood dimensions:42
    - Social capital: Create and strengthen local women's networks and groups, as well as representation in local institutions.
    - Economic capital: Promote new livelihoods through securing access to water, irrigation, and agricultural production;
    - Human capital: promote formal and non-formal education measures targeted towards increasing the number of women who are literate and have gained formalized employment (through training and incentivizing women-led companies, or companies that employ women and apply gender parity as employment policy);
    - Physical capital: promote safe communication and transportation for women and equal access to information. Climate resilient roads and bridges improve mobility of women and their access to local markets and escape routes during and after disasters; climate risk disaster;
    - Natural capital: Promote stability of communal land for increasing overall climate resilience and land productivity.
